FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 TUESDAY, MARCH 29, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met in executive session, at 10.20 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in executive session for a continuation of the hearings on H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450.

Our witnesses this morning are Vice Adm. L. C. Heinz, U.S. Navy, Director of Military Assistance, Office of the Assistant Secretary of Defense for International Security Affairs, and Admiral U. S. G. 

Sharp, U.S. Navy, Commander in Chief, Pacific. 

Admiral Sharp, you have a prepared statement, and Admiral Heinz, you have a prepared statement. If you would each make your statements, we will then direct our question to either or both of 

You may proceed first, Admiral Heinz. you.

STATEMENT OF VICE ADM. L. C. HEINZ, U.S. NAVY, DIRECTOR OF MILITARY ASSISTANCE, OFFICE OF THE ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF DEFENSE FOR INTERNATIONAL SECURITY AFFAIRS 

Admiral HEINZ. Mr. Chairman, I would like first to give you a brief presentation on the congressional presentation document of this year and talk to my statement.

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, since succeeding 

General Wood as Director of Military Assistance last September, I 

have looked forward to testifying in support of the program. I hope to make the most of this opportunity by giving you a report on the status of our fiscal year 1966 military assistance assets and by presenting the proposed fiscal year 1967 military assistance program. 

First, your attention is invited to the changes which have taken place in fiscal year 1966 since our proposals were submitted to the Congress last spring. The originally proposed fiscal year 1966 military assistance program was based on new obligational authority in the amount of $1,170 billion-a $1 billion "baseline" program and an additional $170 million earmarked to provide for the special combat needs in Vietnam and Laos. (Total obligational authority, including recoupments and reappropriations, amounted to $1.258 billion.) The

Secretary of Defense cautioned that the total of $170 million for southeast Asia, "may represent a conservative estimate, since it is based 229

230 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

upon our current analysis of possible developments over which we do not have full control."

Recent events underscore the significance of the Secretary's statement. They account for the fact that we have had to make full use of the $300 million drawdown authority provided in section 510 of the authorizing legislation a recourse used only when interests vital to the security of the United States are at stake. Adding the $300 million drawdown and an increase of $33.3 million over our original estimate of recoupments and reappropriations to the initial fiscal year 1966 figure of $1.258 billion in total obligational authority, we arrive at a revised figure of $1.591.3 billion in total obligational authority for fiscal year 1966 (as of January 1966). 

Also as a result of the conflict in Asia, new arrangements have been made to fund the rapidly rising requirements for support of South Vietnamese and other free world military assistance forces currently engaged in combat operations against the Vietcong and North Vietnamese infiltrators. In accordance with the southeast Asia supplemental appropriations bill for fiscal year 1966, responsibility for funding such support and related costs is being assumed by the military departments. All unexpended balances of current and prior year programs for Vietnam originally funded under the military assistance program are being "transferred to and merged with the accounts of the military departments.' 

99 Under this same appropriation, there is being allocated to the Department of Defense the $375 million specifically appropriated to the President in the supplemental "for liquidation of obligations incurred pursuant to the authority in section 510 of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended." The supplemental appropriation for military assistance has been requested "to replenish Department of Defense stocks of equipment and materiel which were used" (in fiscal years 1965 and 1966) because of "increased military assistance requirements in Vietnam." It permits compliance with the provision of section 510 of the Foreign Assistance Act, which makes drawdown of defense stocks and services "subject to subsequent reimbursement therefor from subsequent appropriations for military assistance." 

In addition to the changes resulting from the war, certain other changes have been made in fiscal year 1966 country programs. The suspension of deliveries to India and Pakistan last September is the most obvious case in point [security deletion].

The Republic of Korea units are giving an excellent account of themselves in combat operations [security deletion]. 

An increase in the worldwide and regional costs reflects the need for emergency support requirements of the inter-American peacekeeping forces in the Dominican Republic, and for replacement of stocks to accommodate increased demands placed on the Logistics Center [security deletion] because of Vietnam. There has also been an addition to worldwide and regional costs for research and development associated with modification, test, evaluation, and development of technical data for the intsallation of jet engines on C-119 aircraft.

Now, let us turn to the proposed fiscal year 1967 military assistance program. I shall summarize that program and some of the basic reasoning underlying it.

This distribution prompts me to digress for a moment and explain the arrangement of this year's presentation document. Two years ago we departed from the traditional geographical pattern and divided the proposed program into "objective categories" rather than regional sections. Our purpose was to emphasize the relationship between the allocation of assets and the specific purposes military assistance was intended to serve. It became apparent, however, during the course of two successive congressional presentation cycles that the advantages we anticipated were clearly outweighed by the disadvantages. We have, therefore, returned this year to the more conventional regional arrangement. This arrangement parallels the regional organization of the State Department bureaus and their Defense Department counterparts, as well as your subcommittee structure. For this reason, I believe you will welcome its return.

Now, in accordance with the expressed desires of the committee, I shall run through the presentation document so that you will be familiar with both its contents and arrangement. My introductory remarks have already covered the general and historical data contained in the introductory summary, including the section 634(d) report, which is made in accordance with the statutory requirement that the Congress be informed of changes in the current fiscal year program.

I shall, therefore, move forward to a survey of the fiscal year 1967 program presentation for the European region. 

Military assistance is reaching the end of its job in Western Europe. 

Since the inception of the program in 1950, the number of European countries receiving grant materiel aid has dropped from a total of 14 to the 4 still included in the program for fiscal year 1967. Whereas programs for the remaining four countries-Denmark, Norway, Portugal, and Spain-now account for only 3.5 percent of the worldwide total, some 79 percent of the total program went to Western Europe in 1950, when the major purpose of military assistance was to build up allied strength against the threat of Communist aggression directed toward that area. Programs for 10 countries have already been phased out or consist of the final deliveries of equipment programed in previous years.

This trend will continue. We expect to terminate grant materiel assistance for Denmark and Norway in the near future. The proposed 

Danish program completes our commitments [security deletion]. In 

Norway, military assistance has played a major role in facilitating effective Norwegian participation in NATO. In 1962, we made an agreement with Norway to assist the buildup of her air forces, as we

232 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

had done with its naval forces in 1960. The 1967 program is the last large program for this country. 

There is a small program for Portugal [security deletion]. 

Grant aid for Portugal consists primarily of training assistance; also payments which were prior funded on the cost-shared destroyer program dating back to a 1960 commitment. Other deliveries include 

T-37 trainer aircraft, spares for the NATO committed maritime patrol squadron, and training aids, documents, and publications [security deletion].

Since 1963, the United States has continued to station forces and operate in the Azores on a de facto basis pursuant to assurances from the Portuguese Foreign Minister, pending negotiations. These negotiations continue in the form of occasional discussions between our Ambassador and the Portuguese Foreign Minister and by the Portuguese Ambassador with the State Department in Washington, but no progress has been made recently. [Security deletion.] 

Military assistance programed for Spain is also keyed to insuring our continuing access to strategically important bases and installations which are vital to optimum deployment of U.S. forces. [Security deletion.] 

Military assistance to Spain has both provided the quid pro quo for valuable and essential base rights and has generated substantial Spanish military procurement in the United States. 

While Western Europe is approaching military self-sufficiency, many of the countries in the next section, the Near East, still require substantial U.S. assistance. [Security deletion.] 

We are requesting a total of $240 million in grant aid during fiscal year 1967 for the 11 other countries in this area. This represents 

23.4 percent of the anticipated total obligational authority for the coming year.

A major emphasis of our total military assistance effort throughout the Near East and south Asia is on developing programs which will encourage and elicit maximum self-help on the part of recipient countries.

A case in point is Greece, which is taking on more of the burden of supporting her own forces. We no longer provide any commercial consumables. Greece has also set up maintenance, assembly, and overhaul lines for vehicles which we provide in "where is-as is" condition. 

This arrangement enables us to furnish, within the resources available, a greater amount of other kinds of assistance. 

The goal of our program for Greece is toward modernization of the army and the air force. We recently had talks with the Greeks concerning the composition of their forces, encouraging more realistic planning over a 5-year period, taking into account total resources available. We expect that discussions with Greece will continue, and hope they will result in increased efficiency in the application of both Greek and United States funds to the strengthening of the country's defense posture.

Turkey is another country wherein application of the principle of self-help is producing results. [Security deletion.] Turkey is also producing increasing amounts and more types of ammunition, and augmenting her maintenance and repair capabilities for all types of

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

233 equipment. She is now capable of assembling small naval craft. As this capability develops, we are substituting small-craft kits instead of completed craft. Through such measures, we are able to accomplish much more toward maintaining Turkish defenses. [Security deletion.] 

We feel that the program we are proposing for Turkey-which is finely balanced between much-needed investment in modernization and equally essential operating costs, including training and maintenance is essential to both Turkish and NATO defense. [Security deletion.] 

Another large country in the area-Iran-is now shifting gradually from grant aid. As it becomes economically more viable, Iran is increasing its purchases of equipment through military sales and relying less on grant-aid assistance. [Security deletion.] 

We are simultaneously stressing self-help. Iran not only is becoming financially able to buy more, but is also developing increased local capability for maintenance, repair, and rehabilitation of equipment, and for manufacture of ammunition. [Security deletion.] 

Our encouragement and support of such efforts are strengthening a pro-Western ally, whose ability to defend itself and deter any encroachment from outside is vitally important to the security of the entire region.

In past years we have supported major military assistance programs for both India and Pakistan in consonance with our military as sistance objectives [security deletion]. The sharp but brief [security deletion] clashes in Kashmir and the Punjab last September caused us to suspend equipment deliveries to both countries, however; and our policy toward the subcontinent still reflects the evolutionary nature of the situation in the area. In mid-February, we announced that in light of the excellent progress made by both countries in withdrawing their troops to positions held last August, and on a number of other bilateral differences, we would resume on a selected, case-by-case basis sale of nonlethal military equipment to both parties. This remains our policy as of this moment. [Security deletion.] 

Going on to the smaller country programs-composed almost exclusively of training-I would first mention Afghanistan where we propose to continue to provide limited training [security deletion]. 

Similarly, small training programs for Iraq and Syria [security deletion]. 

The program for Jordan includes both training and materiel. Military assistance coupled with economic aid has been an essential factor in Jordan's [security deletion]. 

For Nepal, the proposed program includes equipment and training 

Training programed for Saudi Arabia supports the sale of military equipment to that country. We provide no equipment on a grant-aid basis. [Security deletion.] 

The minor amounts proposed for Lebanon and Yemen are limited to small [security deletion] training programs. 

The smallest percentage of the total program is allocated to Africa. 

Through the judicious allocation of military assistance amounting to about 3 percent of our anticipated total obligational authority, we seek to promote three major objectives: (1) The independence of African

234 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

countries from Communist influence and control; (2) the maintenance of regimes which are capable of maintaining internal stability as a precondition to orderly social and economic development, 

Each of the 11 country programs proposed for Africa in fiscal year 1967 is designed to promote one or more of the three basic objectives. 

All are in consonance with the congressional restrictions on military aid. The total African program is $31.8 million, of which $22.2 million is for equipment-an amount well below the current legislative ceiling of $25 million for defense articles. 

The almost [security deletion] which we propose to allocate to the Congo will be used solely to strengthen internal security. Together with assistance provided by other free world nations, our program is directed toward the development of the minimum forces needed to provide that measure of internal stability and security in the Congo which is prerequisite to its future growth and development. 

The program proposed for Ethiopia is the largest in the area [security deletion]. It reflects both our effort to improve the internal security of the country [security deletion]. 

The primary threats which confront Ethiopia are [security deletion]. Ethiopia needs our assistance to improve the capabilities of its armed forces in order to cope with these difficult situations. We are also encouraging the use of Ethiopian armed forces in civic action projects. Our assistance to Ethiopia serves to promote its stability and encourages its moderating influence in African councils. 

With respect to Morocco [security deletion]. The fiscal year 1967 

program is composed primarily of training, spares and aircraft sup port equipment. [Security deletion.] 

The program for Libya amounts to slightly over [security deletion] and is designed to improve internal security capability of that country, which makes valuable facilities available to the United States. While 

Libya is relatively stable at present [security deletion]. U.S. equipment is being effectively absorbed and, coupled with United States and United Kingdom training programs, has established the basis for a small but effective Libyan Army. [Security deletion.] 

In Guinea, our past civic action program has been quite successful. 

We plan to continue the program in fiscal year 1967 by providing [security deletion] in training and follow-on spare parts for the construction equipment already delivered. The program is designed to develop an engineer company capable of road construction and of contributing to other civic action projects. 

The modest program for Liberia continues our effort to improve the capabilities of its small security force. For fiscal year 1967, we propose to provide [security deletion] worth of training, replacement materiel and follow-on spare parts. [Security deletion.

Our program for Senegal supplements French assistance by supporting an engineer battalion with modern construction equipment. 

These units are now assisting in the development of the civilian economy through a program of road and air strip construction which is also increasing the mobility of the Senegalese armed forces. The fiscal year 1967 program will provide training and follow-on spares to a value of [security deletion].

The proposed program for Mali amounts to [security deletion] and is composed chiefly of training, with some spare part support of an engineer company. Our program provides the Government of Mali with [security deletion] and has had a favorable effect on our rela tions with Mali [security deletion]. 

The proposed program for Nigeria consists entirely of training assistance designed to assist in the maintenance of internal security. 

While our African programs are small, the stakes are large. Se- curity deletion.] We must, therefore, join other free world nations in assisting key African nations to improve their stability in order that they may move rapidly toward political maturity and economic development.

At the outset of a discussion of the Far East, it should be noted that the presentation document reflects the transfer of Vietnam to the military department budgets. Military assistance proposed for the remaining eight recipient countries in the area amounts to $387 million or 37.7 percent of our anticipated total obligational authority. 

[Security deletion.] 

The proposed program for the Republic of China (GRC) amounts to [security deletion]. China continues to face the threat of Communist forces across the Taiwan Straits, and strong GRC forces are needed for the defense of Taiwan. The security of the Republic of 

China is important to the security interests of the United States, and the Mutual Defense Treaty of 1954 continues in force. The economy of the Republic of China is healthy and growing, and the GRC no longer depends on the United States for economic assistance. Although that Government devotes increasing resources each year to its own defense budget, the economy is not yet able to bear the full burden of its defense needs. The proposed program represents the additional 

U.S. support considered necessary to maintain adequate Republic of 

China forces. Major emphasis is being placed on modernization and development [security deletion]. 

The program proposed for Japan in the amount of [security deletion] consists largely of residual amounts of training required to fulfill prior commitments related to a cost-sharing program for air defense, radar, and missile units. The remainder will provide orientation training designed to enhance cooperation between United States and Japanese forces and to pave the way for continued military sales to Japan.

The proposed program for the Republic of Korea (ROK) amounts to [security deletion]. This program is required to support those 

South Korean forces which, together with available U.S. forces, are necessary to defend the Republic of Korea against a possible assault by North Korea and Communist China across the uneasy truce line. 

Prior U.S. military assistance has helped to train and equip Republic of Korea forces so that Korea was able to respond promptly with some 25,000 men who are now fighting bravely and effectively alongside Republic of Vietnam, United States, and other free world military assistance forces in Vietnam. [Security deletion.] 

That portion of cost borne by the United States for South Korean forces in Vietnam is funded in the Vietnam program under the military departments budgets. However, the threat to South Korea has

not diminished, and with sizable ROK forces deployed to Vietnam, we must continue a large program designed to maintain a strong defensive posture in South Korea. The program proposed represents requirements associated with support and modernization of those ROK forces necessary to the defense of their own country. 

The proposed program for Laos amounts to [security deletion]. 

The objective of this program is to support non-Communist forces at the level necessary to enable them to defend essentially those areas under non-Communist control in 1962 [security deletion]. The increased tempo of activity in Laos has increased requirements for ammunition, attrition aircraft, and other supplies and equipment con sumed in combat. [Security deletion.] Laotian Government forces continue their resistance on widely scattered fronts, and the air force in particular is becoming more and more effective against Pathet Lao and North Vietnamese forces.

The small training program proposed for Malaysia is designed to demonstrate U.S. support for Malaysia in its effort to build up its own defense forces, and to maintain its independence and internal security. Malaysia presently relies primarily on British and Commonwealth forces and military support in maintaining its ability to resist aggression.

The program for the Philippines amounts to [security deletion] and is designed to help support Philippine forces and strengthen their capacity to resist external attack [security deletion] and to control effectively insurgency, should it break out in the Philippines.

U.S. military bases in the Philippines are of vital importance in support of our efforts in southeast Asia. [Security deletion.] 

The Philippine Government is currently proposing to send a combat engineer battalion to South Vietnam in direct support of free world efforts. It is also beginning to show progress in its efforts to improve the standard of performance of its forces. The proposed program represents the amount of military assistance considered necessary to provide for support and limited modernization of these forces. 

The proposed program for Thailand totals [security deletion]. 

Thailand is experiencing increased pressure from Communist subversive activity. Communist China has named Thailand as its next victim; and a "Thailand Independence Movement," as well as a "Thailand Patriotic Front," has already been established. In the past year, there have been a number of acts of terrorism against the population and civilian officials in sparsely settled northeast Thailand. The proposed program is designed to increase Thai capability to deal with this growing threat, and to strengthen the capacity of Thai forces' to resist on their own an external attack. [Security deletion.] 

The program proposed represents the minimum amount of U.S. military assistance considered necessary in support of these Thai forces and defense efforts.

I shall now turn to Latin America. Here, the specific objectives of military assistance are the development of Latin American forces capable of maintaining internal security against threats of violence and subversion, whether Communist-inspired and supported or "home grown;" encouraging the armed forces to support and strengthen democratie institutions and to undertake civic action projects which both

contribute to the social and economic development of the country and bring the armed forces and civilian populace closer together; and, finally, to develop selected units for possible use in carrying out peacekeeping assignments under the aegis of the Organization of American 

States or the United Nations. 

We are also seizing every opportunity to urge upon Latin American nations the need for a realistic correlation between both force levels and defense budgets and the actual requirements which must be met to insure their countries' security.

Let me expand a bit on the first objective, that of strengthening internal security. The Communist Tri-Continent Conference, held in 

Havana in January 1966, called for the continued active support of armed insurgency in Latin America, with Venezuela, British Guiana, Peru, the Dominican Republic, Haiti, Paraguay, Nicaragua, and Guatemala mentioned as specific targets. We can expect the subversive struggle to go on, and to continue to call for a planned, organized effort by the countries concerned to overcome it. This effort will require not only well-trained and equipped armed forces, but also coordination of their operations with those of improved police units in the target countries. There are gratifying evidences of progress in this direction. 

We are also encouraged by the increasing participation of Latin American forces in civic action projects which include such diverse and useful projects as port visits in the upper reaches of the rivers by small naval units and flying hospitals and clinics which utilize air force aircraft.

The program we propose for Latin America in fiscal year 1967 is specifically designed to support the efforts and encourage the trends I have been discussing. It will provide assistance to 19 countries in the total amount of $71.9 million in full compliance with the legislative provisions which apply to our effort in Latin America. The functional breakout is as follows:

This breakout reflects the increased emphasis which we are placing on our training effort throughout Latin America. I would also point out that we are in transition from training programs concentrating on military skills to those of broader scope which provide training in management at levels appropriate to the capabilities of particular groups of trainees. This is another trend we hope to continue and encourage.

I also want to mention and emphasize another area of general effort in Latin America, that of communications. We are assisting recipient countries to develop joint operations centers for all services and to improve their internal communications; and we are also working on communications between the operations centers of the various countries. These efforts are particularly productive in those countries associated in the Central American Defense Council. We believe that development of improved communications systems is a logical first step toward carrying out last year's congressional directive with respect

to promoting and encouraging the coordinated use of force under the OAS. And because communications are essential to coordination, support of these efforts is included in the internal security portion of the functional breakout.

Focusing now on individual countries, I shall start with Argentina [security deletion]. It consists mainly of types of training and equipment which would be most helpful in dealing with any threat to in ternal security [security deletion]. 

Our program for Bolivia consists primarily of internal security support. It includes civic action assistance for three engineer battalions which are engaged in road building [security deletion]. 

The largest country in South America is, of course, Brazil, which has one-half the population of the entire South American continent. 

Brazil might have fallen into Communist hands in 1964, had not the revolution of the middle classes and military forces frustrated a trend toward leftist authoritarianism. The armed forces are strongly anti- 

Communist. They are capable of maintaining public order in support of other law enforcement agencies; but they also have a large country to cover, and they need the improved communications and mobility which we plan to provide. We have also programed a substantial amount of training.

The Brazilians make up by far the largest Latin American component of the Inter-American Peace Force in the Dominican Republic. 

Brazil has repeatedly shown herself ready to take part in collective efforts for security and defense. This attitude we strongly support, and military assistance is important in encouraging such efforts. 

With respect to Chile, another major country in South America, our objectives are to improve the internal security capability of its armed forces, to encourage their participation in civic action projects, and to enhance their ability to contribute to OAS and U.N. peacekeeping missions. [Security deletion.] 

In Colombia, insurgency is likely to increase sharply in the near future. The country is faced with possible new waves of violence by extremist groups as a result of forthcoming election activities. The military have steadfastly backed the Government [security deletion]. 

Faced with a threat of Communist-inspired and supported subversion, Colombia has a continuing problem on its hands and [security deletion] mobility and communications capability we propose to provide.

In Costa Rica, we propose a small program tailored to the capabilities of the [security deletion] Civil Guard. The country has a sound, democratic Government and no military force. The Civil Guard concerns itself with the principal threat which is one of Communistfomented disorders and infiltration by subversive units. Costa Rica participates in the Central American Defense Council and provides a small police contingent to the Inter-American Peace Force. 

The Dominican Republic is a special case. Here, we have been confronted for the past year by close to a civil war which has been held in bounds, for the most part, by the presence of the Inter-American Peace Force (IAPF). We still are hopeful that elections will take place in 

June [security deletion].

. 

The program proposed for Ecuador illustrates the general direction of military assistance to Latin America. We have provided training and minimum equipment essential for the development of an internal security force. Three-fourths of the fiscal year 1967 program is designed to strengthen that force. We also plan to provide equipment and communications in support of civic action projects. 

The overall purpose of the small program for El Salvador is to provide equipment and training for handling the internal security situation.

Guatemala is the prime mover of the Central American Defense 

Council. [Security deletion.] About one-fourth of our proposed program for Guatemala consists of training. In addition, we are providing equipment designed to improve the mobility and communications of the armed forces.

Honduras has fairly competent military forces. It was the first 

Latin American country to send a contingent to the Inter-American Peace Force in the Dominican Republic. There is underway a good civic action program which concentrates on building roads in the border area, providing medical facilities and help in constructing schools. Honduras is undertaking some self-help in establishing automotive maintenance facilities. [Security deletion.] The proposed program is designed to support these efforts. 

Grant military assistance to Mexico is limited to a small training program. Mexico does not desire grant of defense articles, and her military purchases from the United States have amounted to several million dollars. Military assistance facilitates cooperation with this country at our southern border. 

Nicaragua is pro-United States [security deletion]. About onehalf of the program we propose for 1967 consists of training [security deletion.] Nicaragua also provides a contingent to the Peace 

Force in the Dominican Republic. Additionally, it is interesting to note that cadets of the Nicaraguan Military Academy spend the fourth year of their education at the School of the Americas in Panama.

The small program proposed for Panama is designed to improve the internal security capability of the National Guard, [security deletion]. The problems confronting it are concentrated mainly in 

Panamá City and Colón. 

One-half of the program for Paraguay is devoted to training, with special emphasis on internal security and civic action. Internal security is vital to the continued development of the country. The armed forces are engaged in road construction, health and sanitation efforts, vocational and literacy training, and in helping to build schools. We support this effort and feel that civic action will continue to receive strong emphasis in Paraguay. Paraguay has also contributed forces to the IAPF in the Dominican Republic. 

Peru, the fourth largest country in South America, has had an insurgency problem during the past year. [Security deletion.] The capability of the armed forces to control rural insurgency has been demonstrated. Civic action has proven its worth here where the "campesino" support of the insurgency has been weakened by the

land reform efforts of the Government and by civic action projects. undertaken by the armed forces. [Security deletion.] 

The armed forces of Uruguay lean heavily on United States assistance. The army is undertaking establishment of engine-rebuild facilities and development of technical schools. The navy can now make small ship repairs, and the air force can overhaul engines. The armed forces are engaged in civic action undertakings such as road construction, school and hospital construction, reforestation, irrigation and literacy training. [Security deletion.] 

The last of the countries in this area is Venezuela, grant military assistance for which is limited to a training program with military equipment being furnished through credit and direct sales. [Security deletion.] 

This completes the rundown of the country programs. 

Finally, we have the worldwide and regional programs, which are summarized in the last section of the presentation document. This section includes the United States share of NATO infrastructure in the amount estimated as necessary to meet our obligations to this international endeavor, United States support of international military headquarters, administrative expenses, regional costs, and, finally, funds for the credit assistance required in connection with military sales to countries economically capable of purchasing equipment for their defense needs.

As you know, Admiral Sharp, who is here with me today, and the other commanders of unified commands who will follow me in testifying before this committee, have direct responsibility for U.S. security interests in their respective areas, and therefore are intimately concerned with the military assistance programs in these areas. 

I am sure you will have many questions they are uniquely qualified to answer. 

If you have any other questions you want to put to me, concerning my overall responsibilities, before hearing Admiral Sharp, I shall be happy to answer them.

Two years ago we departed from the traditional geographic pattern of the congressional document and presented our program in the objective categories rather than regional sections. It became apparent, however, that the advantages of this were less than we had anticipated, so this year we have returned to the more conventional regional arrangement.

This arrangement parallels the regional organization of the Department of State bureaus and the Defense counterpart and to your subcommittees. I think you will welcome this arrangement.

Turning to the book you will notice on pages 2 to 4 we have quoted the purposes of the military assistance program as stated by the Congress. Also we show in here a brief history of the program as well as a narrative of the fiscal year 1967 program, the planning, and legislative proposals.

On page 4 we show the procedures for development of the annual program. On page 5 we summarize the source of funds for the military assistance program.

I would invite your attention to the changes that took place in the fiscal year 1966 program since our proposals were submitted to the Congress last spring. The original program was based in a new

obligational authority of $1.170 billion, which was a baseline $1 billion program with $170 million additional earmarked to provide for the special combat needs in Laos and Vietnam. 

If you recall at that time the Secretary of Defense cautioned that the [security deletion] "may represent a conservative estimate, since it is based upon analysis of possible developments over which we do not have full control."

I think the events of the past year underscore the significance of the Secretary's statement. Due to the increased costs for Vietnam we had to make full use of the $300 million drawdown authority provided in section 510 of the authorizing legislation. 

Mrs. BOLTON. The drawdown, what do you mean?

Admiral HEINZ. This authorizes the drawdown of service stocks up to a total of $300 million in any one year. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Does it have replenishing?

Admiral HEINZ. It says this will be replaced by subsequent appropriations. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Nothing else? 

Admiral HEINZ. No. This drawdown together with an increase of $33.3 million over our original estimate of recoupments and reappropriations resulted in the revised figure of $1.591.3 billion for obligational authority in fiscal year 1966, as of January 1966, when this book was made up.

As a result of the recent legislation, the funding of the support for Vietnam and other free world military assistance forces in being were transferred to and merged with the accounts of the military. departments.

The last column on page 5 shows our proposed fiscal year 1967 program, in which we are requesting new obligational authority of $917 million. We estimate $10 million in reappropriations and $100 million in recoupments for a total funding availability of $1,027 million. 

Turning to pages 6 and 7, you will see the details of the country programs that we propose for fiscal year 1967. On these pages the total amounts for each country is broken down into the three categories of operating costs, investment costs, and supply operations. 

You will note at the right on the bottom of page 7 we take into account the estimated $110 million of reappropriations and recoupments, the recoupments being savings we anticipate from prior year programs, and in this manner reduce our new obligational authority to $917 million.

You will note that operating costs are less than investment costs. 

We are making a determined effort to have the countries pick up a greater part of the burden of their operating costs, and the result is reflected in this table.

Pages 8, 9 and 10 show historical data of military assistance since its inception in 1950.

Pages 11 to 13 are concerned with excess stocks which the military assistance program can deliver to the countries for repair and rehabilitation costs only. However, the values shown here are the acquisition costs of the various items concerned since it is the only way we can present this excess program. 

Turning to pages 14 and 15, we have a schedule of deliveries by fiscal years to the various countries concerned. 60-929-66-pt. 2- -2

Starting at page 17, we have the report required to the Congress which informs you of the changes in the current fiscal year program from that presented to you in last year's congressional document. 

These changes occur not only because of price changes but changes in requirements on the program throughout the year. The best example is the changes shown in the major increases on page 21 for Vietnam and Laos. 

The next section on page 25 and going through page 27 shows details of our proposed training program. In connection with this, I would mention that we have recently completed a worldwide training conference and have placed a great deal of increased emphasis on training during the coming year. We are going to make a determined effort to single out potential future leaders for our training. 

I now move forward to the country programs. This starts with the 

European region, which is summarized on pages 30 and 31. Military assistance is reaching the end of its job in Western Europe. Since the inception of the program in 1950, the number of countries receiving grant materiel aid has dropped from a total of 14 to the 4 shown in this year's program. The programs now for these four countries account for only 3.5 percent of our worldwide total. In 1950, some 

75 percent of the program went to Europe. We had the major purpose of building up allied strength against the Communist threat. 

The programs for 10 countries have already been phased out. This trend will continue as we expect to phase out Norway and Denmark in the very near future, leaving only Portugal and Spain.

The remaining pages of this section, commencing on page 40, are historical data of our phaseout programs. 

While Western Europe is approaching military self-sufficiency, many countries in the next section, the Near East and south Asia, still require substantial assistance. 

The summary is shown on pages 54 and 55. In this summary [security deletion]. We are requesting a total of $240 million of grant aid during 1967 for the [security deletion] countries in the area. 

This represents 23.4 percent of the anticipated total obligational authority of the program.

A major emphasis of our total military assistance effort is placed on developing programs which will encourage and elicit maximum self-help from the countries concerned. In regard to this self-help, I would mention that Greece is taking on more of the burden of supporting her own forces and increasing her maintenance and repair facilities. We no longer provide her with any commercial consumables.

Turkey is another country where self-help is producing results. 

[Security deletion.] 

Turkey is also producing increasing amounts of ammunition and more types of ammunition. Another large country in the area, [security deletion] as it becomes economically more viable, [security deletion] is increasing its purchases of military equipment through military sales and relying less on grant aid assistance. 

In past years we have supported major military assistance programs for both India and Pakistan in consonance without military assist ance objectives [security deletion.] 

www

The sharp but brief border clashes in Kashmir and the Punjab last September caused us to suspend all deliveries to both countries on September 8, 1965. In mid-February we announced in the light of the excellent progress made by both countries in withdrawing their troops to positions held in August of 1965 that we would resume on a selected case-by-case basis the sale of nonlethal military equipment to both countries.

This is the status of our policy at the present time. [Security deletion.] 

The other programs in the Near East and south Asia are minor and consist mainly of training. I would turn now to Africa which is summarized on pages 84 and 85. Here through the allocation of military assistance amounting to about 3 percent of our anticipated total obligational authority, we seek to promote three major objectives: the independence of African countries from Communist influence and control, the maintenance of regimes which are capable of maintaining internal stability as a precondition to orderly social and economic development, and the continued United States use of bases in 

Ethiopia and Libya.

Each of the 11-country programs proposed for Africa is designed to promote one or more of these three basic objectives. All are in consonance with the congressional restrictions on military aid. The total African program is $31.8 million, of which $22.2 million is for equipment, an amount which is well below the legislative ceiling of $25 million for defense articles. 

You will note the program for Ethiopia is the largest in the area. 

It reflects both our effort to improve the internal security of that country and [security deletion]. While our other African programs are relatively small, the stakes are large and there is, of course, political instability prevailing through most of the continent which provides a wider range of opportunities for Chinese, Soviet, and other local Communist activities. We therefore join other free world nations in assisting key African countries to improve their stability in order that they may move more rapidly toward political maturity and economic development.

The next regional area is that for the Far East, which is summarized on pages 116 and 117. At the outset of the discussion in this area, I would mention that it should be noted that document reflects the transfer of Vietnam to the military department budgets. Military assistance proposed for remaining recipient countries in the area amounts to [security deletion] million, or [security deletion] percent of our anticipated obligational authority. Since Admiral Sharp will cover this region in detail, I propose therefore to spend no more time on these important programs in the Far East, and to proceed to the Latin American section which runs from pages 140 to 181, and which is summarized on pages 140 and 141. 

In Latin America the specific objectives of military assistance are the development of Latin American forces capable of maintaining internal security against threats of violence and subversion whether Communist inspired or "home grown." We also encourage the armed forces to support and strengthen democratic institutions and to undertake civic action projects.

Finally, we hope to develop selected units which may have possible use in carrying out peacekeeping assignments under the aegis of the Organization of American States and/or the United Nations. [Security deletion.]

I think there is progress in this direction. The program we propose for Latin America is specifically designed to support the internal security and civic action efforts. It provides assistance to 19 countries, as I mentioned. It is in full compliance with the legislative provisions which apply to our effort in Latin America. I would mention the functional breakdown. Internal security takes 53.5 percent, civic action 

13.8 percent, maritime defense, 8 percent, general training 15.5 percent, and supply operations 9.2 percent. This breakout reflects the increased emphasis we are placing on training throughout Latin America. In connection with this I would point out that we are in a transition in our training programs from concentrating on military skills to those of broader scope which provide training in management at levels appropriate to the capabilities of particular groups of trainees. 

This completes my rundown of the country programs. 

Finally, we have the worldwide and regional programs which are summarized on page 183 and which continue through page 200. As shown on page 183, this section includes the U.S. share of NATO infrastructure in the amount estimated as necessary to meet our obligations to this international endeavor. The U.S. support of international military headquarters, administrative expenses, regional costs, and, finally, funds for the credit assistance required in connection with military stales to countries economically capable of purchasing equipment for their defense needs.

The summary of our NATO programs runs from page 184 through page 187. The second category in the worldwide area, "Program administration," has its details on page 188 through page 195. Here our request for funds shows some slight reduction from 1966 expenditures. 

This is shown at the bottom of page 188. 

Regional costs are explained on page 197. You will note we carry here the India-Pakistan program as I mentioned previously. 

The last pages of the book, 201 to 204, contain the details of our proposed credit sales program to which we have allocated $56,7 million in new obligational authority for fiscal year 1967. Mr. Kuss is sched-. uled to appear before you on this part of the program.

I hope that this introduction has given you an insight into the material we have prepared for you and will prove helpful in your consideration of the program this year. STATEMENT OF ADMIRAL U. S. G. SHARP, U.S. NAVY, COMMANDER 

IN CHIEF, PACIFIC Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I appreciate the opportunity to appear again before this distinguished committee. 

Last year in discussing the PACOM military assistance program, my comments encompassed the strategic situation in the Far East, particularly in southeast Asia. My comments this year will follow the same pattern, with emphasis on military assistance programs as they relate to the strategic situation. 

I will address my comments to three main aspects which, taken together, form the backdrop of the military assistance program. 

First, there is the Communist threat, second the strength of friendly and allied forces assisted by the United States, and third, the war in southeast Asia. I shall take up each of these subjects separately. 

First, the threat. The aggressive militant threat of Communist power in Asia continues and that is our basic problem. The primary military threat is, of course, Communist China and their number one asset is their manpower approximately 2,500,000 in their armed forces.

During the past year, China's threat has focused increasingly on Vietnam. The Chinese Communists are obviously testing the effectiveness of so-called wars of national liberation as an instrument for the achievement of Communist control over other nations. The war in southeast Asia will provide a critical test of the future success or failure of that concept. They must already be having some second thoughts about the ultimate success of these tactics. Further, I believe that the Chinese Communists probably now realize that they cannot force the United States to abandon the free countries of the Far East.

Meanwhile, the heaviest concentration of Chinese scarce resources has been allocated to the development of nuclear weapons. This has been done even though living standards, while slowly recovering from the debacle of the great leap forward, remains extremely low. Within the next few years it is possible that they will have a very limited medium range ballistic missile capability which will expand during the next decade.

This threat could be used in the Far East as a form of regional blackmail and to enhance Peiping's prestige. [Security deletion.] 

As you well know, our forces in Vietnam have been rapidly increased this last year. Korea and Australia have also made significant contributions to free world military strength there. [Security deletion.]

While most of these increased forces are positioned with respect to the war in Vietnam, we still must support our allies around the whole perimeter of the west Pacific and maintain adequate reserves capable of rapid deployment when and where needed. [Security deletion.] 

South Korea, although under an armistice, still faces the threat of 

[security deletion] North Korean line divisions [security deletion] 

in position for rapid movement south. It can be assumed the Chinese 

Communists can substantially reinforce the North Koreans in a relatively short time with [security deletion]. We must maintain effec-

tive Republic of Korea and United States forces in Korea to face this threat. [Security deletion.] 

As you know, about 21,000 troops of the Republic of Korea are on duty in Vietnam. The Tiger Division, which arrived in South Vietnam last October, has performed very well. It is a well equipped, well trained, and a most capable unit. [Security deletion.] These forces testify to the effectiveness of our military assistance program. 

Japan has made progress, with our assistance [security deletion] in building its self-defense forces which are almost wholly selfsupported. The Japanese maintain their own internal security, provide security for United States and Japanese military facilities and, in coordination with the United States, contribute to defense against external aggression. The Japanese air self-defense force has assumed responsibility for the air defense of Japan. [Security deletion.] We are continuing to advise the Japanese, to provide training, and to assist in their purchase of U.S. military equipment. 

[Security deletion.] 

The Republic of China armed forces, with a strength of approximately 600,000, are not only effective in preventing Chinese Communist invasion of Taiwan and the Pescadores, but also tie down a substantial number of divisions on mainland China. Nationalist 

China is a major link in our defense strategy. Its bases, airfields, and early warning capabilities are valuable in supporting free world defensive requirements [security deletion]. Also, the Nationalist 

Chinese have provided nonmilitary assistance to the Republic of Viet nam. [Security deletion.] 

Our national policy toward grant-aid recipient countries has emphasized the need to develop self-sufficiency programs. Under this policy, the Republic of China has increased her production capabilities at the Combined Service Forces arsenals and factories. They are manufacturing parts for rebuilding of World War II vehicles which constitute the majority of all vehicles in their armed forces. [Security deletion.]

Our military assistance program has been and will continue to be a key factor in the efforts of the countries I have mentioned to resist Communist domination, and this leads me to southeast Asia. [Security deletion.]

Our ally, Thailand, has been threatened by the Chinese Communists that it will be the next target of the "war of national liberation." [Security deletion.] Thailand does not intend to become the victim of aggression. [Security deletion.] 

Our military assistance program in Thailand is concentrated on equipping and training units that are required for counterinsurgency. [Security deletion.] Within our materiel program we are also attempting to modernize their armed forces in accord with their capability to use and maintain the equipment. We have confidence that Thailand can and will put forth the effort to justify the military assistance we will offer. [Security deletion.]

At this point, I want to speak briefly about the war in Vietnam. 

As you know, the funding for Vietnamese forces is no longer contained in the military assistance program. However, the countries of southeast Asia are so interrelated that a review of the Vietnam situation is pertinent to these hearings.

In 1965, the size of the enemy forces in South Vietnam and the pattern of infiltration changed significantly. Infiltration had been used primarily to encadre the Vietcong, [security deletion] but in 1965, units of the regular North Vietnamese Army were introduced as tactical fighting units. We have identified several North Vietnamese units of regimental strength in South Vietnam [security deletion]. 

A new element recently appeared with reports of the infiltration of an antiaircraft battalion. [Security deletion.] To our knowledge, this would be the first unit introduced solely to provide protection against aircraft.

It is estimated that the trails through Laos and the southern part of North Vietnam have the capacity to move over 4,500 men per month and may have actually achieved that level toward the end of 1965. Of course, the routes must also carry ammunition, supplies, and equipment. [Security deletion.] 

What is Hanoi's strategy? They probably believed that the introduction of their regular army units last year would provide the extra combat power needed to achieve complete victory some time in 1965. This did not happen because we committed U.S. and other free world forces to combat. The enemy's strategy now seems to be based on the conviction that we will tire before he does. Hanoi has stated that the enormous costs of our long supply lines, coupled with the casualties they intend to inflict on U.S. forces will cause us to negotiate on their terms. The Communists bank on their willingness to expend lives at a rate we would consider unacceptable. 

Now as to the forces on our side. Vietnamese regulars total about 

300,000, of which 267,000 are army. [Security deletion.] The paramilitary forces which now number about 250,000 are steadily increasing their strength. South Korean, Australian, and New Zealand forces total about 23,000 and United States forces now exceed 220,000. 

Now to turn to our air operations against the enemy. What has been the result of this campaign? We have concluded that it has a considerable impact on North Vietnam. [Security deletion.] 

What is the military outlook in South Vietnam? In the long run, the war must be won in the south. There are some signs of declining morale among the Vietcong. The enemy's established base areas in 

South Vietnam are no longer secure because of our ground operations and B-52 raids. The South Vietnamese Army is slowly increasing in size and effectiveness. Our great advantages of firepower and superior mobility will become increasingly effective as our units gain experience.

On the other hand, we face a long and grim struggle, but with the prospect of the pendulum swinging slowly to the side of South Vietnam. How fast and how decisively this swing occurs depends on many factors. However, the most important step toward achieving our objectives in South Vietnam will have been taken when we convince Hanoi that we are going to keep on fighting until our objectives are achieved.

We are engaged in a difficult war in southeast Asia which is demanding a large part of our military resources in the Pacific. Our prospects of achieving our objectives are good. At the same time, we

are strengthening the free countries on the periphery of communism in Asia.

Our overall military position in the Far East is favorable because of the progress made through the military assistance program. The forces developed by this program, backed by U.S. strength, are maintaining a strong bulwark against Communist subversion. The military assistance program has, in addition, given our allies the confidence of their own strength and encouraged their confidence in the United States. It was the Communist threat which brought this program into existence and it is the same threat which requires its continuation. I urge your continued support. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Admiral Sharp. 

Admiral Sharp, the United States is still continuing to assume the cost of our Korean fighting forces in Vietnam. There have been reports that we pay per diem for every man as well as transportation and equipment costs.

I think it would be desirable for this committee to have a clear statement as to which expenses of the Korean forces in Vietnam are being paid by the Defense Department. Can you give us a breakdown on them? (A classified memorandum was subsequently supplied in response to Chairman Morgan.) Admiral SHARP. Essentially the Republic of Korea Government is paying the costs they would have incurred had the troops remained in South Korea. We are paying the costs that are incremental and associated with their operations in South Vietnam. Their pay comes in two parts. One is what you would call their normal compensation, and basically that is paid by the Korean Government. The other is what you would call temporary duty compensation, and essentially that is being paid by us, not directly to the soldiers but by additional payments made by us to their Government which their Government in turn uses to pay them.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Admiral Sharp, on behalf of the Far East Study Mission, I want to again express our deep appreciation for your kindness during our stopoff in Hawaii. Your briefings were extremely helpful during our onward travels to Vietnam and other countries in Asia. We became somewhat alarmed at certain figures that we received. They differed from place to place. I presume, Mr. Chairman, that we will have inserted in the record for the information of the committee statistics on the number of Vietcong and the U.S. forces and our allied forces killed in action in Vietnam.

During my recent trip to South Vietnam, I found considerable difficulty, for example, in getting accurate statistics on the ratio of civilians killed during a "search and clear" operation to the number of Vietcong killed. The ratios I received varied from 9 to 1 to 6 to 1 in one area to less than 1 to 1 in another place. It appears that two civilian casualties for each Vietcong killed is a more likely ratio. 

I still find myself greatly puzzled as to what is the accepted or announced figure by the Defense Department. I hope there is some way that we might get this clarified, because the newspapers are making some unfortunate guesses about it. 

Admiral Sharp, you say we must win in Vietnam. I think the question in the minds of most of the people of the United States is, Will we win, and what will be considered a win? In your statement you reported that the strength of the Red Chinese was approximately 

212 million men. How effective are these 212 million men? Where are they deployed? How big a threat is Communist China to the countries on its periphery?

Beginning in late January the Far East and Pacific Subcommittee held a number of hearings concerning Red China. There were a

number of differences of opinion among the witnesses as to how much of a threat Red China is. Also there were differing views as to whether Red China would enter into the conflict in Vietnam, and at what point.

Admiral SHARP. As to the civilian casualties, of course, there are some civilian casualties. We do our utmost to hold these to a minimum. I think we have been fairly successful. However, this is a war. 

In war civilians get killed along with military, unfortunately. All 

I can say is that I know the Secretary of Defense is trying to obtain these figures. They are very difficult to obtain. When you are fighting under the conditions that you saw in Vietnam, it is very difficult to determine the number of casualties on the other side, and even more difficult to determine the number of civilians who may have gotten into the line of fire. As to the size of the Chinese Communist armed forces, they are large, as I have said. Of course, there are variations in the state of training of this large army. 

The better divisions are good fighting troops.

It must be a difficult item to ascertain-if they move 10 hours a day, that would be 15 per hour. There would be logistical aspects involved there. 

Thank you very much, sir. 

(The information follows:) INFILTRATION FROM NORTH VIETNAM 

The 4,500 figure consistently used as the estimated monthly capability of North Vietnamese to infiltrate combat troops into South Vietnam is neither a fixed nor a magic number. It is rather the result of careful evaluation of pertinent factors. For example, we now have persuasive evidence, in part from extensive interrogations of North Vietnamese soldiers captured in South Vietnam, that during the winter of 1963 and spring of 1964, Hanoi embarked on a major program to increase infiltration, including infiltration of regular units of the North Vietnamese Army into South Vietnam through the Laos infiltration trails that had been used as early as 1960. 

We know from a study of our aerial photography that, concurrently, North Vietnam was completing the construction of a string of infiltration staging and supply points within its own territory and in Laos. Throughout 1965, we observed the extension of motorable routes southward. Most recently, for example, our reconnaissance has documented the systematic expansion of motorable routes deep into southern Laos. Segments observed have been constructed to within 15 miles of the tri-border area of Cambodia-Laos-South Vietnam, and clearing operations for this new road extend to within 5 miles of the border. 

In short, what emerges from thousands of photographs is a picture of an enormous effort by North Vietnam, beginning more than a year before American combat troops were in South Vietnam, to develop supply lines and to transport supplies through Laos to South Vietnam.

It is from analysis of information such as this that we have developed our estimate that North Vietnam has the capability to generate and infiltrate up to 

4,500 combat troops monthly.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Admiral Sharp and Admiral 

Heinz.

COMPARISON OF CENTURION AND PATTON TANKS 

A number of observers and commentators on the Indo-Pak border war last September have examined and reflected upon the outcome of its fierce armored engagements, and several British analysts have contended that the Centurion

tanks employed by the Indians proved superior to the U.S.-built Patton tanks with which the Paks were equipped. Although the record is by no means clear in this area, postbattle evaluation strongly suggests this was not in fact the case. 

The ultimate advantage in armored engagements depends to a large degree on variables which are always difficult to assess. Of these, the most important are the effective technical employment of armor, the degree of training of tank crews, and the terrain.

No tank is designed to withstand completely penetration by all antitank weapons because the factors of weight versus vulnerability have such a profound effect upon mobility (i.e., as armor thickness increases, weight increases, and mobility decreases). The heavier the tank, the greater the problems of track ground pressure, bridging limitations, and logistics support. 

Further, penetration of a tank hull is also a function of range and type of projectile; i.e., a high-velocity kinetic energy round of armor-piercing ammunition when fired at close range will penetrate a greater thickness of armor plate than the same round fired at a greater range.
