WEDNESDAY, MARCH 30, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C. 

The committee met in executive session at 10:25 a.m., in room 2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets this morning in executive session on a continuation of the hearings on H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450, the foreign aid bills for fiscal year 1967.

Our witnesses this morning are the Honorable Robert S. McNamara, Secretary of Defense, and Gen. Earle G. Wheeler, U.S. Army, Chair man of the Joint Chiefs of Staff. 

Both you and the general have prepared statements. I understand both of you want to insert your statements and make short informal statements.

STATEMENT OF HON. ROBERT S. MCNAMARA, SECRETARY OF DEFENSE 

Secretary MCNAMARA. If that is agreeable with you and the members of the committee, we would propose to do that. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, both statements will be incorporated in the record. (The statements of Secretary McNamara and General Wheeler are as follows:)

STATEMENT OF ROBERT S. MCNAMARA, SECRETARY OF DEFENSE, BEFORE THE HOUSE FOREIGN AFFAIRS COMMITTEE IN SUPPORT OF THE FISCAL YEAR 1967 MILITARY ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, today, for the seventh time in 6 years, it is my privilege to testify before the Foreign Affairs Committee in support of the military assistance program. As I have stated on each previous occasion, I remain convinced that military assistance is an important instrument of U.S. foreign policy and a significant extension of our own national defense posture.

Since I last appeared before this committee in support of the military assistance program, we have been forced by the infiltration of increasing numbers of regular North Vietnamese troops into South Vietnam to employ U.S. combat forces there. And the need to achieve greater efficiency in the joint United 

States-South Vietnamese military effort has made it necessary to separate military assistance to South Vietnam from the remainder of the military assistance program. By shifting the cost of supporting South Vietnam's forces to the Department of Defense budget we have relieved the military assistance program of some of the pressures which in recent years have threatened to distort seriously the remainder of the country programs. I believe the military assistance pro- 265

gram can now be maintained on a more rational basis, and that we will be able to avoid some of the political and economic costs of reprograming to meet the rising costs of active hostilities in Vietnam. Moreover, we should be able to plan ahead more effectively and to relate the development of free world forces more effectively to the development of our own forces. 

During the last year I reviewed the military assistance program for the next 5 years, based on a detailed reappraisal of selected country programs. This reappraisal examined the threat, the proposed strategy of defense, the strength and missions of local forces, and the strength and missions of U.S. forces likely to be available, with respect to major recipients of military assistance. The analysis also endeavored to evaluate, in each case, the extent to which political. considerations are important determinants of the size and character of the military assistance program. Accordingly, it was not confined solely to examining the military rationale for MAP.

I want to assure you that this reappraisal has confirmed the fundamental validity of our military assistance program and demonstrated anew its vital contributions to the attainment of our military, political, and economic objectives. 

Moreover, I am convinced as a result of the reappraisal that we can make significant progress within a $1 billion program toward meeting the most critical needs for modernizing free world forces. Our studies indicate, however, that a heavy burden of modernization costs will be with us through the 5-year planning period. 

Further, they indicate the need for continuing attention to the interrelationships between U.S. and MAP-supported forces if we are to achieve balance in free world military capabilities and to get the most for our MAP dollars. 

Now, with these points in mind, let us turn to a consideration of the proposed fiscal year 1967 military assistance and sales program. It is entirely consistent with the provisions of the new Military Assistance and Sales Act of 1966 which has been submitted to the Congress as separate legislation and introduced by your distinguished chairman. I am sure you are fully conversant with the general tenor of the bill. In addition to requesting a 5-year authorization, it calls for:

Effective coordination between economic and military programs. There will be no change in existing policy with respect to coordinating military assistance with economic assistance and foreign policy. 

Greater emphasis on self-help by those we aid. 

Greater emphasis on civic action programs for nation building. 

Emphasis on training and education of foreign military personnel. 

Continued shift from grant aid to military sales wherever possible.

THE FISCAL YEAR 1967 REQUEST 

You may recall that when I appeared before this committee almost 3 years ago, on April 8, 1963, shortly after the issuance of the Clay Committee report recommending progressive reduction of military assistance programs, I stated that, "barring any large scale unforeseen emergency, we hope to reduce the new obligational authority budget to a level of $1 billion no later than fiscal year 1968." 

The program we are presenting today is based on $917 million in new obligational authority. The total obligational authority in the amount of $1,027 million results from the addition of an estimated $110 million in recoupments and reappropriations to the $917 million budget request. 

This program-which excludes Vietnam-would be somewhat less than $900 million but for the abnormal requirements elsewhere in southeast Asia. Put another way, it is the equivalent of a $950 million program, including provision for the normal needs of Laos and of Vietnam calculated at roughly double the 

1960 level.

This is the first time since the inception of the program in 1950 that we have been able to ask for less than $1 billion. We have succeeded in reducing the program to the $1 billion level by reducing the number of recipient countries to those which are in demonstrable need of continuing grant military support and which are making maximum use of their own resources, and by concentrating our grant support on those forces whose potential contribution to free world security is the greatest. But I must add a word of caution here. Developments in southeast Asia, particularly in relation to the security of Laos and Thailand, could impose additional requirements for MAP support beyond those provided for in the $917 million request. 

The difficulties inherent in such a reduced program are large. The proposed fiscal year 1967 miiltary assistance program is the product of many hard choices.

The proposed program will meet only the minimum essential needs of allied and friendly forces whose defense capabilities enhance the security of the entire free world. It will provide a modest amount to meet credit assistance needs principally in those countries shifting from grant to sales programs. 

DISTRIBUTION OF THE PROGRAM 

We have once again allocated almost three-fourths of the total program to the 10 countries adjacent to the borders of Soviet Russia and Communist China where our forward strategy draws the front lines of free world common defense. 

Because the armed forces of countries such as Greece, Turkey, Iran, and the Republics of China and Korea are essential elements of the total free world deterrent, it is in our national interest to see that they do not lack the means to match their will to resist aggression. 

In newly independent and developing nations less directly threatened by Communist neighors, the military assistance program is primarily designed to strenghen internal security. Our assistance to such countries will continue to emphasize civic action programs through which indigenous military forces can contribute to nation building. We shall also continue to emphasize the training component of military assistance which has long proved to be one of the most valuable aspects of the program.

We are taking steps to insure that military assistance training complements other United States efforts, referred to in the President's state of the Union message, to encourage a broader and deeper distribution throughout the world of the priceless benefits of education. In this regard, we have just concluded a conference designed to foster greater understanding of the benefits accruing from attendance by foreign military personnel at military and civilian schools in the United States under the military assistance program. Our training program provides unique opportunities to expose potential leaders of the developing nations to our institutions, goals, and ideals and thereby to develop valuable understanding and friendship.

The 55 recipient countries included in the proposed program fall into 5 regional groupings which parallel the organization of your subcommittees and set the pattern for this year's presentation.

FAR EAST 

I shall begin with the Far East, to which area we propose to allocate $387 million or 37.7 percent of our anticipated total obligational authority in fiscal year 1967.

The power situation in the Far East is characterized basically by the great disparity between the inherent economic and military strength of allied and friendly countries and that of Communist China. The small countries located on the fringes of the Chinese land mass-South Vietnam, Laos, Thailand, the Philippines, and the Republic of China and Korea-are inherently at a power disadvantage with respect to their powerful neighbor. The strength of the 

United States, our presence, our will, our commitments to defend them are, therefore, essential ingredients of stability in the Far East. Military assistance is important both for what it contributes directly to the strengh of these nations and as a manifestation of U.S. will and commitment. 

Military assistance to free countries in the Far East area greatly enhances their will and ability to resist external aggression. It enables these small countries to maintain sizable military forces well in excess of their own economic capacities. It strengthens their capabilities to maintain internal security and resist conventional attack, fosters a sense of military and political self-confidence in the face of Communist China's threats and permits them to contribute toward the maintenance of a favorable balance of military power in the Far East. Military assistance thus exerts a favorable impact on the balance of power in Asia and on the maintenance of stability around the rim of mainland China. 

A few brief comments on individual country problems and programs will, I believe, support these general conclusions. Thailand Thailand is a nation of major importance to the United States in southeast Asia because of its size and strategic position on the mainland, its political stability, and its encouraging economic growth. Thailand faces an immediate and growing threat of subversion and insurgency, sponsored by Hanoi and

Peking. By virtue of its geographical position, it is also exposed to the danger of attack from conventional military forces, and the threat of seizure by enemy forces of strategic areas in Laos along the Thai border. 

In the last year Red China has blatantly advertised its preparations for subversive insurgency designed to overthrow the Thai Government; and, in fact, armed bands have become increasingly active in the northeastern provinces bordering Laos. In consideration of the growing internal security problem and the possibility that hostilities in Laos and Vietnam could spread to Thailand, it is clearly in the U.S. interest to strengthen the capabilities of the Thai military forces for counterinsurgency. The proposed Thai MAP program will focus our support on this objective. 

The Philippines 

Our military assistance program for the Philippines provides the means for urgently needed improvements in the training and equipment of Philippine Armed Forces. The Philippines maintains modest forces, in order to insure its internal security and to be prepared to fulfill its obligations as a member of SEATO. 

South Korea 

The Republic of Korea provides a striking example of the benefits we and our allies continue to derive from the military assistance program. More than 20,000 

ROK forces, trained and equipped with military assistance funds, are now deployed and actively engaged in the struggle in Vietnam. These Korean troops are proving again their well-known fighting mettle. Their presence in South Vietnam was made possible by the confidence in U.S. commitments and support which our assistance program has helped to instill. 

The proposed MAP program provides for the modernization of certain critical categories of equipment and for essential operating costs of the substantial Korean forces which stand guard, together with two U.S. divisions, along the demilitarized zone in Korea. I believe the continuing threat in the Far East requires that we continue major military assistance to the Korean Armed Forces. This military assistance will enable the Republic of Korea to maintain these forces without excessive limitations on its promising economic and social progress. 

The Republic of China 

Red China's leaders have clearly stated their intention to use every means open to them to eliminate the Government of China and seize control of Taiwan. Our bilateral security commitment to the defense of Taiwan and the Pescadores remains vital to the survival of the Government of the Republic of China. Although it has been increasingly successful in improving the military supply system, maintaining equipment and bearing an increasing share of its own defense costs, we shall have to continue to supply certain types of military equipment which cannot be produced locally. Military assistance is essential if the burden of defense is not to reach a level endangering the social and economic progress on Taiwan-those advances which serve as a reminder to the mainland of the benefits of freedom. We are now considering a modest military assistance sales program, denoting our confidence in the future economic growth of the Republic of China.

NEAR EAST-SOUTH ASIA 

Because of their importance to the United States, the countries of this area have received almost $5 billion worth of military assistance through fiscal year 1966. We are proposing that they receive another $240 million in military grant aid in fiscal year 1967, representing 23 percent of anticipated total obligational authority. 

Greece and Turkey

In the Near East and south Asia, Greece and Turkey form important elements in our collective security arrangements by protecting the southeast flank of NATO. Their forces, together with those of the United States and other 

NATO allies, constitute a strong deterrent to the Communist military threat. 

The record of our military assistance to Greece and Turkey since 1947 is noteworthy. It has measurably aided in defeating aggression and in preventing its recurrence. It has built effective military capabilities from forces that once were ill-equipped and poorly organized. Furthermore, it is aimed not only at building and maintaining forces to meet the external threat, but also at enabling this to be done without diverting resources critical to their social and economic

16 ? 1 development. Both countries still face serious difficulties in maintaining modern forces with their own resources, even when supplemented by military assistance at the proposed levels.

We have recently begun discussions with both Governments, as a part of an overall NATO effort. to reexamine their present force goals and structures with a view to bringing them into closer alinement with the best estimates of total available resources. To this end, we are encouraging the Governments of 

Greece and Turkey to adopt 5-year force planning. While this proposal does not involve any commitment of U.S. funds, we believe it will nevertheless lead to an improved allocation of those resources (Greek, Turkish, and United States) that can be made available for military purposes. 

I believe it has been demonstrated that achievement of our objectives in Greece and Turkey-containment of the military threat, improvement of force structures, adequate modernization, and economic stability and growth calls for a continuation of substantial U.S. military assistance. For the next fiscal year it will require, at a minimum, the application of MAP funds at the levels requested.

Iran Iran occupies a strategic location between the Soviet Union and the warmwater ports of the Persian Gulf and the important petroleum resources of the Arabian Peninsula. Its military forces, strengthened by our military assistance, constitute a significant deterrent to Communist aggression and help to assure Iran's continued internal stability. U.S. military assistance, in the form of both grants and sales, thus continues to be important in helping to provide the environment necessary for political, social, and economic development.. 

Through military assistance, we contribute to the significant economic progress taking place in Iran, which is already enabling Iran to pay for a greater share of its own security costs. 

South Asia

I now turn to south Asia which has become, through a combination of circumstances and geography, a vital strategic area in the present contest between the expansionist and nonexpansionist power centers. In friendly hands or as nonalined States, south Asia can be a bridge between Europe and the Far East and a major physical barrier to the southward expansion of Red China and the U.S.S.R.; in hostile hands, it would seal the long term hope of building a free 

Asian coalition able to provide adequate counterweight to an expansionist China. 

It is, therefore, an area of great importance and consequence to the United States.

The United States needs national independence and stability in south Asia, and current U.S. strategy assumes this. If India and Pakistan were to be lost to communism or chaos, the struggle in South Vietnam would lose much of its strategic validity. We must recognize, however, that achievement of these aims, which are also those of both India and Pakistan, is dependent on the outcome of complex internal struggles and external conflicts. 

Success in the struggle to develop modern states in both India and Pakistan means coping with the problems of sustaining vast populations while at the same time advancing toward modernization. This central task demands the full energies of the peoples concerned. Its successful accomplishment must not be frustrated by either the divisive effects of territorial disputes or the designs of expansionist powers bent on dominance in Asia. 

With respect to the outbreak of armed conflict between India and Pakistan, as you know, we suspended military aid and commercial sales deliveries on September 8, 1965. We have strongly supported the United Nations Security 

Council resolution of September 20, calling for a cease-fire, for a withdrawal of armed personnel to the positions held before August 5, 1965, and for further consideration of steps that can be taken toward a settlement of the political problem underlying the present conflict. It is encouraging that the Soviets have adopted a similar position as evidenced by their role at the Tashkent 

Conference in January of this year.

In light of India's and Pakistan's compliance with the withdrawal provisions of the Security Council resolutions and with the Tashkent Declaration, and in view of other encouraging steps taken by both to restore peace in the subcontinent, we have modified the ban on military shipments to South Asia to the extent of permitting selected and limited [security delection] sales of nonlethal military equipment on a case-by-case basis. Our ban on grant military

assistance continues. Any further relaxation of our policy will be contingent on the further course of events in south Asia and on a further assessment of the U.S. interest in supporting the military forces of India and Pakistan. 

With respect to Pakistan, we desire improved and cordial relations. We recognize that Pakistan as a sovereign power is going to maintain a respectable level of military forces [security deletion]. With respect to India, an important fact is that we share with that country the basic aim of containing Chinese expansion. It is also true that India's strength and stability are of decisive importance to the question whether Asia is going to develop along democratic or totalitarian lines.

LATIN AMERICA 

Our major objective in Latin America is economic and social development, toward which the entire Alliance for Progress effort is directed. But we cannot hope to attain that objective unless governments throughout the area have efficient and adequate security forces to thwart elements interested in blocking that progress for their own ends. Unfortunately, such elements are at work in almost every Latin American country. There are groups which oppose change simply because they wish to preserve the benefits they derive from maintenance of the status quo. There are also those groups which see a chance to seize power in the atmosphere of dissatisfaction and unrest which results from unsatisfactory social and economic progress. These latter groups are usually, but not always aided and abetted from abroad. 

It is for this reason that our military assistance program for Latin America continues to be oriented toward internal security and civic action. This assistance is not directed at eliminating protest or enforcing conformity, but rather at helping provide an environment in which economic and social tasks can be effectively pursued. Governments must be able to eliminate violence if peaceful change through democratic processes is to be achieved. 

Accordingly, the specific objectives of the military assistance program for Latin America are to encourage and support the development of indigenous forces capable of maintaining internal security against threats of violence and subversion, dealing effectively with guerrilla warfare and the clandestine movement of men and armaments across land, sea, and air borders for subversive purposes. We also seek to augment and facilitate the participation of appropriate Latin American military units in civic action projects. These projects contribute both to the nation building and to the establishment of greater rapport between the populace and the Armed Forces, which frequently represent the authority of the Government in the eyes of the people. The military assistance program also enables us to help those nations that wish to develop selected units for possible use in carrying out peacekeeping missions under the aegis of the OAS and the U.N. Finally, we count upon military assistance programs for Latin America as a channel through which to communicate our continuing interest in fostering a constructive and democratic approach by the military to their professional responsibilities and to the solution of national problems.

The proposed fiscal year 1967 military assistance program for Latin America amounts to $74.9 million. This will provide for 19 individual country programs amounting to [security deletion] million, or [security deletion] of our anticipated total obligational authority, and another [security deletion] million for Latin American regional programs and multilateral military activities which have been given impetus by the "Fulbright amendment" to the Foreign 

Assistance Act. [Security deletion.] 

In accordance with the general and specific objectives I have outlined, the major portion of the proposed program for Latin America [security deletion] will be devoted to strengthening internal security capabilities of indigenous forces, with emphasis on communications, mobility, and counterinsurgency training. Another [security deletion] of the total program is allocated to the provision of equipment and training associated with civic action. Programs for all 19 countries include training for Latin American military personnel at U.S. schools in the Canal Zone and in the United States. Such training not only contributes to the improvement of military skills and professional attitudes, but it also promotes the broader objectives of U.S. policy by exposing Latin 

American military personnel to American concepts and enhancing the mutual understanding and respect which must characterize our relationships with them if our cooperation is to be fully productive.

AFRICA 

For Africa, we propose a military assistance program of $31.8 million for fiscal year 1967 (of which $22.2 million is for equipment and $9.6 million for training and related purposes). This amount, about 3 percent of anticipated total obligational authority, will mainly go to a few countries where the United States has important interests and installations. The balance will be distributed among 10 other countries, chiefly in the form of training programs, communications, and transportation equipment. Programs for six of these countries amount to less than $1 million each. 

U.S. foreign policy in Africa south of the Sahara is directed toward helping these countries to preserve their independence and to achieve the balanced economic, social, and political development essential to freedom and stability. 

Military assistance can play a subordinate but significant role in support of that policy by assisting, where necessary, in the development of forces capable of providing the internal security necessary for progress in national development. 

While the former metropoles are expected to provide continued security assistance, there are certain cases in which U.S. military assistance is the only realistic alternative to unstable internal security situations. In such cases, however, our assistance is carefully designed to prevent its contributing to an arms race. 

The African areas of most immediate strategic concern to the free world are those bordering on the Mediterranean and in the horn. The former guards the southern flank of NATO, and the latter stands at the approaches to the Red Sea. 

Our military assistance helps these countries to maintain internal security and stability essential to their economic and social development. It also helps to lessen the tensions resulting from the inflow into the area of large quantities of 

Soviet materiel and training.

Africa will continue to face many difficult problems in the immediate future, as indicated by the recent flurry of coups. While these coups have been triggered for a variety of reasons, the frail political structure of the new African governments is clearly demonstrated. At the same time, the Communists are vigorously pursuing programs designed to enhance their influence. 

Until the African nations have had an opportunity to develop their economies and strengthen their political institutions, there will be a wide variety of opportunities for both Sino-Soviet and local Communist activities. We should therefore continue, within the resources available for this purpose, to join other free world nations in assisting certain African nations to improve their stability in order that they may effectively combine economic aid and their own resources to move more rapidly toward their own chosen political and economic goals. Withholding such assistance at the present time would greatly handicap the countries concerned in their efforts to build stable and viable nations. 

EUROPE 

Today, Western Europe presents a picture of unprecedented prosperity and stability, thanks in no small measure to the great efforts which the American people have made to rehabilitate the war-ravaged economies of that area and to bolster its defense against the Soviet threat. NATO, in its 17th year of existence, continues, despite what one reads in the press, to fulfill the purposes for which it was created by its members and "to unite their efforts for collective defense and for the preservation of peace and security."

Our own basic objectives in Western Europe are simply to insure the security of that area against Communist aggression and to further its economic growth and political stability. We are neither unaware of, nor dismayed by the problems confronting us in or about NATO. Despite the disruptive actions of a present member, France, our support of the most successful defensive alliance in history will continue unabated. While we regret French actions, we know that our allies share in our determination to maintain the strength and cohesiveness of NATO. We are, and will remain, committed to the North Atlantic Alliance because it continues to provide an indispensable means of maintaining the collective security of the West.

I believe that the accomplishments of NATO over the past year have demonstrated that the alliance is fully responsive to changing circumstances. As long as the Soviet Union represents a major potential military threat in Europe, there will be no substitute more effective than the collective and integrated defense of the West. The changes which have taken place in the nature of the Soviet

threat to Western Europe in recent years have not affected the basic realities that made the alliance a political and military necessity some 17 years ago. 

Those same 17 years have, however, brought a tremendous change in Western European requirements for military assistance. Although more than half of our total investment since the inception of the program in 1950 has gone into the NATO buildup, the signal success of that common defense effort, together with the rapid economic recovery of Western Europe, have made possible a corresponding reduction in the proportionate amount of military assistance allocated to the area. This reduction is clearly reflected in the drop from a high of 79 percent to a low of 3.5 percent which represents the $36 million earmarked for grant aid to Western Europe in fiscal year 1967. 

Today, all NATO nations except Greece and Turkey, which I have already discussed, are capable of providing for their own self- and common-defense needs. 

Of the original 14 Western European recipients of grant aid, including both NATO and non-NATO countries, only 4 will continue to receive grant aid in fiscal year 1967. Two of these-Norway and Denmark--will also be phased out as soon as prior-year commitments are completed in the near future. Grant aid programs for the other two-Spain and Portugal-will be continued in consonance with our continuing need for the important bases and installations which they make available to the United States. 

WORLDWIDE AND REGIONAL 

In addition to the funds we propose to allocate to the 55 individual country programs included in the 5 regional groups I have been discussing, another $259.6 million will be required to provide for the various functions and costs which are not properly chargeable to those individual country programs but which are vitally important to optimum use of military assistance as an instrument of national policy. These several items have been brought together, for presentation and accounting purposes, under the title of "Worldwide and Re gional Programs," as follows: 

NATO infrastructure.. 

International military headquarters.

Administration_. 

Regional programs..

Credit sales_. 

Millions $90.0 20.4 23.5 69.0 56.7

I shall leave detailed discussion of these items to subsequent witnesses who will be prepared to deal with each in any depth the committee desires. I do, however, want to make two or three general points. 

First, the $90 million listed for NATO infrastructure is the amount required in fiscal year 1967 to fund the agreed cost of the U.S. share in the development of joint use facilities essential to effective deployment of U.S. and other national forces committed to NATO. The increase of $20 million over the amount of last year's share is attributable to accelerated progress in the NATO air defense ground environment (NADGE) program which is designed to fill a longstanding need in the Western European common defense posture. 

Second, despite steady increases in component costs, we have managed to reduce the administrative expenses of the military assistance program to $23.5 million-a slightly lower level than last year. 

Third, $69 million is earmarked for regional programs, including the $2.9 million I mentioned earlier as the amount programed for support of regional and multinational undertakings in Latin America. Sales

I want to make special mention of the $56.7 million credit sales item listed among worldwide and regional programs. This amount represents only the new obligational authority which we need to augment credit funds already available in our MAP revolving account for sales. It will enable us to provide a total of $244 million in credit sales, mainly for developing countries, many of whom are shifting from grant to sales. This credit, supplemented by credit available through private and Eximbank sources for the industrially developed countries, and combined with cash sales, should make it possible to achieve military export sales orders of at least $1.5 billion in fiscal year 1967. 

The use of military assistance funds to facilitate military export sales in cases where the purchasing nation cannot afford to buy for cash is clearly consistent with both the national interest and the intent and spirit of the pending

Military Assistance and Sales Act of 1966. The director of the sales program will testify later in support of the sales portion of the proposed new legislation. 

He will elaborate on our efforts to encourage appropriate purchase of military equipment from the United States by allied and friendly countries economically capable of making such purchases. These efforts are directed toward the dual objective of shifting from grant aid to military sales wherever possible and of exerting a favorable influence on the U.S. balance of payments. Our growing sales program is closely coordinated within the Government to insure its compatibility with our economic assistance program and with foreign policy. 

SUPPORTING ASSISTANCE 

Although supporting assistance is a responsibility of the Agency for International Development rather than the Department of Defense, it is an integral part of our total effort to promote free world peace and progress. Once again this year, I consider it appropriate to advocate favorable legislative action on the administration's $747.2 million fiscal year 1967 request therefor. The $550 million earmarked for Vietnam is the largest single item in the proposed economic aid budget, and it is vital to an eventually favorable outcome of the struggle in the hard-pressed country. Another $197 million will be used for aid to countries whose security is directly threatened. It is concentrated in programs for Laos, Korea, and Thailand, which are directly threatened. 

CONCLUSION 

It is my firm and final conclusion, afer taking into careful consideration all pertinent factors and possible alternatives, that the authorization and the appropriations requested by the administration are clearly consistent with the national interest and represent the most economical means for attaining the specific security and foreign policy objectives which military assistance is designed to promote. I know of no other means by which we can make equivalent contributions to our own and to free world security at comparable cost. I sincerely hope that your own evaluation of the proposed program will lead you to the same conclusions. 

[Security deletion.] STATEMENT BY Gen. Earle G. Wheeler, U.S. Army, ChAIRMAN OF THE JOINT CHIEFS OF STAFF, BEFORE THE HOUSE FOREIGN AFFAIRS COMMITTEE ON MILI- TARY ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I welcome this opportunity to appear before you in support of the military assistance program for the fiscal year 1967.

The Joint Chiefs of Staff consider an adequate military aid program to be an essential and integral part of the military strategy of the United States, a required complement to our own national military programs. 

Cooperative effort and joint commitment by allied and friendly nations have created a free world military posture which represents a total strength far greater than the strength of any single nation. The concept of military assistance to free world countries is a response to a spectrum of threat to the United States and to U.S. worldwide security interests as well as to these free world countries. This response is set forth in the joint strategic objectives plan which translates national objectives and policies into military objectives, prescribes strategic concepts for the employment of forces, and defines basic undertakings to achieve these objectives and concepts. The MAP is not a simple extension of U.S. forces and military power but is a reflection of the mutual security interests of the United States and MAP recipients involving political as well as military factors. Thus, U.S. forces supplement and complement; they can substitute and offset only in a limited sense. Furthermore, our U.S. capability, even considering projected increases in mobility, power, and versatility, is not without limit. Equivalent dollars applied to U.S. force levels will not provide an equivalent free world posture [security deletion]. A direct and consistent correlation of enemy threat, MAP funding levels, and U.S. objectives in the strategic context is difficult to establish arithmetically. The many political and diplomatic variables which affect MAP appropriations; international political, economic, and military instability which impact on specific U.S. objectives; and the existence of open hostilities in Vietnam, have required reasoned judgments where quantitative computations could not be made. 

---

In the end, the military assistance we recommend is pared down to those countries that are of particular importance to our security and to those military forces within those countries that bear a particular complementary relation to our own. Thus, we in the Joint Chiefs of Staff are constantly reminded of the interlocking relationships existing between our national military programs and those of military assistance. I trust the committee will also keep in mind this important relationship.

The Joint Chiefs of Staff have viewed the level of the military assistance programs of the last 3 years with concern. Annual appropriations at the current level will barely provide the required day-to-day operating costs of ships, aircraft, and equipment necessary to prevent backsliding in current capabilities. 

Since fiscal year 1964 many well-planned force improvements have had to be deferred indefinitely. This deferment is the more significant because the quality of the equipment of the armed forces of the Soviet Union, Communist China, and the other Communist-controlled nations is improving steadily. While we are not unmindful of the fact that there are considerations other than military, such as our ultimate political and economic objectives for the several areas, which must be evaluated in determining the level of the annual military assistance program, we are of the opinion that further reductions below the current level are not justified by the military situation confronting the United States and its free world allies today and in the near term. 

Almost three-fourths of this year's program will go to support forces in countries on the periphery of the great Communist land mass. These funds will enable Korea, the Philippines, Republic of China, Thailand, Laos, Iran, Turkey, and Greece to maintain almost 2 million men under arms in collective defense of mutual security interests, i.e., those which we share with them. It would be far more expensive if we had to replace any part of this 2 million men with men from our own Armed Forces. None of these peripheral countries has the capacity to assume full support of its forces which are essential to our mutual security interests. It is appropriate for them to maintain their forces in the interest of common defense, and it is appropriate for us to assist in support of these forces. 

To reduce our assistance is to weaken forward defense forces and is an invitation to Communist effort to occupy the power vacuums that could result. 

In my opinion, reduction of the funds allocated to such country programs would be the poorest kind of economy for the United States, and one for which we might ultimately have to pay many times over. 

Of the small remainder of the annual program, the assistance to our Latin American neighbors is extremely important. Our country programs for this area are oriented primarily toward internal security. Not to recognize the era of social, economic, and political change that our Latin American neighbors are facing would be to ignore a key trend of our time. Change is a traditional feature of Latin American politics, and in recent years it has been the military establishment which in many instances has provided the greatest stabilizing factor for orderly change and continuity of constitutional government. There are other nations, of course, who would gladly offer military assistance to our Latin American friends in order to gain opportunities for political infiltration. 

In short, this is no time for the United States to reduce this part of the military assistance program below a level that will further these ends. 

Those remaining funds programed for other areas and purposes, while relatively small in dollars, are significant since they are tied to our own special interests and specific obligations. The military assistance program as a whole is a very tightly constructed package. 

To conclude, in the words of the President, this MAP budget "is the bare minimum required if we are to keep our commitments to our allies and friendly armed forces to provide the equipment and training essential to free world defense." I assure you that the program submission before you, which is minimal, has the full support of the Joint Chiefs of Staff. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Secretary MCNAMARA. There lies in front of you a table summariz ing the fiscal 1967 military assistance program as we propose it to be authorized and for which we ask appropriations. 

I would like to comment briefly on the program, compare it with prior years, and draw attention to certain of its major elements, and then take your questions.

You will notice that there is circled in red under the fiscal 1967 column the figure $917 million. That is the requested authorization for military assistance for fiscal 1967, excluding the military assistance to be provided to the armed forces of South Vietnam, funds for which have been provided in the regular defense budget. 

You will also notice on the same line with the $917 million under the fiscal 1961 column, the figure $1,683 million. That was the authorization and appropriation in fiscal 1961, excluding the funds for Vietnam. 

You can see, therefore that between fiscal 1961 and fiscal 1967, the authorization will have dropped from $1,683 million to the requested level of $917 million, a reduction of $770 million over the period. 

This downtrend illustrates the pressure we have put on reducing the cost of military assistance to the lowest level consistent with our own security. 

We think that in the future we can keep it below a billion dollars, including an allowance for normal expenditures in South Vietnam. 

When appearing before the Clay Committee in 1963 and also when appearing before this committee in 1963, I stated it would be our objective to reach by 1968 a level of a billion dollars in the military assistance program. We have achieved that level this year. We believe we can continue to do so in future years. 

Next, note the figures circled in blue in the fiscal 1967 column, the first of which is $40.2 million. This is the total amount proposed for the nations of Europe. The two biggest items in that amount are 

[security deletion] for Norway and [security deletion] for Spain. 

However, fiscal 1968, data for which doesn't appear on this table, the Norwegian program will drop to [security deletion]. In other words, for all practical purposes, this [security deletion] in fiscal 1967 completes the Norwegian military assistance program. 

The amount allowed for Spain [security deletion] and is associated with a program under which Spain is to procure in the United States a specified quantity of equipment to be paid for in dollars. 

The total amount provided for Western Europe, as I noted earlier, is some $40 million.

Next, the figure for Africa, a total of $32 million in fiscal 1967. It is made up of two sizable items, one for Ethiopia [security deletion] 

and [security deletion] the other for Libya. [Security deletion.] In effect, we have nothing but a training program for Africa apart from [security deletion] the programs in these five countries. 

Next, you can see that in fiscal 1967 we propose for Latin America $75 million. This is the total military assistance program for 

19 countries. [Security deletion.] 

The remaining figure circled in blue is the amount requested for "credit assistance" for fiscal 1967-$56.7 million. This amount of loans we believe will help finance sales of about $245 million. [Security deletion.] These loans will greatly assist our sales program and will be bona fide loans that will be repaid in dollars. 

The bulk of the program is represented by the amounts circled in green. Under the Near East and South Asia section and the Far 

Eastern section, there are 10 countries circled in green [security deletion] for which we are proposing about $680 million, or some 75 percent of the total military assistance program. Those are the countries that lie on the periphery of the Soviet and Chinese Communist blocs.

They are the one that are in greatest danger of aggression from Soviet- 

Chinese sources.

Mr. MORSE. Mr. Chairman, might I interrupt for clarification of the 

Secretary's testimony there? [Security deletion.] 

Secretary MCNAMARA. At the present time, we are only selling to 

India-Pakistan, not granting military aid, and we are limiting our military sales to nonlethal equipment. [Security deletion.] 

Mr. MORSE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Secretary MCNAMARA. In any event, the 10 countries circled in green would, in total, receive about $680 million under the terms of this proposal, some 75 percent of the total military assistance program. 

They are the ones we are concentrating on because they are on the periphery of the Sino-Soviet bloc, and they are the nations that face the potential aggression from that bloc. 

This summarizes our program, Mr. Chairman. General Wheeler has one or two comments to make, and then we will be happy to take your questions.

Chairman MORGAN. General Wheeler. 

STATEMENT OF GEN. EARLE G. WHEELER, U.S. ARMY, CHAIRMAN, JOINT CHIEFS OF STAFF General WHEELER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I wish to tell the committee that the Joint Chiefs of Staff consider an adequate military aid program to be an essential and integral part of the military strategy of the United States, a required complement, indeed, to our own national military programs. Also, as I am sure members of the committee know, the Joint Chiefs of Staff consider the military assistance program as a part of our overall strategic planning. 

The military assistance program is not a simple extension of U.S. forces and military power, but a reflection of the mutual security interests of the United States and the military assistance program recipients. Finally, I wish to assure the committee that this program lying before you has the full support of the Joint Chiefs of Staff. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, General.

Secretary MCNAMARA. [Security deletion] cost to the north of that movement and the movement of the associated materiel.

1966 ?

Secretary MCNAMARA. It is slightly higher. It was $878 million for fiscal 1966. We feel the increase is justified because of the very severe reduction that we made in fiscal 1964, 1965, and 1966 in the programs in [security deletion] in order to finance the unanticipated costs of our programs in South Vietnam and Laos. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Secretary, yesterday when Admiral Heinz was here I asked him a few questions about why some of our so-called friends around the world were receiving military assistance. In 1967 large demands have been made on us in South Vietnam, and I wonder why military assistance to some other countries could not be reduced. 

You feel then what you are requesting here for 1967, which as you say represents a slight increase for the other countries around the world, is absolutely necessary? 

Secretary MCNAMARA. Yes, sir; I do. These amounts are substantially below the amounts recommended by the unified commanders, Admiral Sharp, General Lemnitzer whom you will hear, and the other unified commanders. They are substantially less than the amounts recommended by the Joint Chiefs of Staff. They are the absolute minimum that I consider consistent with our own security. 

I have said to this committee before, and I would repeat it now, that there are no other dollars in the $60 billion defense program for fiscal year 1967 which contribute as much in my opinion to the security of this Nation as these particular MAP dollars. 

I hope it won't be necessary for the Congress to reduce the total defense program of some $60 billion; but if it is necessary to do so for reasons that the Congress believes right and proper, the reduction should be taken out of the other $59 billion and not out of this $917 million, in my opinion. I think that opinion is more than shared by the Joint Chiefs.

General WHEELER. I agree with the point expressed by the Secretary, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Secretary, the $680 million which you marked in the green, you feel is definitely essential in each case? 

Secretary MCNAMARA. Yes, sir. In every case, those amounts are substantially less than the ambassadors have recommended, less than the senior military commanders in the areas have recommended, less than the unified commanders have recommended, and less than the 

Joint Chiefs have recommended. [Security deletion.] 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Secretary, in fiscal year 1966, was it necessary for you to shortchange any other country that had a military assistance program in order to take care of our needs in Vietnam? 

Secretary MCNAMARA. Yes, it was.

The original estimate for fiscal 1966 for South Vietnam was $230 million. The best estimate we can make today for fiscal 1966 for

South Vietnam is $795 million. There was an increase from $230 to $795 million, a total increase of some $565 million. 

That increase for South Vietnam was financed by three specific actions: (1) a drawdown of existing inventory under the provision of section 510 to the extent of $300 million; (2) a supplemental budget request of $204 million, and (3) transfers from other nations of some $65 million. These transfers hurt because the other nations had been put in at what were considered to be minimum amounts initially. The answer to your question is that we have reduced other nations in 1966 to help finance South Vietnam. It won't be necessary to do that in 

1967 because we have transferred the South Vietnamese program out of the military assistance program into the basic defense budget. One of the reasons we did it was to avoid a whiplash effect because of the unpredictable nature of expenditures in South Vietnam, on the remaining countries in the program.

Secretary MCNAMARA. The basic change in our legislative request is a proposal for a multiyear authorization for the program. Mr. Secretary, would you like to use the rest of my time in answering or commenting upon the military situation in Vietnam and how we are doing out there and how long you foresee this is going on?

I would like you to specifically tell us if you think we ought to close the port of Haiphong by one method or another, either by bombing or mining or what have you. If you think not, why not?

Secretary MCNAMARA. May I take about 2 minutes of the rest of your time and then ask General Wheeler to use the rest of the time. 

How are we doing? The dominant factor affecting operations in

Vietnam today is the continuing expansion of the Vietcong forces (a) by the infiltration from the north of approximately 4,500 men a month, and (b) by further recruitment, in part by terror tactics, of some 3,000, 4,000, or 5,000 men a month in the south. Through this recruitment and infiltration they are able to offset the very high fatalities they are now suffering and, in addition, expand their battalions. 

We think they may, in the calendar 1966; expand their force in terms of battalions by about 50 percent. This is important for two reasons. 

First, it indicates that unless we are to be in a constantly weakening military position, we must continue to expand our U.S. combat forces 

Second, it indicates a conscious decision by the North Vietnamese to intensify the level of conflict. This increases our risks for the future. We have expanded our force. We have about 230,000 men there today compared to roughly 185,000 at the end of last year. 

[Securit v deletion.] 

The air operations have expanded dramatically. They are being carried on against [security deletion] the infiltration routes and North Vietnamese targets at a level of about twice the rate just prior to the pause in December. The air operations in the south have also expanded substantially. In total we are dropping, in terms of tons of bombs, about two and a half times the bomb tonnage this month than was dropped in the average month of the Korean war. 

I mentioned the fatalities. The fatalities last week, by body count, approximated 1,980 Vietcong and North Vietnamese. This is the highest loss in any single week. They have been suffering fatalities at the rate of about a thousand a week, which is 50 percent above the level of last year. We think that the high level of fatalities and the continued bombing in the north are having an adverse effect on the morale of their people and their forces. 

We see a rise in the number of defections from the Vietcong and the North Vietnamese troops to the South Vietnamese Government.

We see, in letters and prisoner reports and interrogations and other sources of intelligence, some reflection of declining morale. We see no change in the decision of the North Vietnamese Government to prosecute this war to a successful conclusion from their point of view. [Security deletion.]

Should we close the port of Haiphong? The first question is, Can we? The answer is "Yes." [Security deletion.]

Should we? The answer to that, I think, lies in balancing the pros and cons. [Security deletion.] General WHEELER. General Westmoreland is conducting offensive operations on a schedule which is laid out for about 3 or 4 months in advance, which has been agreed between his own headquarters and that of the Joint General Staff, the Vietnamese staff. These are combined operations usually; that is, they have U.S. forces, there are other forces and Vietnamese forces engaged together.

His objectives [security deletion]. 

We have captured, since the first of the year, something over [security deletion] a very important support element for them. General 

Westmoreland has been highly successful in this campaign to date. 

As a matter of fact, he has suffered no setbacks in his program. consider that he is on the right track, that in [security deletion]. 

Mr. HAYS. Nothing you have said has changed my mind. I think we ought to mine the port of Haiphong-period.

This is a strange war that is being fought in Vietnam. The U.S. 

Air Force is virtually denied the right to effectively attack harbors, power sites, and other installations in North Vietnam. You know and everyone else knows that the great sea and air power of this country is barely used in ratio to its potential. 

General McConnell in his speech in Detroit some 3 months ago said our only hope of stopping these supplies is to discourage the North Vietnamese from supporting the Vietcong by making such support too costly to them. This strategy, which is best described as "strategic persuasion," gives the President a highly flexible tool in inducing North Vietnam to accept his offer of unconditional discussions. It is true, General McConnell said, we could achieve this objective virtually overnight by destroying North Vietnam and force its surrender.

Why does this Government continue to keep American sea and air power throttled and fight a costly ground war? Is this your decision or that of President Johnson?

EXTRACTS OF STATEMENTS BY HON. ROBERT S. MCNAMARA ON THE OUTLOOK IN SOUTH VIETNAM (JANUARY 1, 1963, THROUGH JANUARY 1, 1966) 1. January 30, 1963, House Armed Services Committee 

"As I have said before on several occasions, victory over the Vietcong will most likely take many years." 

2. February 10, 1963, AP interview

Question. What is your assessment of the prospects in South Vietnam a year after this country started its big drive to help the pro-Western government stamp out the Communist guerrillas?

Answer. You may recall that while I have expressed satisfaction on several occasions during the past year with the progress of the operations in South Vietnam, I have on each occasion cautioned against the conclusion that the progress is leading toward a near-term victory. On each occasion I have indicated that a period of years would be required to wipe out the Communist aggression in that country. Progress has been made, however, during this past year. 

I can cite some of the factors that lead me to make that statement * * * I cite these as favorable factors indicating that we believe the South Vietnamese Government and we have made progress during this past year. But I don't wish to indicate to you that that progress has resulted or will result in the near future in defeat of the Vietcong. We have blunted the attack, but we haven't defeated it. 

3. February 19, 1963, Senate Armed Services Committee 

Senator STENNIS. Mr. Secretary, I have noticed a release of a statement by you in relation to Vietnam in which you said in substance it would be a long hard struggle. Could you specify a little more, you are not expecting any victory or any accomplishment there or termination of goals or anything like that for several years; is that right? That is what I inferred from your statement anyway.

Secretary MCNAMARA. I hope for a gradual strengthening of the control of the Government over the activities of that nation, and a gradual weakening of the influence of the Vietcong. I think this will go on, hope it will go on, for a substantial period in the future. I can't really put a number on the years involved, but I think it would be maybe 3 or 4 years. 

4. February 28, 1963, press conference

Question. Mr. Secretary, you were optimistic in your foreword to your budget message about the progress of things in South Vietnam. General Wheeler was also optimistic. Are you still optimistic or do you think it may take a little longer?

Secretary MCNAMARA. I don't believe I have ever indicated it would be other than a long, hard struggle extending over a period of years. I have felt that way in the past after each of my meetings with our commanders in South Vietnam and with the Ambassador in South Vietnam. I have so reported after each of the visits I have held with those individuals both in Honolulu and Saigon. I feel exactly the same way today. I am optimistic in the sense that progress is. discernible. The drive of the Vietcong supported by the North Vietnamese against the established Government in South Vietnam has been blunted. 

There are a number of factors that are favorable, a number of indications that the South Vietnamese are stronger in relation to the Vietcong than they were a year ago. In that sense there has been progress. For that reason I am optimistic. But I am not predicting the termination of the conflict. It will be a long, hard, dangerous conflict. ***

I want to repeat again that although there has been a favorable development in the ratio of Vietcong versus South Vietnamese casualties, a favorable development in the improvement in the ratio of arms captured by the South Vietnamese versus the Vietcong, a favorable development in the reduction in Vietcong incidents, nonetheless the Vietcong have been able to reinforce their forces, they have been able to replace all their losses, and they continue to function as a very dangerous arm of the North Vietnamese and Red Chinese Communist Parties. 

5. June 18, 1963, Senate Foreign Relations Committee 

Although the conflict (in Vietnam) will continue for some time to come, I believe we are making significant gains, particularly in the carrying through of the vital strategic hamlet program, in the gradual extension of Government control,

and in the key indexes of comparative defection and the growth in spontaneous intelligence reflecting the growing loyalty and confidence of the people of Vietnam in their Government. It may be of interest to note that in recent weeks we have talked to representatives of other allied and neutral nations who have been observing the picture closely; they share the judgment that we are making significant progress against the Vietcong. 

Senator AIKEN. I noticed that you, * * * were more optimistic over the situation in Vietnam, and feel that that is coming to a successful conclusion. 

Secretary MCNAMARA. Senator Aiken, I would not wish to obscure the very serious crisis in that country associated with the conflict between the Buddhist sect and the Government. I think this is a cause for great concern. But excluding that factor, progress against the Vietcong, which I specifically referred to in my statement, has been very satisfactory indeed. 

6. July 19, 1963, press conference

Question. Do the religious problems in Vietnam have an active effect on the military effort?

Secretary MCNAMARA. I think the effect of the religious controversy, or what started out, at least, as a religious controversy in Vietnam, has been limited to date on the military effort. The military operations in South Vietnam have been proceeding very satisfactorily and this is true no matter what method you apply to them. If you examine the casualty rates, the rate of incidents, the relative exchange of weapons, those gained versus those lost, the number of prisoners taken, the number of defectors, for example any one of these measures that you apply to current military operations compared with those of a year ago, or 2 years ago, indicate that they have been proceeding very, very satisfactorily indeed. 

We are quite pleased with the results. But I don't think they can help but be affected if the current controversies, religious and/or political, continue into the future for any extended period of time. *** 

7. September 23, 1963, planeside press interview, Andrews Air Force Base 

Question. Could you summarize the purpose of your visit (to South Vietnam)? 

Secretary MCNAMARA. Yes. Until very recently the progress of the military effort of the South Vietnamese, assisted by the United States, against the Vietcong was proceeding very satisfactorily. It is our purpose to determine whether that military effort has been adversely affected by the unrest of the past several weeks. For that purpose President Kennedy, after consultation with Ambassador Lodge, has asked General Taylor and me to visit Saigon and extensively review the military program there. 

8. September 25, 1963, CBS interview

Question. Might the war (in South Vietnam) be won on the battlefield and lost in Saigon?

Secretary MCNAMARA. The current period is a difficult one to appraise. Certainly, instability has been accentuated in the last several weeks by the actions of the Government. It is entirely possible that they have alienated important elements of the population, and unless the Government and the population can work together in a unified effort to defeat the Vietcong, they won't be defeated. 

9. October 2, 1963, White House statement 

1. The security of South Vietnam is a major interest of the United States as other free nations. We will adhere to our policy of working with the people and Government of South Vietnam to deny this country to communism and to suppress the externally stimulated and supported insurgency of the Vietcong as promptly as possible. Effective performance in this undertaking is the central objective of our policy in South Vietnam. 

2. The military program in South Vietnam has made progress and is sound in principle, though improvements are being energetically sought. 

3. Major U.S. assistance in support of this military effort is needed only until the insurgency has been suppressed or until the national security forces of the Government of South Vietnam are capable of suppressing it. Secretary Mc- 

Namara and General Taylor reported their judgment that the major part of the U.S. military task can be completed by the end of 1965, although there may be a continuing requirement for a limited number of U.S. training personnel. They reported that by the end of this year, the U.S. program for training Vietnamese should have progressed to the point where 1,000 U.S. military personnel assigned to South Vietnam can be withdrawn. 77

4. The political situation in South Vietnam remains deeply serious. The 

United States has made clear its continuing opposition to any repressive actions in South Vietnam. While such actions have not yet significantly affected the military effort, they could do so in the future. 

10. November 19 1963, planside interview, Hickam Air Force Base 

Question. We've heard several optimistic reports of General Harkins of the (SVN) war there and how our American boys are still dying over there, and we noticed today that there was another insurgency action. When can we expect that you are going to start ordering the American boys to start coming home from that war?

Secretary MCNAMARA. I expect that the return of certain U.S. personnel will be one of the subjects we will discuss at this meeting. It is our objective to provide the training and logistical assistance which the South Vietnamese Government has requested of us, and upon completion of certain facets of that training, small numbers of the U.S. personnel will be able to return by the end of this year.

Question. Do you consider that the change of regime in Vietnam makes possible a more decisive action now to end the war? 

Secretary MCNAMARA. I think it is quite clear that the previous regime had lost the support of large segments of the population, and a war of this type a war against guerrilla action, a war against subversion--can only be won with the full support of the people. I think this Government is bound to have, and is already receiving, more support than the previous one either had or deserved. 

11. November 21, 1963, planeside statement, Andrews AFB

The meeting that was held yesterday (in Hawaii) was attended by Secretary Rusk, General Taylor, and myself, and other representatives of this Government. It was a very fruitful, worthwhile discussion; we reviewed in great detail both economic and military programs of assistance to South Vietnam. We are very encouraged by the excellent working relationships which have developed between our representatives and the new Government in South Vietnam, and we are equally encouraged by the prospects for progress in the war against the Vietcong. 

12. December 21, 1963, remarks at the White House 

The members of my party and I returned this morning from South Vietnam. 

We have just completed our report to the President of our observations. We observed the results of the very substantial increase in the Vietcong activity, an increase that began shortly after the new Government was formed, and has extended over a period of several weeks. 

During this time, the Vietcong have attacked, and attacked successfully, a substantial number of the strategic hamlets. They have burned the houses, the fortifications, and in many cases have forced the inhabitants to leave. The rate of that Vietcong activity, however, has substantially dropped within the past week to 10 days.

This rapid expansion of activity, I think, could have been expected. It obviously was intended to take advantage of the period of organization in the new Government, a period during which there was a certain amount of confusion confusion that you might have expected would result from the replacement of the province chiefs and other key administrators in the Government. 

We reviewed in great detail the plans of the South Vietnamese and the plans of our own military advisers for operations during 1964 We have every reason to believe they will be successful. We are determined that they shall be. 

13. January 27, 1964, House Armed Services Committee 

The situation there continues grave. Last September we had hoped we could bring sufficient pressure to bear on the Diem government to persuade it to abandon its oppressive measures against the Vietnamese people and get on with the task of winning the war against the Vietcong. Although the military situation in the delta region was still very bad, good progress had been made in the northern areas and especially noteworthy work had been done in the key coastal provinces where Vietcong strength had once threatened to cut the country in half. In the central area and the highlands, progress had been steady, though slower. The situation was still difficult in the provinces to the west and north of Saigon itself. Throughout the northern two-thirds of the country, the strategic hamlet program had developed very well and freedom of movement in the rural areas had grown steadily. We concluded then that top priority should

be given to the delta region which contains approximately 40 percent of the population. This region has traditionally resisted central authority. It is the center of Vietcong strength, and the swampy nature of the terrain makes it the most diffcult area to pacify.

The first step in that direction had already been taken by September when a third division was moved to the delta. But we felt that additional measures were needed, particularly the consolidation, rather than the further spread, of strategic hamlets; the elimination of many fixed outposts; better hamlet defenses; and more trained hamlet militia. We also felt that the regular Republic of Vietnam Army units should be reserved for use in mobile actions and for "clear and hold" operations in support of the strategic hamlet program. 

With these further measures in view, we felt that a start could be made in reducing the number of U.S. military personnel in Vietnam as their training missions were completed. Accordingly, we announced that about 1,000 men were to be withdrawn by the end of 1963 and expressed the hope that the major part of the U.S. military task could be completed by the end of 1965, although we recognized that there might be a continuing requirement for a limited number of U.S. advisory personnel.

In this connection, we must recognize that the U.S. advisory effort cannot assure ultimate success. This is a Vietnamese war, and in the final analysis it must be fought and won by the Vietnamese. To leave our advisers there beyond the time they are truly needed would delay the development of Vietnam's initiative. Therefore it has been our policy to transfer U.S. responsibilities to the Vietnamese wherever this can be done without impairing the total war effort. 

Unfortunately, the Diem government did not choose to follow the advice we offered. In November that government was overthrown and replaced by a new government made up of military officers and civilians. The Vietcong was quick to take advantage of the growing opposition to the Diem government and the period of uncertainty following its overthrow. Vietcong activities were already increasing in September and continued to increase at an accelerated rate in October and November, particularly in the delta area. And I must report that they have made considerable progress since the coup. 

The new Government, however, has considerably more popular support than its predecessor and the Military Revolutionary Committee is beginning to take action to intensify military operations and to improve civil administration. The strategic hamlet program which had been overextended in the delta area is now being built more solidly. And the new Government is now applying "clear and hold" tactics in that area.

We hope that, with our full support, the new Government can take hold and eventually suppress the Vietcong insurrection. The dry season will give us a firmer basis for this judgment. However, the survival of an independent government in South Vietnam is so important to the security of all of southeast Asia and to the free world that I can conceive of no alternative other than to take all necessary measures within our capability to prevent a Communist victory. 

We must prove that Communist aggression cannot succeed through subversion, but will fail as surely as it has failed in direct confrontation. 

14. January 27, 1964, Brinkley on NBC

McNamara said the war in South Vietnam has improved very little, if any since the new Government took over, and he thought the United States must take all necessary measures to prevent a Communist victory. 

15. January 27, 1964, CBS-TV

Cronkite reported that McNamara's progress report on the war against communism in Vietnam was far from optimistic. He confirmed reports that the enemy had successfully taken advantage of the change in regimes there. 

Secretary MCNAMARA (in film clip). I think we should go back to the period immediately following the coup. As you might have expected, there was a period of instability during the reorganization of the Government. The Vietcong, the Communist insurgents, took advantage of that period of instability, increased the rate of their attacks and greatly increased the pressure they were putting on the South Vietnamese, particularly in the rice-rich delta area south of Saigon.

That period of increased Vietcong pressure endured both through November and December. Since that time, however, Government forces have been greatly strengthened, and we're seeing a reversal of that situation today. Much remains to be done. 60-929-66-pt. 2-5

16. January 28, 1964, Pentagon press conference

Question. Could you expand a bit on your statement about the war in Vietnam which you made to the House committee yesterday?

Secretary MCNAMARA. The situation in Vietnam is this: The coup took place on November 2, and at that time the military junta took control of the political administration of the country, and there was what you might have expected-a period of turmoil and instability, uncertainty, as the responsibility for the 

Government shifted.

The Vietcong took advantage of that, as you would have expected they would. 

They increased the intensity of their attacks, increased the number of their attacks severalfold, and particularly in the area south of Saigon, the delta area, major portions of which had been under the control of the Communists for more than 20 years, way back into the early 1940's. 

The Communists moved in, and never at any time since roughly 1940 have major areas of that delta been under control of anyone other than the Communists. In any event, the Vietcong, expanding from those Communists bases, those long-held Communists bases in the delta, raised the intensity of their attacks, and had many successes during the period of November and December as the new Government formed and consolidated its activities. 

The Government was made up of military leaders primarily, as you know, excluding the Prime Minister To, who was a civilian. But General Don, General Minh, General Dinh-all of the others that are members of that committee-in effect held dual roles. They retained their responsibility for direction of military operations while at the same time they assumed this additional responsibility for the administration of the political and economic institutions of that country, a country under severe attack from the Communists. 

You can imagine, therefore, that something suffered, and I think that what suffered was the military administration or the administration of military operations, as their attention was forced onto these political and economic problems. They took action, as I remember, toward the latter part of December or early in January, to divide their responsibilities, appointed additional military commanders, sorted out the responsibilities so that greater concentration on military problems was possible, and there has been a very noticeable improvement in the operations as a result.

The Vietcong attacks have decreased in intensity and number and the Government successes have increased. The situation in the delta remains grave, but I am encouraged by the progress of the last 2 weeks. 

17. February 3, 1964, joint Senate Armed Services-Appropriations Committees 

Question. Would you make a few comments about the new South Vietnamese government?

Secretary MCNAMARA. I can't speak with any real authority on it. I spent considerable time with General Khanh during my visits to Vietnam. He has impressed me as a very intelligent, articulate, courageous, aggressive, determined army commander. I felt that before he entered the Government last week and I feel it today. But I speak of him in my association with him as an army commander, not as a chief of state. I am not prepared to evaluate his qualities in that latter role, although I think that many of the characteristics that I have observed in him as an army commander will stand him in good stead in his role as chief of state. Secretary MCNAMARA. Last fall I was not as optimistic perhaps about the course of the war as I was about being able to bring back our personnel in certain numbers by the end of last year and also in increments. I still am hopeful of doing that. We did, of course, bring back a thousand men toward the latter part of last year. I am hopeful we can bring back additional numbers of men. 

I say this because I personally believe this is a war the Vietnamese must fight. 

It is a guerrilla war that must be fought by Vietnamese countering the local Vietcong guerrillas. I don't believe we can take on that combat task for them. 

I do believe we can carry out training. We can provide advice and logistical assistance. But after all, the training, by the very nature of the work, comes to an end at a certain point. We will have started this expanded training program and carried it out for a period of 4 years, by the end of next year. 

I don't believe we should leave our men there to substitute for Vietnamese men who are qualified to carry out the task. This is really the heart of our proposal. 

I think it was a sound proposal then and I think so now. I don't in any way wish to minimize the difficulties we face in Vietnam. I think they are great but I do
