FIREBALL REPORT 

Persons observing a fireball or meteor should report the information to the American Meteor Society. The information desired is contained below. 

A very brilliant meteor or fireball is reported to have passed in your vicinity on *** at the hour of 4 ** * Will you please answer as fully as possible the following questions, which are asked on behalf of the American Meteor Society in order that permanent records of such phenomena may be obtained. When these reports are published each contributor whose report is fairly complete will be mentioned, if possible, and due credit given. It is only by the help of those who can give personal information that data can be secured for the computation of the orbits of meteors. These data are of great scientific value and all reasonable efforts should be made to obtain them. You will be unable probably to answer all questions below, but answer those you can, as they may be of the greatest importance. (1) Give your name and address. (2) Where were you when you saw the meteor? (If the town is small please give county as well.) (3) Give the date, hour, and minute when the meteor appeared; also kind of time used. (4) In what direction did it appear (or in what direction was it first seen)? 

This is not asking in what direction it was going. (5) In what direction did it disappear (or in what direction was it last seen) ?

For questions 4 and 5, simply N, E, S, or W is not accurate enough, unless these were the exact directions. If compass is used, state it; also if magnetic correction has been applied to compass reading. (6) At what height did it appear? (Use degrees in answering.) (7) At what height did it disappear? (Use degrees in answering.) (8) Did it pass directly overhead (i.e., through the zenith)? (9) If not, to which side of the zenith did it go, and how far from it? (Use degrees in answering.) (10) Did it appear to reach the horizon? What sort of a horizon have you? (11) What angle did the path of the meteor make with the horizon and in which direction was it then going? (12) If you are familiar with constellations describe the path of the meteor through the sky with reference to stars. (13) Did the meteor appear to explode? (14) What was the duration of its flight in seconds? (15) Describe the train if one was left.

If it lasted long enough to show 

Give sketch, if possible, drift, most carefully tell in what direction train drifted. showing this with regard to horizon. (16) What was the duration of the train in seconds? (17) Did you hear any sound? How long after seeing the meteor was it before you heard this sound? Did you hear an actual explosion? How long after seeing the explosion was it before you heard it? (18) Of what color was the meteor? (19) What was the size of the meteor? (Compare it with the Moon or with a planet or star.) (20) Was more than one body seen before the explosion (if any)? (21) What was condition of sky at time? (22) Give names and addresses of others who saw the meteor. (23) Please mail this reply to: Charles P. Oliver, American Meteor Society, 521 North Wynnewood Avenue, Narberth, Pa.

SPECIAL REPORT OF THE USAF SCIENTIFIC ADVISORY BOARD AD HOC COMMITTEE TO REVIEW PROJECT "BLUE BOOK" MARCH 1966 Members participating: Dr. Brian O'Brien (Chairman), Dr. Launor F. Car ter, Mr. Jesse Orlansky, Dr. Richard Porter, Dr. Carl Sagan, and Dr. Willis H. Ware.

SAB Secretariat: Lt. Col. Harold A. Steiner. 

I. INTRODUCTION 

As requested in a memorandum from Maj. Gen. E. B. LeBailly, Secretary of the Air Force Office of Information, dated September 28, 1965 (tab A), an SAB 

Ad Hoc Committee met on February 3, 1966, to review Project "Blue Book."

The objectives of the Committee are to review the resources and methods of investigation prescribed by Project "Blue Book" and to advise the Air Force of any improvements that can be made in the program to enhance the Air Force's capability in carrying out its responsibility. 

In order to bring themselves up to date, the members of the Committee initially reviewed the findings of previous scientific panels charged with looking into the UFO problem. Particular attention was given to the report of the 

Robertson panel which was rendered in January 1953. The Committee next heard briefings from the AFSC Foreign Technology Division, which is the cognizant Air Force agency that collates information on UFO sightings and monitors investigations of individual cases. Finally, the Committee reviewed selected case histories of UFO sightings with particular emphasis on those that have not been identified. 

II. DISCUSSION 

Although about 6 percent (646) of all sightings (10,147) in the years 1947 through 1965 are listed by the Air Force as "Unidentified," it appears to the Committee that most of the cases so listed are simply those in which the information available does not provide an adequate basis for analysis. In this connection it is important also to note that no unidentified objects other than those of an astronomical nature have ever been observed during routine astronomical studies, in spite of the large number of observing hours which have been devoted to the sky. As examples of this the Palomar Observatory Sky 

Atlas contains some 5,000 plates made with large instruments with wide field of view; the Harvard Meteor project of 1954-58 provided some 3,300 hours of observation; the Smithsonian Visual Prairie Network provided 2,500 observing hours. Not a single unidentified object has been reported as appearing on any of these plates or been sighted visually in all these observations. 

The Committee concluded that in the 19 years since the first UFO was sighted there has been no evidence that unidentified flying objects are a threat to our national security. Having arrived at this conclusion the Committee then turned its attention to considering how the Air Force should handle the scientific aspects of the UFO problem. Unavoidably these are also related to Air Force public relations, a subject on which the Committee is not expert. Thus the recommendations which follow are made simply from the scientific point of view. 

III. CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 

It is the opinion of the Committee that the present Air Force program dealing with UFO sightings has been well organized, although the resources assigned to it (only one officer, a sergeant, and secretary) have been quite limited. In 19 years and more than 10,000 sightings recorded and classified, there appears to be no verified and fully satisfactory evidence of any case that is clearly outside the framework of presently known science and technology. Nevertheless, there is always the possibility that analysis of new sightings may provide some additions to scientific knowledge of value to the Air Force. Moreover, some of the case records which the Committee looked that were listed as "identified" were sightings where the evidence collected was too meager or too indefinite to permit positive listing in the identified category. Because of this the Committee recommends that the present program be strengthened to provide opportunity for scientific investigation of selected sightings in more detail and depth than has been possible to date.

To accomplish this it is recommended that: 

A. Contracts be negotiated with a few selected universities to provide scientific teams to investigate promptly and in depth certain selected sightings of UFO's. 

Each team should include at least one psychologist, preferably one interested in clinical psychology, and at least one physical scientist, preferably an astronomer or geophysicist familiar with atmospheric physics. The universities should be chosen to provide good geographical distribution, and should be within convenient distance of a base of the Air Force Systems Command (AFSC). 

B. At each AFSC base an officer skilled in investigation (but not necessarily with scientific training) should be designed to work with the corresponding university team for that geographical section. The local representative of the Air 

Force Office of Special Investigations (OSI) might be a logical choice for this.

C. One university or one not-of-profit organization should be selected to coordinate the work of the teams mentioned under A above, and also to make certain of very close communication and coordination with the office of Project 

Blue Book. 

It is thought that perhaps 100 sightings a year might be subjected to this close study, and that possibly an average of 10 man-days might be required per sighting so studied. The information provided by such a program might bring to light new facts of scientific value, and would almost certainly pro- Ivide a far better basis than we have today for decision on a long-term UFO program.

The scientific reports on these selected sightings, supplementing the present program of the Project Blue Book office, should strengthen the public position of the Air Force on UFO's. It is, therefore, recommended that: 

A. These reports be printed in full and be available on request. 

B. Suitable abstracts or condensed versions be printed and included in, or as supplements to, the published reports of Project Blue Book. 

C. The form of report (as typified by "Project Blue Book" dated February 1, 

1966) be expended, and anything which might suggest that information is being withheld (such as the wording on p. 5 of the above cited reference) be deleted. 

The form of this report can be of great importance in securing public understanding and should be given detailed study by an appropriate Air Force office. 

D. The reports "Project Blue Book" should be given wide unsolicited circulation among prominent Members of Congress and other public persons as a further aid to public understanding of the scientific approach being taken by the Air Force in attacking the UFO problem. 

DEPARTMENT OF THE AIR FORCE, OFFICE OF THE SECRETARY, 

Washington, September 28, 1965. 3. 

Memorandum for Military Director, Scientific Advisory Board. 

Subject: Unidentified flying objects (UFO's).

In keeping with its air defense role, the Air Force has the responsibility for the investigation of unidentified flying objects reported over the United States. The name of this project is Blue Book (attachment 1). Procedures for conducting this program are established by Air Force Regulation 200-2 (attachment 2). 

The Air Force has conducted Project Blue Book since 1948. As of June 30, 1965, a total of 9,267 reports had been investigated by the Air Force. Of these 

9,267 reports, 663 cannot be explained.

It has been determined by the Assistant Deputy Chief of Staff/Plans and Operations that Project Blue Book is a worthwhile program which deserves the support of all staff agencies and major commands, and that the Air Force should continue to investigate and analyze all UFO reports in order to assure that such objects do not present a threat to our national security. The Assistant Deputy Chief of 

Staff/Plans and Operations has determined also that the Foreign Technology Division (FTD) at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base should continue to exercise its presently assigned responsibilities concerning UFO's. 

To date, the Air Force has found no evidence that any of the UFO reports reflects a threat to our national security. However, many of the reports that cannot be explained have come from intelligent and technically well qualified individuals whose integrity cannot be doubted. In addition, the reports received officially by the Air Force include only a fraction of the spectacular reports which are publicized by many private UFO organizations. 

Accordingly, it is requested that a working scientific panel composed of both physical and social scientists be organized to review Projects Blue Book-its resources, methods, and findings and to advise the Air Force as to any improvements that should be made in the program in order to carry out the Air Force's assigned responsibility. 

Dr. J. Allen Hynek, who is the chairman of the Dearborn Observatory at 

Northwestern University, is the scientific consultant to Project Blue Book. He has indicated a willingness to work with such a panel in order to place this problem in its proper perspective. 

Dr. Hyneck has discussed this problem with Dr. Winston R. Markey, the former 

Air Force Chief Scientist. 

E. B. LEBAILLY, Major General, U.S. Air Force, Director of Information.

(The information follows:) IMPLEMENTATION OF SECTION 511(b) OF THE FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1961, as AMENDED 

Answers to the four questions posed by Mr.

Fraser are included in the "Report on Implementation of Section 511(b) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended" forwarded to the Speaker of the House of Representatives and to the chairman, Committee on Foreign Relations, U.S. Senate by the Assistant 

Secretary of Defense, International Security Affairs on March 30, 1966. The report, which is required by section 511(b), is quoted below: "Section 511(b) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, provides that the President shall submit semiannual reports to the Speaker of the House

Se

IN to וד of Representatives and to the Committee on Foreign Relations of the Senate on the implementation of the provisions of the above-mentioned section. This section directs that: "To the maximum extent feasible, military assistance shall be furnished to American Republics in accordance with joint plans (including joint plans relating to internal security problems) approved by the Organization of American States." This is the first report since enactment of the amendment on 

September 6, 1965, and covers the period from September 6, 1965, to December 

31, 1965.

REPORT 

"Progress toward fulfilling the intent of section 511(b) has been limited by the lack of an effective military planning body within the Organization of American States as presently constituted. There is a provision in the Charter of the 

Organization of American States (articles 44-47) for an Advisory Defense Com mittee. This committee is intended to be composed of the highest military authorities of the American States; however, the committee has never been convoked. There is no other military organ provided within the present Organiza tion of American States Charter. 

"There is a regional military organization, however, within the inter-American system which has representatives from all of the member nations of the Organization of American States, that is, the Inter-American Defense Board which was created in 1942 by the third meeting of the Ministers of Foreign Affairs of the American Republics. The Inter-American Defense Board is made up of military officers detailed from each of the member nations of the organization of American States for military planning for the Americas on a hemispheric basis. 

These officers represent their respective nations rather than the Organization of American States or any other international body. Although the Inter-American 

Defense Board does not have a formal status within the Organization of American States framework, it has addressed itself to the problem of hemisphere defense planning. U.S. military assistance provided pursuant to bilaterally developed military assistance plans is fully consistent with the general military plan of the Inter-American Defense Board. 

[Security deletion.] 

"There is also a subregional body in Central America which offers an opportunity for multilateral cooperation in military planning. It is the Central 

American Defense Council with membership from El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, and Costa Rica which was formally installed in June 1964 for the purpose of providing an organ of consultation in questions of regional defense and to monitor the collective security of member states. [Security deletion.] The military assistance program has provided materiel and training in support of the Central American Defense Council through its member states with particular emphasis on communications systems and mobility requirements.” 

Mr. FRASER. Next, and you may have covered this before I came in, I would like a statement that describes the change in the nature of your planning. I understand you are now on a 5-year program basis with respect to these countries. I would like to know what you have been doing, and what you are doing now, and a description of this to the extent that it is not already covered in the record. 

(The information follows:) THE MILITARY ASSISTANCE PLANNING PROCESS 

The new feature in the planning process is the reappraisal of selected countries referred to in my opening statement. This reappraisal consisted of a "hard look" at the nature of the threat for certain countries and an assessment of the increased ability of U.S. forces to meet the threat. Otherwise, the planning process is the same as in the past; that is, long-range military assistance plans prepared by the Unified Commanders and reviewed by the Ambassadors, the Joint Chiefs of Staff, the Office of the Secretary of Defense and the Department of State. The program now before the Congress represents the first year of fiscal year 1967-fiscal year 1971 Unified Commanders' plans, amended to reflect the results of the plan review and the reappraisal of selected country programs.

(The information follows:) JUSTIFICATION FOR MILITARY ASSISTANCE TO PAKISTAN 

Military assistance to Pakistan, in the form of sales of nonlethal military goods, is in the interest of the United States. Pakistan's size, geographic position, and membership in CENTO and SEATO enhance the value of improved and cordial relations with that nation. Pakistan is a sovereign power and can be expected to maintain a respectable level of military forces during the fore seeable future. [Security deletion.]

I would like to have some description of the area which the allied forces have controlled in South Vietnam over the last 6 months, what are the ways of measuring it, and what changes have been made, either in our favor or against. 

(The information follows:) AREA OF SOUTH VIETNAM COUNTRYSIDE CONTROLLED BY VIETCONG [Security deletion.] 

The Vietcong, on the other hand, have consistently claimed that they exercise effective control over four-fifths of the land area of South Vietnam.

The control of territory in South Vietnam has little meaning as a measure of the progress of the war. Safe and unsafe areas of operations do not remain static. Geographically South Vietnam is basically made up of densely populated lowland and delta areas and sparsely populated highland areas. The bulk of VC main force and organized guerrilla units are based and move around in sparsely populated areas, moving into more densely populated areas to attack and harass. 

These forces will move into areas where they consider they can operate without meeting overwhelming resistance from Government of the Republic of Vietnam forces. They will not move into areas where the odds are against success. 

In terms of densely populated rural and urban areas, few sections of the country offer the necessary favorable conditions for large-sized VC units. At a lower level of activity, VC guerrilla teams engaged in sabotage and terrorism can strike with varying degrees of risk, both in villages and hamlets in rural areas and in urban areas. 

On the Government of the Republic of Vietnam side a similar situation holds in reverse. Government of the Republic of Vietnam forces can enter virtually any area in the country if they allocate sufficient strength to do so. "Sufficient strength" may vary from nothing in urban areas to a large combat force in long-held VC base areas. To illustrate further the difficulty in defining land 

(a) A single VC sniper may shoot down an aircraft in a secure area or in an insecure area. In the former case a few policemen may be able to locate and seize him with little difficulty. In the latter case it may take a platoon or more of 

Government troops to fan out over an area to capture him. In a remote rural area his risks of capture are less than in an urban area. (b) The casualty rates for U.S. military and civilian advisory personnel living and working at the provincial and district level in rural areas are very low. 

These personnel can move around in their areas of operation with varying degrees of security precautions, in one area alone, in another area with two or three companions, and in a third area under an escort of an armed squad or more.

STATEMENT OF GEN. LYMAN L. LEMNITZER, SUPREME ALLIED COMMANDER, EUROPE, AND COMMANDER IN CHIEF, U.S. EURO- PEAN COMMAND 

General LEMNITZER. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, it is again a real pleasure for me to appear before this committee in support of the U.S. military assistance program. This is my 17th consecutive appearance before Congress on the question of military assistance. I take great pride in having had the opportunity to assist for so many years in the implementation of the program which, in my view, has contributed so much to the security of the free world. 

It was my privilege as the first director of military assistance to share in the original planning of the military assistance program and in the initiation of its operations. Since then, from a number of different vantage points, and covering a period of almost continuous association with the program since 1949, I have had the opportunity to supervise and observe its later contributions to the security of the United States and our allies-both in the Far East and, today, in Europe as Supreme Allied Commander, Europe, and Commander in Chief of the U.S. European Command. Among the many accomplishments of the program was the rebuilding of military strength in Europe in the early days of NATO, when the United States was the principal source of military hardware which was so badly needed in the European area. 349

While the military assistance program was designed to follow immediately behind the Marshall plan and to provide rebuilding of military strength in Europe, its scope has not, by any means, been limited to Europe. It was the military assistance program that was responsible for building great military strength in the Republic of Korea, southeast Asia, and many other parts of the world. Thus, it can be truly said that this program has been global in character. 

I continue to be a strong supporter of the military assistance program. It has demonstrated that it is vital to our national interests and security. It is an integral part of the U.S. defense effort. With regard to Europe in particular, it has helped to create deterrent forces among our allies-deterrent forces that have played a key part in maintaining peace and security and in preventing any Communist military "adventures" in that part of the world.

The military assistance program planned for fiscal year 1967 in the U.S. European Command is influenced by two major factors. The first of these is the continued improvement in the Soviet and Warsaw Pact military capabilities. The second is the continuing shift from grant assistance to military sales in those countries which have attained the economic capability of assuming their own defense burden. 

The military assistance planned in the area of the U.S. European 

Command reflects the concept of collective and mutual security which is the basis of unified action by nations of the free world. The basis for the level and type of our military effort in NATO Europe is the military threat presented by the Soviet Union and other Warsaw Pact nations. This threat includes impressive forces of all typesland, sea, and air; nuclear and nonnuclear.

Apart from the great military strength of the Soviet Union itself, it has been providing the forces of the other Warsaw Pact nations with increasing amounts of modernized military equipment. [Security deletion.]

Three Soviet fleets, based in the Black, the Baltic, and the Barents Seas, constitute the naval threat to NATO. [Security deletion.] 

This, briefly, is the Soviet bloc threat which faces my NATO Command, Allied Command Europe, along its 3,600-mile frontier, which ranges from the northern tip of Norway to the eastern frontier of Turkey. The defense effort within this area causes the requirements of the European Command military assistance program to vary over a wide range from major grant aid programs in Greece and Turkey on the critical southeast flank of NATO to major sales programs in the United Kingdom and the Federal Republic of Germany. 

I would first like to discuss with you our major grant aid programs in Greece and Turkey. Next, I will discuss the programs [security deletion].

Finally, I will discuss the phasing out of our programs in Norway and Denmark and review the roles of our military assistance advisory groups in the countries no longer receiving assistance. 

With regard to Greece and Turkey, military assistance was initiated under the Truman doctrine, before the original foreign military assistance program was approved by the Congress. In Greece and 

Turkey, the program provides needed military aid to these two countries, which are so strategically important on the right flank of Allied Command Europe. I have testified before this committee previously

regarding [security deletion]. These two countries have important missions and roles in protecting the vital southeastern flank of Allied 

Command Europe [security deletion]. 

Greece and Turkey do not have the capability for financing their total defense requirements, and are dependent upon outside assistance to maintain and modernize their forces [security deletion]. [Security deletion] of the funds proposed for countries in the EUCOM area are earmarked for Greece and Turkey. The fiscal year 

1967 program for Greece totals [security deletion] and will provide for force maintenance, training, and [security deletion] force mod ernization. [Security deletion.] 

The proposed program for Turkey of [security deletion] also will provide [security deletion] force modernization. Key items here are: 

[Security deletion] F-5 aircraft, [security deletion] helicopters, [se curity deletion] minesweepers, and [security deletion] equipment to increase combat mobility, firepower, and communications. 

It is apparent to me that [security deletion] U.S. support must continue in order that the armed forces of Greece and Turkey will be capable of performing their assigned missions. 

Turning to Libya, Portugal, and Spain [security deletion]. These bases are of great strategic importance and are vital to the accomplishment of the mission of the U.S. forces in Europe. [Security deletion.] 

Wheelus Air Base in Tripoli is necessary for operational training, and thus to the continued readiness of the U.S. Air Forces, Europe. 

No comparable area exists in Europe that provides the uncluttered airspace, the land for bombing and gunnery ranges and the favorable weather which so facilitate the high level of training essential to high standards of performance.

The basic objective of the [security deletion] fiscal year 1967 program for Libya is [security deletion] to contribute to the development and improvement of the Libyan armed forces in their internal security role [security deletion]. 

The fiscal year 1967 program proposed for Portugal is [security deletion] and provides primarily training and miscellaneous maintenance support. The original base rights agreement authorized wartime use of the Azores during the life of NATO. U.S. peacetime use of these bases expired in 1962. However, Portugal has permitted our continued use of the bases in the Azores on a provisional basis. [Security deletion.]

The fiscal year 1967 program for Spain [security deletion]. 

The proposed fiscal year 1967 program consists primarily of components and equipment for Navy construction, support of the air defense [security deletion] and training.

Two other countries on the Mediterranean coast of North Africa are in the area of the European Command and have grant aid programs. These are Morocco and Tunisia. For the present, these programs consist primarily of training. The training, of course, is directed [security deletion] toward improving in-country capability to employ equipment provided for internal security under prior year programs. [Security deletion.] 

The remaining country programs in the European Command are for Denmark and Norway. These programs provide only for meeting and carrying out prior U.S. commitments. [Security deletion.]

A most important phase of military assistance in the U.S. European 

Command consists of the multinational undertakings-infrastructure and the operating costs of international military headquarters and agencies. Infrastructure [security deletion] has contributed enormously to the strong military posture which NATO has today. We and the other NATO countries are contributing to our common defense by sharing costs in the construction of essential military facilities. 

This is a common effort, which is enabling us to integrate and strengthen our defenses in Europe. I cannot overemphasize the importance of its benefits both to the United States and to NATO. 

[Security deletion.] 

I would like to make two additional points before I conclude. 

The first is the importance of military assistance training. Aircraft, ships, and tanks are essential to an adequate military force. However, the man who must use and maintain the equipment must not be overlooked. Use and maintenance of modern weapons require highly trained personnel. Unless these personnel are available, the weapons are useless.

I believe that the most enduring value of the military assistance dollar is achieved through the training of allied personnel which we conduct in the United States, in the European Command, and in the host countries themselves.

Along with the contributions which training affords to the common defense in terms of operational capabilities, there are additional and attendant benefits. Some of these are not readily apparent but nevertheless are important. Beyond the purely military considerations, the formal training courses or orientation visits to U.S. training establishments, wherever they are physically located, contribute to an understanding by Allied military personnel of American principles, society, institutions, and ideals. The selection process utilized by the host countries to pick officers to attend our senior service colleges generally results in the choice of individuals who will ultimately fill positions of great responsibility and influence in their own countries. 

A working knowledge of U.S. doctrine and tactics that these officers acquire from attendance at our service colleges is but one of the many valuable advantages that accrue to the United States. The design of military equipment is directly influenced by the doctrine and tactics under which it will be used [security deletion]. The understanding of U.S. concepts and methods may also facilitate the introduction into allied countries of improved management techniques, with very far-reaching effects. 

I am convinced that the MAP training greatly contributes to the strength of our collective defense effort and to long-term mutual understanding between the United States and the other countries. 

The final point I desire to make is the importance I place upon our military assistance advisory groups, or MAAG's. The personnel in our MAAG's work jointly with their counterparts in the host countries on a daily basis. [Security deletion.] 

The initial purpose of MAAG's was, of course, to administer grant aid military assistance programs in the host countries. In some countries, this remains their purpose. However, grant aid programs have been phased out in those European nations which have achieved economic recovery. Large quantities of grant aid equipment remain in

the active inventories of these latter countries, and there remains a statutory requirement to maintain surveillance over this equipment for proper utilization and upkeep. The MAAG's are the agencies best suited and capable of performing this function. This active supervision permits early identification of equipment which may become excess to the host country's need, and subsequently can be redistributed to meet grant aid requirements in other countries. 

The military assistance program now includes aspects which have been added since the initial establishment of MAAG's. Among these are activities in connection with multilateral production programs, cooperative research and development programs, cooperative logistics arrangements, and military assistance sales. All of these require increased involvement on the part of the MAAG's. 

The professional understanding, trust, and confidence between MAAG personnel and their counterparts in host countries are important assets which are being applied to these additional activities. 

These are the established assets which assist us in achieving U.S. 

Sales in the EUCOM area during the period fiscal year 1962 through 1965 have totaled $5 billion. The annual averages over the next 3-year period are expected to exceed substantially the 1962-65 annual average. 

Close supervision of the MAAG strengths is maintained through a European command effort called the resources management program. This program provides periodic review of personnel strengths vis-a-vis MAAG missions to insure that the minimum level, consistent with essential operations, is maintained.

Overall, for value returned, MAAG operating costs are not great, nor do they particularly aggravate the balance-of-payments deficit in Europe. Of the [security deletion] annual cost of operating seven 

Western European MAAG's, approximately [security deletion] is contributed by the host countries. The multiple benefits that we receive through the maintenance of our military presence in these countries is well worth the [security deletion] it costs us. I am concerned as I was last year, about the trend to downgrade the importance of the MAAG's. A continuing in-country U.S. military presence is of incalculable advantage to the United States. It is my view that continued U.S. leadership in Europe [security deletion] are necessary for maintaining peace in important areas of the world that are vital to U.S. security interests. So long as the MAAG's continue to serve so usefully in furthering U.S. objectives, I consider that it would be highly imprudent to reduce their capability substantially. 

In concluding, Mr. Chairman, I remain convinced, as I have been in my previous 16 consecutive appearances before the Congress on this question, that the U.S. military assistance program continues to serve the security of our country well, and that it continues to support the foreign policy and general welfare of the United States and to carry out the intent of the Congress to promote the peace. The European area provides an example of the effectiveness of the application of self-help programs and mutual application of the principles of collective security by the voluntary actions of free world nations. The program under consideration will continue to enhance the military posture of our allies and, in turn, promote our mutual security. This, 60-929-66-pt. 2- -9

I believe, provides the surest way to the development and maintenance of the collective strength to deter aggression in Europe. 

Accordingly, I recommend and urge your support of this program. 

Mr. Chairman, that completes my prepared statement. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, General Lemnitzer.

General, many members of this committee are worried about the amount of money programed this year for NATO infrastructure. On page 184 of the book, the largest category of infrastructure is for 

NATO air defense ground environment. What does this mean?

General LEMNITZER. NATO air defense ground environment is usually discussed in the NATO arena under the name of NADGE. It provides those radars and control equipment which must be placed in strategically located areas on the ground, to provide the effective control of our air defenses.

We are in a very important phase of the program in Europe at the present time. High-performance modern aircraft are being introduced that require this ground control. 

It has been under discussion for a number of years and we finally have an agreed program. We are now proceeding to lay out the entire program. There are consortia who are bidding on the program, and we hope that in the reasonably near future these contracts will be awarded and the equipment provided and installed. 

Chairman MORGAN. How much of this money will be spent to build installations in France?

General LEMNITZER. I have it here, Mr. Chairman. The propor tion of the present slice of infrastructure that would be built in France 

Chairman MORGAN. Will this air defense ground environment have to be redesigned or reorganized as a result of President de Gaulle's requirement that the U.S. forces be taken out of France? 

General LEMNITZER. That will depend upon the final arrangements that are worked out between the alliance and France. [Security deletion.]

This is one of the many questions that has been raised by the position recently taken by France with regard to withdrawal of forces from the military activities of the alliance. 

Chairman MORGAN. General, how much of the more than $300 million already obligated for air defense ground environment has been spent and committed for projects that will be useless if De Gaulle goes through with his withdrawal of the program?

General LEMNITZER. I don't have the exact figures. [Security deletion.]

Chairman MORGAN. Again, General, this is of concern to many members of this committee. On the bottom of page 184 of the book it shows there is $128,509,000 of infrastructure funds already obligated, but not yet expended. Is any of this money going to be expended in France?

General LEMNITZER. This also remains to be determined. [Security deletion.]

The NATO Headquarters in Paris has a very large and important committee known as the Infrastructure Committee. [Security de letion.]

Chairman MORGAN. General, you can answer this question off the record if you wish. In view of your close association with many of the NATO officers-I am talking about military NATO officers, including the French-will you tell us anything you can about the opinion among the NATO military staff about President de Gaulle's move in trying to withdraw France from NATO?

 Chairman MORGAN. Off the record. 

(Discussion off the record.)

Chairman MORGAN. General, when the Secretary of Defense was here yesterday, one member of the committee asked him a question I about the possible relocation of SHAPE Headquarters, and he said it was under consideration. Are you, as the commander, and other 

NATO countries, looking at possible headquarters sites in case you are going to move out of your present place?

General LEMNITZER. We have done a great amount of planning on this. Our planning is limited, however, because it would be quite inappropriate for me to send reconnaissance parties into various countries looking for headquarters sites without an invitation from the countries in which we might locate. This is not to say we have not done a great deal of thinking on the possible areas. 

[Security deletion.] 

Chairman MORGAN. General, the press has carried stories about two possible sites. They mentioned one in Luxembourg and one in Belgium. Do you feel the best possible site would be the Benelux countries?

General LEMNITZER. There are other considerations involved which would affect the mere geographic problem. For example, I would like to know where the North Atlantic Council is going to go, because it is most important that SHAPE be located in close proximity to the North Atlantic Council, or NAC, as we refer to it. There are many transactions which we conduct on a day-by-day basis, a large number indeed, where members of my staff at SHAPE are in daily conferences with the members of the International Staff under the 

Secretary General. 

Then even beyond that [security deletion]. All of these indicate a preference for being relatively close together. You can't attain this if the Council, for instance, should remain in Paris and SHAPE should move elsewhere.

These are the questions I am asking the political authorities and the answers will go a long way toward clearing up the various alternatives that I have available to me. [Security deletion].

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, General. 

Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

It is good to see you again.

General LEMNITZER. Thank you, Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON.

You say that the military assistance program is to assist in maintaining U.S. influence. What influence? What areas of influence are not met by the embassies, USIA, the AID, and economic programs that you have been studying?

General LEMNITZER. It is primarily in the military field. In many of the countries we are dealing with the military occupy a very important position in their respective governments. They perform a

key role in maintaining internal and external security, and in defense matters generally. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, I thought that ought to be in the record.

How many countries and what are they, that Soviet Russia assists as well as ourselves?

General LEMNITZER. The Soviet Union does have a military assistance program. I can't give you the exact number of countries to which they furnish military assistance. I think you will find Soviet equipment in the hands of every country behind the Iron Curtain. Soviet equipment is found not only behind the Iron Curtain, but some of it is also in countries in Africa and in other underdeveloped areas. 

They have a sizable program. Egypt, for example, has a large amount of Soviet military equipment. 

Mrs. BOLTON. In any of those countries, do we give military assistance, also?

General LEMNITZER. Not to my knowledge. I am sure we don't give any aid, military aid to the Warsaw Pact countries. Those are the ones with which I am principally concerned. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Do you think our giving food to Egypt makes it easier for Russia not to do that and for them to do the purely military? 

General LEMNITZER. I don't think I am in a very good position to judge that problem. I think it is primarily a political problem. The people who are concerned with the overall military, fiscal, and political considerations are in the best position to judge that one. 

Mrs. BOLTON. What can you tell us of why Saudi Arabia, which does have a lot of money, doesn't pay for the help it gets? 

General LEMNITZER. Saudi Arabia happens not to be in my area, and I am not really acquainted with the situation there. I think 

General Adams will be coming. Saudi Arabia has recently been removed from my area of responsibility and transferred to the area for which General Adams has responsibility for U.S. military assistance. 

Mrs. BOLTON. We can ask him next week.

Why have the military programs for Guinea, why do we have that and don't have them for the Ivory Coast or Nigeria? 

General LEMNITZER. This is out of my area. I couldn't tell you. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Jordan is the same thing? 

General LEMNITZER. Yes. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Let's go to the Azores. We were very much involved in our sentiments back when the Azores were first made possible for us. Do you think we are going to have success in continuing there? 

Do you think Portugal will agree to it?

General LEMNITZER. As I indicated in my statement, we are there on an ad hoc basis day by day. We give Portugal a small amount of training assistance. But our base there at Lojes is functioning. It is particularly important to me, although it happens to be in Admiral Moorer's SACLANT area. It is the stepping stone for reinforcements and connections between the western part of the NATO alliance and Allied Command Europe. The facilities in the Azores would be heavily used as stepping stones in reinforcing my area. 

They are of tremendous importance, also, in maintaining antisubmarine actions within the Atlantic. That is not a responsibility of my command but of SACLANT's. The Azores are in a very important strategic position.

2 ta 18 

Mrs. BOLTON. Do we have any countermachinery, shall I call it, broadly, to protect ourselves and the world from the submarines? 

There seem to be a great many submarines floating around about the ocean.

General LEMNITZER. I would say the United States has as effective antisubmarine capabilities as any nation in the world-greater than any nation in the world.

Mrs. BOLTON. I will try to get that from the Navy perhaps or something because it has not been my sense of any adequate number of submarines as compared to the Russians, and so on. 

 It is one of our most important requirements, particularly in view of the vast number of submarines which the Soviets have available to them. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Do we have anything?

General LEMNITZER. Indeed, we do. [Security deletion.] 

Mrs. BOLTON. You say they have been designed?

General LEMNITZER. They are in-being. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Are they being used?

General LEMNITZER. They can only be used for detection in peacetime, of course. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Are they possible of use?

General LEMNITZER. Indeed they are. 

Mrs. BOLTON. In adequate numbers?

General LEMNITZER. This is an area that I am not as familiar with as others who are scheduled to appear before the committee. [Security deletion]. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you for that very much, because I have been rather unhappy that we have made no mention of that in any of the hearings that we have. I am glad to have you reassure me.

General LEMNITZER. [Security deletion.] 

Mrs. BOLTON. I hope so, General. I hope the research money has begun to be made visible not just be research. 

General LEMNITZER. Definitely not that. We do have ships and aircraft and the weapons to deal with submarines. The submarine threat is a very difficult one to deal with. I would say we have made enormous advances in the field in recent years. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you very much for the reassurance.

General LEMNITZER. You mean the U.S. equipment?

Mrs. KELLY. What are the prospects from a military standpoint of reducing our military forces in Germany?

General LEMNITZER. The decision, of course, is up to the United 

States [security deletion]. 

This question is frequently asked me, and I would like to explain my position on it. We are having contacts with Communist forces around the world including the contact in southeast Asia of which we are all so well aware of [security deletion]. 

We all know that there is a centralized control to Communist efforts and they could easily shift their activities into the European area. 

I believe that the peace, security, and stability which we have been able to obtain in Europe as a result of NATO's strength enable us to deal more effectively with decisions in other parts of the world than we would have otherwise, having to deal with several fronts. 

[Security deletion]. 

Mrs. KELLY. I had hoped that you would have that answer, General. 

What is the condition of the European armed forces? Are they well equipped or to what degree.

I know you and I and the military generally feel that an army is never fully and currently equipped with the most up-to-date equipment. What percentage of the European armed forces is equipped with new, modern, up-to-date weapons?

General LEMNITZER. It varies by country. It even varies among the armed forces of the country.

I will take the Federal Republic of Germany, for example. They have recently completed the activation of [security deletion]. 

Those that were activated a few years ago are among the best divisions that would come under my command in the case of an emergency 

[security deletion]. 

Mrs. KELLY. General, could we say that roughly speaking-the 

European NATO countries have attained [security deletion] or [security deletion] percent of maximum capacity for self-defense?

Could we roughly estimate a percentage there with respect to selfdefense?

General LEMNITZER. I wouldn't want to put it in terms like that. 

The purpose of the NATO alliance was to take the military capabilities of the various nations and to weld them into a viable whole. Their capabilities vary by country, but the objective is to get the best overall capability out of them.

In Central Europe at the moment we have approximately [security deletion]. I would say we have considerable strength there. In the 

Norway and Denmark area [security deletion] generally speaking, we believe we have a strong capability throughout the Allied Command Europe.

Mrs. KELLY. I have one more question and I will save the rest for this afternoon.

While you commended highly the work of the MAAG groups throughout Europe, with the present situation are they really needed there?

General LEMNITZER. As I tried to point out in my statement, I consider that they are badly needed, for advice, and as a link which strengthens the efforts of the alliance as a whole. They provide valuable guidance and assistance to the armed services of the countries in which they are located. 

They provide a means of assisting countries in identifying their military needs, and in selecting the best equipment for their needs. 

In general such equipment is that obtained from the United States. 

So the MAAG's do provide valuable advice with regard to the availability of modern military equipment in the United States. 

The United States is regarded also as the center of the most up-todate military learning and education.

I consider the military advisory groups in these countries very important, and that is why I devoted so much attention to it in my prepared statement.

Mrs. KELLY. I realize that. But when there is so little military assistance going to these countries, I wondered why we continue to support sizable MAAG groups? The reason for their being in those countries in the first instance has disappeared. 

I also want to comment on the sale of military equipment to the countries involved. I know that they can come directly to private brokers and industries instead of coming to our Government. I wondered if that had anything to do with the integration of the forces in Europe, politically.

General LEMNITZER. The MAAG's are very welcome in their respective areas. They do provide most valuable advice and guidance. 

They contribute greatly toward the integration of individual country efforts with the entire NATO effort. I could run down a long list of the advantages in having MAAG personnel there who can provide that kind of advice.

We constantly review on the size of the MAAG's. They are not nearly as large as they used to be. They are frequently surveyed to insure that they have the personnel required to do their job, and no more.

General LEMNITZER. Thank you, sir. 

Mr. O'HARA. General, I am a little concerned, reading your statement, the Soviet buildup apparently has been large, and you seem to feel that in the modernization of weapons we are not keeping pace? 

General LEMNITZER. I don't think I said that.

Mr. O'HARA. You didn't say that in so many words. I read between the lines. Did I read accurately or inaccurately? 

General LEMNITZER. I didn't hear the last part of your question. 

Mr. O'HARA. Did I interpret accurately or inaccurately? 

General LEMNITZER. I am sorry to say inaccurately. I don't believe that anything in my statement implies that, Mr. O'Hara. It was not put in that way.

With regard to equipment, techniques, tactics, things of that character, insofar as modernization of the U.S. forces is concerned, we are more than keeping pace. [Security deletion] but generally I would say that the United States forces in Europe are among the best equipped and the most capable in the world. 

I have a variety of forces in my comand.

Mr. O'HARA. You feel, General, with the program as recommended for fiscal 1967, we are taking adequate steps to meet the [security deletion] needs of the situation? 

You feel this program is adequate?

Mr. O'HARA. General, many of us feel that the situation as regards 

Russia is getting better. Is there a possibility that we are deceiving ourselves? Is there a possibility, however remote, of a surprise attack?

General LEMNITZER. Well, here we get into the questions, Mr. 

O'Hara, of dealing with Russian intentions, vis-a-vis their capabilities.

They maintain an enormous military capability west of the Urals. 

Those are generally the forces that would confront my forces in NATO. I see nothing that the Russians are doing or intend to do that should cause us to relax our efforts. They are improving their military capabilities despite their words to the contrary. I have been before this committee almost on a year-by-year basis since the NATO treaty was signed. The Soviets have improved their capabilities continuously over the years. In addition they are now one of the two great nuclear powers of the world. [Security deletion.] 

Furthermore, they continue to improve the forces of their satellites.

I wouldn't want my remarks to be interpreted that they intend tomorrow to attack NATO Europe. But I do maintain that they are improving their massive capability to do so. The reason that NATO was established was to provide a unified effort among the nations of Europe to convince the Russians that there are no promising aggressions they can carry on in the European area. That is the purpose of our efforts there. It is the purpose of maintaining our NATO military strength.

Throughout the years that this alliance has been established, it has succeeded in deterring any adventures on the part of the Soviet Union or its satellites in the NATO area.

They do have enormous military capability today, and in spite of their statements to the contrary, they continue to maintain and improve this capability. It is far beyond the level needed for their own security. NATO is a defensive alliance and its forces are primarily defensive [security deletion].

We do not base our military preparations on intentions. We can only plan properly on their military capabilities. I see no evidence that they are decreasing that capability within the European area at all. In fact, they are improving it. 

Mr. O'HARA. General, that is as I read your remarks. You also pointed out they have the ability to strike quickly and effectively. I thank you for clarifying that which I thought I read in your remarks, stressing the importance of awareness. 

Thank you.

General LEMNITZER. We are doing just the reverse.
