Washington, D.C. 

The committee met in executive session at 10:25 a.m., in room 2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

The committee meets in executive session for a continuation of hearings on the foreign aid legislation for fiscal year 1967, H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450. 

Our witnesses this morning are the Honorable Lincoln Gordon, Assistant Secretary of State for Inter-American Affairs, and U.S. 

Coordinator, Alliance for Progress, and Mr. David Bronheim, Deputy U.S. Coordinator, Alliance for Progress, Agency for International 

Development. 

Mr. Secretary, you have a prepared statement and you may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. LINCOLN GORDON, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR INTER-AMERICAN AFFAIRS, AND U.S. COORDINA- TOR, ALLIANCE FOR PROGRESS Mr. GORDON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I have a prepared statement, which has been circulated to members of the committee. If you would like I will be just as happy to file that for the record and perhaps to comment orally on the highlights of it and to supplement it with a few additional observations. Would that be satisfactory? 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, Mr. Secretary, your statement will be inserted in the record and you may proceed, sir. 

(The statement of Mr. Gordon is as follows:) STATEMENT OF HON. LINCOLN GORDON, ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF STATE FOR INTER- AMERICAN AFFAIRS, AND U.S. COORDINATOR, ALLIANCE FOR PROGRESS 

Mr. Chairman, I thank you, sir, and the members of your committee for giving me this opportunity to come before you to speak on behalf of the President's proposal for AID assistance to the Alliance for Progress. I would like to summarize for you this morning the Latin American situation as we see it and the proposals for aid to Latin America in fiscal year 1967. I am accompanied this morning by Mr. David Bronheim, Deputy U.S. Coordinator for the Alliance for 

Progress, and will ask him to join with me in responding to your questions.

As you know, I have been in my present position only for about a month. 

It has been a little more than a year since my predecessor last had an opportunity to go over with you the full political, economic, and social situation in Latin 

America. However, the committee has made several reviews of specific Latin 371

American developments, the most recent being in February when the proposed supplemental appropriation for the Dominican Republic was discussed. In addition to these reviews, many of you have made a particular effort to keep up with the events in Latin America by visiting the countries and investigating for yourselves. I felt it was particularly beneficial to the U.S. delegations to have Congressmen Selden and Mailliard at the Rio Conference last November and Congressmen Cameron and Whalley at Buenos Aires just last week. The recent firsthand observations by at least seven other members of the committee have been most useful to me and to my colleagues.

POLITICAL SITUATION 

On the political front, the hemisphere shows a decided trend toward greater stability and strengthening of democratic institutions as well as economic growth. There are some fragile situations, to be sure. We know of the swiftness with which adverse circumstances can develop. But the general trend is encouraging.

There is a healthy trend in Latin America, encouraged by the philosophy of the Alliance for Progress toward making economic and social progress the central concern of political movements and of the new generation of political leaders. Development, stabilization, and reform are the key issues of public life. There is also increasing awareness of the necessity to build democratic institutions at the grassroots level and to create the opportunity for democracy to develop through expanded economic opportunity and increased social justice. More countries are adopting development programs built upon the determination of people to help themselves. As they work together and build schools, cooperatives, water systems, and meeting centers, people are learning the rudiments of self-government by confronting the economic and social problems of their own communities. Time and energy also is being devoted to the development of the cooperative movement and to the strengthening of labor unions. There is an increasing appreciation of the necessity for full citizen involvement in national development efforts. 

In Chile, Peru, Mexico, Bolivia, Venezuela, El Salvador, Nicaragua, Guatemala, and the Dominican Republic there are community action programs designed to harness the energies of the people to improve their own lives. 

Reviewing the political events of the past year it is clear that there is a growing determination in the hemisphere to develop and improve democratic procedures and political stability. [Security deletion.] Reform-minded constitutional governments are demonstrating that political freedom and economic development can go hand in hand. And in the few cases of de facto governments, most of them are moving their countries toward elections and a return to constitutionality. 

[Security deletion.] 

Much progress has also been made in combating Communist subversion. 

Venezuela, Peru, Colombia, and Central America have made progress in reducing their guerrilla problems. Elsewhere in the hemisphere, the Communists show little, if any, gain from their efforts. This is due to several factors. 

Public and official awareness of the danger has increased as Communist designs become more evident. The recently concluded Havana Tri-Continent Conference has done more to sharpen this awareness than any other single event. 

Castro's repeated failures at home and his clumsiness in external relations have lost him most of the sympathy and appeal that he once enjoyed. Conflicts within the Communist movement have weakened its effectiveness. The internal security capabilities of the Latin American countries also have steadily increased, supported by our military and police assistance programs. Continued vigilance on this front, however, is essential.

We are encouraged by the success which Provisional President Hector Garcia Godoy is having in establishing a climate in the Dominican Republic propitious for elections scheduled June 1. Over the past month violence has been sharply reduced. Economic activity is picking up. Political parties are preparing for their nominating conventions. Candidates are already campaigning. In the task of preparing for elections, Garcia Godoy is receiving the full cooperation of all elements of the OAS in the Dominican Republic: the ad hoc committee, Inter- 

American Peace Force, the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights, and the Technical Electoral Advisory Committee. The OAS is firmly committed to free and fair elections in the Dominican Republic and the peaceful transfer of

power to the newly elected government on July 1. We fully support this OAS commitment. 

[Security deletion.] 

In Brazil, President Castello Branco is holding firmly to his objective of an early return to constitutional government and to his program of stabilization, development and reform. His successor is to be elected by the present Congress in early October, and the new Congress will be popularly elected in November. 

In October also there will be indirect elections for 11 State Governors (the other 11 were directly elected last October) and in November direct elections for the 

22 State legislatures. [Security deletion.] 

In Argentina, President Illia is taking significant steps to revitalize the economy. [Security deletion.] 

Peru, under President Belaunde, is moving forward with programs pointing toward greater political and economic integration of its disparate regions. For the first time in 400 years, a real effort is being made to bring the Andean Indians into participation in the national life. [Security deletion.] 

President Leoui continues to build on the solid foundations of reform and development left by Romulo Betancourt. [Security deletion.] 

In the Carribean, there are two recently independent nations, and several islands and territories approaching self-government, many of which have had long and close relations with the United States. Jamaica and Trinidad and 

Tobago have confirmed the confidence placed in them to follow the democratic road in dealing with the many problems of nationhood. British Guiana is to become an independent Guayana next month. [Security deletion.] Barbados is also now approaching independence. [Security deletion.] 

Mexico is probably the most stable country in the hemisphere, and is making rapid economic progress. The Central American Common Market is bringing new hope and progress to its five member countries. [Security deletion.] 

In Paraguay, there has been unprecedented economic progress. [Security deletion.]

PROGRESS UNDER ALLIANCE 

This last year has been a year of hope and achievement under the Alliance for Progress. In addition to material advances of real significance, there has been continued progress in the creation of a sound institutional base for more rapid economic development. The basic concepts of the Alliance have become the dominant philosophy in the hemisphere. Self-help, systematic mobilization of domestic resources, rational priorities for investment programs, creation of modern institutions as a base for more productive private enterprise, and reforms looking toward greater efficiency and broader social justice these are not only the commitments of governments but in increasing measure the actual practice. 

A little less than a month ago, on March 13, we celebrated the fifth anniversary of President Kennedy's announcement of the Alliance for Progress. On that occasion, President Johnson said: "Since becoming President, I have often restated my own, and our country's resolute commitment to the goal of a better life for all the people of the Western Hemisphere. "Today I want to issue a new call to our sister nations in the hemisphere to enlarge our truly revolutionary cause the cause of enlarging the lives of all our people. "I am determined to contribute America's resources to this spirit of change a spirit now slowly, surely, confidently growing in the Western Hemisphere." 

I have just returned from the Fourth Annual Meeting of the Inter-American Economic and Social Council in Buenos Aires. I spoke there with colleagues from throughout the hemisphere. There was agreement that, while progress has been encouraging, especially in the last 2 years, there is no cause for complacency. 

Action on land reform and tax reform, we agreed, has not proceeded quickly enough; unemployment and underemployment are desperate problems, particularly among the people flocking into the urban centers in search of better lives; ways must be found to strengthen the markets for Latin American exports of raw materials and to expand and diversify their exports generally; production of food and of manufactured goods must be increased; the population increase may nullify too much of what is being accomplished in economic development; and more rapid progress is needed in the integration movement both in the Central American Common Market and in the Latin American Free Trade Association. The Council drew up at Buenos Aires an action program of the most important things we would all like to see accomplished in the coming year in the

hemisphere. For the first time, it set in motion procedures which will enable us when we meet again a year hence to review in depth the steps that have been taken during the year to carry forward this program. The program includes immediate steps to be taken to improve Latin American planning operations; accelerate agricultural modernization and tax reform, stimulate the private sector, accelerate the integration movement, and secure a better interrelationship between trade and aid. Strong recommendations were also made for action in the education and health fields. In the course of the year, the Inter-American 

Committee of the Alliance for Progress (CIAP) will review the progress made during its country-by-country reviews of performance. To sum up my impressions of the meeting, I can say that the Alliance for Progress has never been more alive. I request that the Buenos Aires action program of the Alliance for 

Progress be entered in the record, along with my statement to the Council of 

March 29.

All of us now recognize that the full achievement of the goals of the Charter of Punta del Este will take more than the 10 years originally envsaged. Our 

Latin American colleagues were both heartened and challenged when President Johnson proposed through Secretary Rusk at the Rio Conference last November that the Alliance for Progress be extended on the basis of continued effective self-help actions by our partner nations and their own undertaking to join in mutual assistance commitments. 

The understandings reached at Rio are of great importance. There for the first time the Latin American countries formally recognized that the development efforts of each affected the development of all and the security of the entire hemisphere. Accordingly, they are now prepared to see that the obligation of self-help be recognized as a treaty commitment to the other members of the Alliance and in addition are also prepared to assume treaty commitments to help one another. We can already begin to see possibilities along this latter line. Mexico has made some significant commitments to Central America, pledging $5 million credit to the Central American Bank and the purchase of $1 million of the Bank's bonds. Venezuela has undertaken technical assistance programs with several countries, and Brazil and Argentina are sharing in multinational projects of special interest to some of their neighbors. 

ECONOMIC INTEGRATION 

Economic integration is one of the most important trends in the hemisphere. 

The Central American Common Market-within which trade has increased 123 percent in 2 years-is helping to illustrate the benefits that can come when Latin American countries work together. The Latin American Free Trade Association, which in 1965 more than doubled intraassociation trade over the 1959-61 volume, illustrates by its diffculties with tariff reduction and trade agreements the hard decisions that must be made if good intentions are to be converted into progress. The will is growing in Latin America to make the transformation from word to deed.

The Inter-American Development Bank will soon broaden those of its activities that contribute to ecomomic integration. Following up on a CIAP suggestion that a regional fund for multinational preinvestment studies be created, the IDB has nearly completed studies preliminary to the establishment of such a fund. Many projects are waiting in the wings for which such preinvestment studies might be appropriate, notably in the fields of river basin development, transportation, and telecommunications.

The United States supports the integration efforts of the nations of Latin America. We will continue to work with the IDB to assure that high priority is given to projects that promise further integration of Latin American development efforts. Such integration will not only advance economic development but will help to lessen political tensions.

FISCAL YEAR 1967 AID PROGRAM 

In keeping with the approach to foreign aid that he instituted 2 years ago, the President has requested an overall AID program that is the bare minimum required to support U.S. policies in relation to the developing world. For the 

AID participation in the Alliance for Progress, this means a program of $500 million for development loans, $95 million for technical cooperation, and $33.2 million for supporting assistance in fiscal year 1967. As he did last year, the 

President has made it plain that should circumstances arise that require addi-

tional U.S. funds for the Alliance for Progress, he will submit a supplemental appropriation request to the Congress.

The President has reemphasized U.S. determination that the basis for our assistance will be support to countries engaged in serious efforts to mobilize and apply their own resources to development. As the President has noted, the nations of Latin America under the Alliance for Progress have been in the forefront of the developing areas of the world in taking steps of this character. 

In his foreign aid message to the Congress on February 1, 1966, the President said: "The keynote of the Alliance for Progress has always been self-help. The pattern of our assistance *** demonstrates our determination to help those who help themselves."

The Congress is now considering legislation calling for a commitment of U.S. human and financial resources and food and fiber to help spur a revolution in education, attack with new intensity the problems of communicable diseases, and make available the agricultural skills and scientific advances that have made U.S. agriculture the most flourishing in the world. If hunger, illiteracy, and despair are to be eliminated, more resources, theirs and ours, must be applied to agriculture, education, and health. In Latin America, AID is already doing much in these fields, especially in the building of effective institutions that will become permanent elements of Latin American development. Among our efforts in these fields are:

Through an AID contract with a group of associated midwestern universities, the National University of Colombia, and the Colombian Agricultura] Institute will expand and improve the faculties of three agricultural colleges, reorienting the teaching toward applied research and extension. U.S. professors will assist in a modernization process whose ultimate objective is to increase and diversify agricultural production. The Ford, Kellogg, and Rockefeller Foundations and the World Bank are also contributing to or plan to contribute to this program. 

Contract technicians from the University of North Carolina have helped Peru establish an outstanding land-grant type college at the National Agricultural University of La Molina. Eighty percent of the faculty is now teaching on a full-time basis, a rarity in Latin America universities. The United States has loaned $4 million to help finance a $9 million expansion of the physical facilities at La Molina, enrollment in which is steadily increasing. 

Some 10,000 farmers are expected to benefit from AID's loan to Chile for fertilizer imports. This loan supports an important part of Chile's overall program to increase agricultural production, which includes more favorable pricing policies, agrarian reform, improved farm credit, and marketing systems. 

As a short-term measure to increase the productivity of Brazil's farms and provide more food for its growing population, AID has made available a $15 million loan to Brazil for fertilizer imports. Local currency derived from sale of the fertilizer is used in a selective agricultural credit program designed to increase basic foodstuff production.

AID is working with the 5 Central American national universities in developing an integrated university system to service the region. Progress is being made toward the development of regional schools in sanitary engineering, veterinary medicine, and business administration. 

During the next year, AID is planning to grant the Pan American Health Organization $1.3 million for malaria eradication, in addition to loans made in this field to several participating coutries. 

AID will continue to respond favorably to requests from the nations of Latin America for assistance in population matters. In fiscal year 1965, AID provided $400,000 to the Latin American Center for Economic and Social Development in Santiago, Chile, headed by the Reverend Roger E. Vekemans, S.J., to establish a center for the study of family and population, and nearly $200,000 for research and training in family sociology to Notre Dame University. 

AID LENDING PROGRAM 

Program lending will again be a major method of providing economic assistance to Latin America by AID in fiscal year 1967. Program loans double dividends. Through letters of credit established with U.S. banks, the dollars provided exchange for imports from the United States by the private sector of the borrowing countries. The local currencies received by the borrowing governments against sale of the dollars are then invested in development activities of high priority for which domestic resources are inadequate, including the

initiation or strengthening of key developmental institutions. For example, low-cost housing is constructed and mortgage insurance systems are strengthened; educational opportunities and facilities are enlarged; credit is made available to more rural people; campaigns are waged against disease; supplies of electrical power are enlarged and new roads are built to serve farmers and open new areas for settlement. 

The value of the program loan in economic development is well illustrated by U.S. experience in Brazil, Colombia, and Chile, countries to which the United 

States this year has made program loans totaling $295 million. [Security deletion.] In September 1965, the Government of Colombia instituted a comprehensive economic reform program that included substantial readjustment in exchange rates, few taxes, a balanced budget, a start toward liberalization of imports, and an anti-inflationary monetary policy. These actions led to negotiations by Colombia with a consultative group headed by the World Bank and including the United States and the IMF, at the conclusion of which AID agreed to make a program loan of up to $65 million in four segments. The Colombian economy is now moving forward.

Brazil and Chile are using program loans to support their measures of reform and modernization, to bolster the private sector and to expand public investment in roads, water and sewer systems, powerplants, education, and agriculture. In Brazil, the Castello Branco administration reduced the annual inflation rate from 140 percent in the first quarter of 1964 to a rate of 45 percent during 1965, with a downward trend during the year. At the same time, it pulled Brazil back from the brink of international bankruptcy and brought about a resumption of growth-to a rate of approximately 5 percent in 1965-after 2 years of near stagnation.

Under President Frei, Chile is demonstrating that a developing country can achieve progressive reforms within a democratic framework. In the first year of Chile's "revolution in liberty" primary and secondary school enrollment increased 20 percent, primary school teachers in the classroom increased 150 percent, and tax reform and improved tax administration provided 23 percent more in government revenue. The rate of inflation was decreased from 38 percent in 

1964 to 26 percent in 1965. Also, in 1965 alone, approximately 4,000 families were settled on their own land, nearly as many as during the preceding 35 years. 

OTHER COUNTRY ASSISTANCE 

In the context of adequate self-help by the Peruvians, some expansion of U.S. assistance to Peru is being considered. [Security deletion.] 

Peru is taking significant steps forward: since passage of a comprehensive agrarian reform law in 1964, more than 200,000 people have benefited from the distribution of 1.5 million acres. An imaginative community development program has had considerable effect among Indian mountain peasants. 

Another major recipient of U.S. assistance in fiscal year 1967 will be the Do minican Republic. [Security deletion.] 

The information distributed to the members of this committee recounts country by country the program proposed for fiscal year 1967. In previous years we have shown country totals as a range between two figures. [Security deletion. Although we are not doing so this year, the efficient use of our resources, the uncertainty as to the time by which specific feasible projects will be ready for support, and other factors make it essential to retain flexibility in the administration of the foreign assistance program. 

Two specific development programs-the housing guarantee program and the use of the mission directors' special development activities fund-warrant special mention.

HOUSING GUARANTEES 

The Congress has provided housing guarantee outhority for U.S. investments in housing programs in Latin America. To date, 30 projects have been authorized in 14 countries with guarantees totaling $162 million. These provide for 28,500 dwelling units, 14,000 of which are under construction and 5,200 of which are occupied. Letters of reservation have been issued for 3 additional projects with 3,000 units and $16 million in guarantees; 17 other projects with 

14.000 units and guarantees of $62 million are under review. 

The Foreign Assistance Act of 1965 increased the available guarantee authority from $250 to $400 million and added four new programs-credit institutions, lower income housing, free labor union housing and cooperatives, and local

participation-to the original pilot demonstration housing program. Constraints in the money market, and the executive branch's strenuous effort to reduce the U.S. balance-of-payments deficit have had a decided impact on the implementation of the program. As part of its current effort the President's 

Committee on the Balance of Payments has established guidelines on the available authority providing that $240 million in guarantee authority may be used for the demonstration program and $100 million for the four new programs: $30 million in fiscal year 1967 and $70 million in fiscal year 1968. 

SPECIAL DEVELOPMENT FUND 

The use of the special development fund has paid dividends far beyond the amount that has been invested. Each mission director is authorized to use up to $50,000 in support of small, high impact activities without prior Washington approval and without separate bilateral negotiations on each activity. 

A small AID contribution to a cooperative, to a community development activity, to a rural irrigation project, to a local effort generated by a Peace Corps volunteer, has resulted in development (and also in good will) far in excess of the amount of U.S. assistance invested. In Bolivia, $250 worth of cement enabled a community to finish construction of a wing on a children's hospital, itself a self-help project. In Costa Rica 195 hand pumps were made available to rural communities that lacked potable water. Axes and machetes were provided for Indian communities in Nicaragua so that they might clear land for the production of subsistence crops. In Peru, four hand sprayers and insecticides were given to a coffee-raising cooperative in a colonization area where the crop was in danger of being destroyed by insects. 

NON-FEDERAL INITIATIVES 

The U.S. contribution to the Alliance consists in growing measure of the work and the concern of private individuals, private enterprise and organizations, and other non-Federal institutions. For example, under the partners of the Alliance program, 30 States have cooperative arrangements with counterparts in 14 Latin American nations. Teachers are exchanged; books, equipment, and other resources flow between the partners groups. Nearly 70 U.S. colleges and universities have had contracts with AID to give assistance in Latin America. The 

American Institute for Free Labor Development is sponsoring housing programs and labor-training institutes. Assistance to the cooperative movement in Latin 

America has been given by the Cooperative Leagues of the U.S.A., the Credit 

Union National Association, and the National Rural Electric Cooperative As sociation. The National League for Insured Savings Associations has helped inaugurate savings and loan associations in several Latin American countries. 

Each year, more nongovernment organizations and individuals from the United States are taking part in the Alliance. Their contributions add materially to 

Latin American economic development and inter-American cooperation. 

LATIN AMERICA AND THE U.S. BALANCE OF PAYMENTS 

The committee may be considering the possible influence of U.S. aid distursements on our balance of payments. Official data, including recent Department of Commerce estimates of the U.S. balance-of-payments position, show that Latin 

America is not a problem area as far as potential dollar leakages to Europe and Japan from AID disbursements are concerned. 

On the contrary, the triangular payments results have been working to the favor of the United States. Latin America has been running a trade and overall balance-of-payments surplus with Western Europe and Japan, and in 1965 several of the larger Latin American countries used a payments surplus with Europe and Japan to finance a balance-of-payments deficit with the United States. 

While there has been some increase from very low levels in the gross foreign exchange reserve position of Latin America, the gold component of the area's reserves has been reduced by $125 million during the last 3 years according to data published by the International Monetary Fund. Thus, our Latin American friends have been helpful to the U.S. payments problem by their willingness to sell some of their gold to our Treasury at a time when other countries in the world were converting dollar holdings to gold.

CONCLUSION 

Significant advances have been made during the first 5 years of the Alliance for Progress. Although we may have been overly optimistic at Punta del Este 

5 years ago, a strong groundwork for further progress has bene laid. 

Most encouraging, Latin America has achieved a 2.5 percent or better increase in per capita gross national product during the last 2 years and every evidence is that at least this will be accomplished again in 1966. And behind these general economics figures there are the physical and human achievements: the tying together of farm and city by new roads, the ending of endemic diseases, the new opportunities for schooling and vocational and professional training, the introduction of modern industry and the skills that go with it-in short, the conversion of once-stagnant societies into participants in the contemporary world.

The President has requested a 5-year authorization for the foreign economic assistance program, including the Alliance for Progress. Congressional approval of this request would give encouragement to the developing countries in initiating and carrying out their own sustained multiyear efforts to accomplish specific goals of economic development, reform, and stabilization. 

May I. before concluding, mention the favorable effect of bipartisan support by the Congress of the United States foreign assistance program and the Alliance for Progress. I was happy to note in the recent statement entitled "New 

Directions and New Emphasis in Foreign Aid" by 25 Members of the House, that the United States has a particular concern and particular responsibility with respect to Latin America and that U.S. relations with the other nations of this hemisphere must be especially wise and especially well administered. 

The importance of an increasingly prosperous, stable, and democratically governed Latin America to a world environment conducive to our own liberty and prosperity should not ever require mention. Yet it is an unhappy fact that at various times in our history, preoccupation with other regions has made us forget this elementary truth. The Alliance for Progress was a compact with our sister nations which should put forever behind us any recurrence of such periods of neglect.

The program the President has proposed in support of the Alliance for Progress is both imaginative and possible. I recommend and urge your favorable consideration. 

Mr. GORDON. Thank you. As you know, I have only been in my present position officially since 

March 9. This follows some four and a quarter years as our Ambassador in Brazil. I am doing a great deal of homework in order to become acquainted with parts of the region which I do not yet know at firsthand.

I am particularly happy that the position for the last 2 years has combined the responsibilities of Assistant Secretary of State and Coordinator for the Alliance for Progress because I have been involved in the Alliance for Progress since it began at the beginning of 1961. 

I regard it, as does President Johnson, as the cornerstone of our policies in the hemisphere.

I would like to summarize first some of the recent political highlights in the year since my predecessor appeared before this committee on the corresponding occasion.

A number of the members, of course, have been traveling in Latin 

America and were present at the Rio Conference last November. We had Congressmen Selden and Mailliard as advisers to that delegation, and at the Conference last week in Buenos Aires Congressmen Cameron and Whalley were advisers to us. 

A few comments on the political highlights: first the Dominican

Republic, which obviously has been a matter of intense concern and interest. There, after a very difficult period indeed, the prospects look very favorable for the holding of a free and fair election on June 1,

which was the date determined by the so-called Institutional Act agreed to by the two contending sides a few months ago. That is to be followed by the installation of the elected regime on July 1. 

The principal parties are all preparing for this campaign. The 

PRD, the party of former President Juan Bosch, is supposed to have its convention next week, the weekend of April 9 and 10, to nominate a candidate. This could be Mr. Bosch. It could be someone else. It is not clear yet. The party at least is showing every indication of participating in the election. The Social Christian Party, likewise, and the other two principal parties who had announced candidates for some time, both former Presidents Balaguer and Bonelly, have been actively campaigning.

The OAS policy there, as you know, has been to help create conditions through the use of the Peace Force for a free election. For a considerable period of time now the occasional political murders have ceased. The new chief of the national police and the new heads of the armed forces appointed by the provisional President, Garcia Godoy, all appear to be devoted to the cause of maintaining order, but not directly involving themselves in politics. We are quite hopeful about the prospects for a gradual restoration or creation of a stable political condition.

In three other Latin American countries there have been elections recently, Costa Rica at the beginning of February, had a very closely contested election [security deletion] and they elected President Trejos, who is expected to take office at the beginning of next month. 

Guatemala [security deletion] also had a fairly closely contested election in which none of the three candidates received an absolute majority of the votes. Therefore the selection of the president falls to the congress which was elected on the same day. The congress is scheduled to meet in the first week of May in order to make this selection. 

[Security deletion.] 

Mr. GORDON. In Colombia there was a very important congressional election a couple of weeks ago, which is to be followed by the presi dential election the first week of May. [Security deletion.] 

In Ecuador there was a change of leadership last week, as you all know. [Security deletion]. 

By common agreement of both military and civilian leaders they replaced last week the then governing junta with Clemente Yerovi, who is regarded as one of the outstanding senior statesmen of Ecuador, a man in his early sixties, very vigorous, former Minister of Economy, a man who introduced bananas into Ecuador which became their chief export earning commodity. [Security deletion]. 

Bolivia, which has been another traditional area of some political tension and difficulty, appears to be proceeding toward an election in July. The principal candidate has been a former co-President, Air 

Force Gen. Rene Barrientos. [Security deletion]. 

Turning to some [security deletion] other situations where the AID program is of special importance: In Chile, after a period of great difficulty in the copper field, the principal strike, which had gone on for about 212 months in the Braden copper mine, was finally settled last week and the men are back at work.

There had been sympathy strikes in some other mines, which didn't last very long. At one of the mines disorders had to be suppressed, and eight people were killed, which is almost unprecedented in Chile. 

[Security deletion.] 

Mr. GORDON. In Brazil, a case which I, of course, know especially well from my experience, the Government's difficult program of economic stabilization, development, and reform is proceeding. It has aroused a considerable amount of popular discontent, which is normal in a stabilization program after galloping inflation. 

The new president, who succeeds Castello Branco, is to be elected in October and the new Congress is to be elected in November. [Security deletion.]

They are trying the difficult process of economic rehabilitation and institutional reform, and, at the same time, trying to restructure their political system on the two-party basis. [Security deletion.] 

The Havana Conference of the representatives of radical movements in three continents made it clear that Latin America was still a major area for Communist subversive attack, and the notion of so-called national liberation movements, which really mean a violent subversive movement, was endorsed with enthusiasm by that conference. There have been indications, at least, in some countries that that has been followed up by direct action.

So much for the general political situation. I find it encouraging. 

It depends, of course, on one's standard. It is not entirely tranquil. 

Democracy and constitutional government are still very tender plants in much of the hemisphere.

This should be expected, I think, in an area where there is so much poverty, illiteracy, and general economic backwardness. But, the trend is favorable. Certainly, as compared with much of Africa and 

Asia, at the present time, Latin America gives a great deal of reason for encouragement.

Turning now to the Alliance for Progress, the meeting which I just attended at Buenos Aires was the fourth annual ministerial review of progress and problems. It was reviewing really the first 5 years of the Alliance, the anniversary of which we will celebrate on the fifth anniversary of the signing of the Charter of Punta del Este, August 17. My impressions from the Buenos Aires meeting are also very encouraging. There was a general spirit of cooperation shown there and a clear desire for concrete action. 

Some of the press reports tried to give an impression of violent conflict between the Latin American delegations and the U.S. delegation. 

This is quite erroneous. There had been some concern at the beginning because of the discussions in Panama on OAS Charter revision, that we might be retreating from what had been agreed to at Rio de Janeiro in November. Once it was made clear that this was a misunderstanding, that concern evaporated and the delegates then turned their attention to the specific kinds of problems for action next year that they should deal with.

The principal document coming out of the conference was the socalled Buenos Aires action program. I ask that it be circulated to all members. I believe you have it before you. You may also be interested in the speech that I made a week ago today to the plenary, which I also ask to be circulated, setting forth the U.S. point of view and our own appraisal of how the Alliance is going.

The net of it all is that the Alliance is very much alive, and in fact governments and principal political forces in the continent nowadays, in contrast with the situation 5 or 10 years ago, have economic development, social progress, structural reform, and self-help in various forms right at the center of their interests. The principal items in the action program include tax improvement, agricultural improvement, both on production and reform, education, especially specialized education for development, incentives for private sector expansion, improvements in capital markets, development banks, and wider shareholding in private enterprises.

The subject of investment guarantees was discussed at some length, and we offered a resolution for consideration of a multilateral form of investment guarantee to be worked out by the Inter-American Committee on the Alliance for Progress, the CIAP. 

This was endorsed by a large majority of the delegation, and the CIAP will be following up on this with such governments as are interested. It is voluntary. I hope that this may prove to be a useful way of proceeding with the investment guarantee idea. 

Some attention was also given to the field of health, including nutrition and population problems. A great deal of emphasis was placed on pushing ahead with Latin American economic integration. There we expressed our own interest in helping this movement through financial and technical support to so-called multinational projects. 

The Inter-American Development Bank is taking a lead in this field. 

By multinational projects, we have in mind especially highways in the South American interior, the eastern slopes of the Andes with branches off into Brazil and northern Argentina, Paraguay, on out to the Atlantic; river basin development, including power and flood control and irrigation where needed; telecommunications; and also possibly natural gas pipelines from the Bolivian and other fields to markets to the East and to some extent to the West. 

The other principal subject of discussion was the expansion of trade in relation to aid. I think there is an increasing realization that these have to go together. Latin American shares of world markets have been tending to decline. This is a matter of great and natural concern.

They realize basically that, in addition to more stable markets which they desire for traditional export commodities they must diversify, they must improve their competitiveness and productivity; and of course they look forward to export diversification as part of the process of industrialization and general economic diversification. 

We also made an improvement in the multilateral machinery of the Alliance by replacing the former so-called Panel of Nine (or nine wise men, as they were sometimes called) by a group of high-level economic specialists to be directly connected to CIAP. The CIAP is now the recognized single main multilateral executive agent for the Alliance for Progress and these technical advisers will be working under their direction. Of course, they maintain their intellectual freedom, but they are working as experts and not governmental representatives.

Mr. Chairman, may I turn to the specific program that we have before the committee, which is summarized on page 12 of the prepared statement. The total program that we have in mind for fiscal 1967

in Latin American involves $628.2 million of which $500 million is in development loans, $95 million in technical cooperation, and $33.2 million in supporting assistance.

The new obligational authority which corresponds to that total is $575.9 million. In the development loan category it is expected that about half of the funds will be used for so-called program loans. As you know, this year about half of the available loan funds have been committed to program loans for three Latin American countries- 

Colombia, Chile, and Brazil.

I was directly involved in the negotiations of the Brazilian loans both this year and last year and have followed the others quite closely. 

I believe this is an extraordinarily useful device, useful for several reasons.

First, it permits a negotiation in terms of the general economic performance of the government concerned, and its whole program for development, for mobilization of its own resources for self-help in the broadest sense. Typically the commitments of the government are set forth in letters to the chairman of the CIAP, they are reviewed by the CIAP in their country reviews, and there is a quarterly review of actual performance in relationship to commitments before the next slice of funds is released. The dollars are used for imports from the 

United States, thereby avoiding an adverse impact on our balance of payments. A fraction of them in Chile and Brazil this year is being set aside for specific imports for private industry expansion with the local currency counterpart being used to help finance those imports and give some credit to the importers to help finance their expansions.

The other great advantage is that the program loan makes available on a noninflationary basis substantial volumes of local currency to supplement the resources being mobilized by the country itself to be used for major fields of importance under the Alliance for Progress-for example, in investment education and health; for building of new financial institutions for agricultural credit; in expanded use of fertilizers and high-quality seeds for industrial expansion; and for new types of housing and financial institutions. 

In addition to the program loans, the main project loan fields that we have been working in and intend to continue working in include power and transportation, although the World Bank and the Inter- American Development Bank are increasingly taking over the financial responsibility for those major basic fields. We have special emphasis this year, in keeping with President Johnson's program on food and agriculture, on education and on health-the health field is still rather small-scale in financial terms. 

We want to give special emphasis to the multinational project idea in support of Latin American integration as I have mentioned, following the lead of the Inter-American Development Bank. Many of those projects are still in the feasibility study stage, but a few of them will be ready for full project starting. 

Finally, we intend to continue to assist in the creation and strengthening of various types of credit institutions. Under the heading of technical cooperation, we are proposing assistance for the institution building, heavy emphasis on education, public administration, and the public safety programs which are of great importance for internal security.

A small but enlarged fraction of these funds will be devoted to a number of countries to work in the population field, closely related to health and nutrition. The supporting assistance category is mainly composed of assistance to the Dominican Republic where because of very special circumstances we are projecting [security deletion] in fiscal 1967 for continued support of investment activities and current operating expenditures of the Dominican budget. 

[Security deletion.] 

Mr. GORDON. Also in that category there is the last $5 million of a grant to Trinidad, which was related to the agreement on the naval base. [Security deletion.] 

One word on the impact of this whole program on the U.S. balance of payments. The dollars that we provide are, of course, tied to procurement in the United States. Sometimes a concern has been expressed that they might substitute for dollars that would otherwise be spent for imports from here and might release funds for purchases in Europe. We made a special study of the Latin American balance-ofpayments situation with relation to the United States on one side, and Europe and Japan on the other. We find for the area as a whole, and for the principal individual countries who are receiving aid from us, that they typically run surpluses in their relationship with Europe, deficits in their relationship with the United States.

The effect of this in fact is to assist our balance of payments by transferring financial resources from Europe and Japan back to the United States. We are satisfied that the net impact of the aid program to 

Latin America on the U.S. balance of payments is very close to neutral, if not favorable.

And to conclude, may I say one word about the necessity for flexibility. We have, of course, submitted to you an illustrative program. 

The actual program during the year is never identical with the illustrative program submitted several months before the fiscal year begins because circumstances do change, new opportunities arise, sometimes project preparation in a particular field takes longer than was hoped, sometimes a happy surprise comes along and projects get ready sooner than we had expected. [Security deletion.]

You have such areas as the major development in fertilizer which a number of American companies are interested in in several parts of Latin America, and which we are very anxious to promote. There the precise time required to get a well-worked-out, technically sound project and to get the necessary arrangements with the governments simply is unpredictable. For this reason we don't know which country will be developing a project first, but the general type of program is fully set forth in the documents we have submitted. 

The long-term authorization which is being requested has been discussed in earlier hearings here. In the case of the Alliance for Progress, it is a continuation of what has been done under the present legislation since 1962. The original period covered has now run out and the request is that it be provided for a new 5-year period. 

We are satisfied that this does encourage long-term programing and long-term self-help commitments by the recipient countries and we think this is salutary. 

That covers, I think, the principal highlights, Mr. Chairman. I am at your disposal.

Mr. GORDON. The program loans, though they come out of funds for our fiscal year, are related in each case to the recipient government's own budget year which is a calendar year, so the Chilean loan of $80 million for our present fiscal year covers their calendar year 1966. 

We are going to have our first quarterly review of the fiscal year 1966 program loans in May. [Security deletion.] But with respect to last year, 1965, the results have been very encouraging. Great headway was made, for example, in increasing the proportion of Chilean resources raised through taxes and other devices for investment, in reducing the rate of inflation there, and in getting started, at least, on a process of more rapid private and public investment and development.

Chilean program will be under very close surveillance by you? 

Mr. GORDON. Yes. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Secretary, I was interested in a reference you made to some of the press statements that came out of the meeting from which you just returned. I was quite concerned about some of the statements I read, to the effect that Latin American countries were disappointed in the United States, reports indicating that they wanted more assistance, and wanted it with no strings attached. 

They also said the United States had to work harder to get the Latin American countries to agree in most general terms. It was said they agreed to a rather meaningless statement, but they wanted specific commitments. Is there anything to these news stories? 

Mr. GORDON. I would say there is a very small amount of flame in relation to a huge amount of smoke. Each of the items that you have mentioned, Mr. Chairman, had some expression of concern by one or another of the Latin American delegations there. On the question, for example, of aid with no strings attached, they would like it if there were not, for example, 50-50 shipping requirements in our aid legislation. That is nothing new.

They have felt that way for years. They would like it if we did not tie our aid to procurement from the United States. They are, however, perfectly well aware that we are suffering from a serious balance-of-payments problem in the United States. 

They also know that our European colleagues have been tying most of their own aid to procurement from them for years, even at a time when we were not so doing. I would say that while there was a certain amount of grumbling on that subject, it wasn't very severe. 

On the question of specific commitments, this I think has mostly to

do with the discussions in Panama and the question of exactly how the agreements at Rio are to be incorporated into the charter of the OAS. This will be a continuing discussion over the next couple of months.

We have made it clear that on the one hand we consider the broad commitments about the principal of mutual aid, made at Rio, to be standing matters of U.S. governmental policy. They are not specific. 

They don't talk, of course, about quantities. They talk about the spirit with which we approach these things. 

We have also made it clear that we believe, as we said at Rio, that the essential principles-broad principles should be put into the charter. 

Our view is that the charter is a constitutional type document and that it is a mistake to put in it details or temporary provisions that would be useful only for a few years. These should be left for annual study by these annual meetings of ministers, and not put into charter language. There was a great deal of clarification of this point during the discussions in Buenos Aires. I believe that the concern on that subject has been greatly reduced. 

The other principal thing which is a matter of real concern, more I think than the other points mentioned in the quotations that you read, Mr. Chairman, has to do with trade policy There is, for example, concern in agricultural exporting countries [security deletion] as to whether the new food-for-freedom program may interfere with their export markets and export opportunities. 

There is concern that as Latin American countries become more efficient in producing certain types of manufactured goods and try to enter the U.S. market that they may find the door shut. Some of them who have been exporting cotton textiles, for example, feel that the limitations which have been put on imports in the United States are excessively severe.

Then there are particular cases-for example, the Venezuelans don't like restrictions on oil imports into the United States. They would like to be treated the way Canada and Mexico are in that respect. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Secretary. 

Mr. GORDON. If I could make one other comment, Mr Chairman, I would say they are even more concerned about the European Common Market policy on restricting imports. They hope, and perfectly justly, that we will in negotiating with the European Common Market try to help open up the European markets to them as well as to ourselves. This has in fact been our policy.

Mr. Mailliard.

Mr. MAILLIARD. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Secretary, following this line just raised by the chairman, I didn't see too much in the way of the press reports as to the Buenos Aires meeting but I saw quite a bit on the meeting in Panama. Some of the criticism that I have read in the press was directed, at, one, the fact that you weren't there, also that Ellsworth Bunker was meeting another crisis in the Dominican Republic and as a result our delegation was a little lower level than it should have been, and the thing tended to get somewhat out of hand. 

Is there any basis in fact for that?

Mr. GORDON. Mr. Congressman, it is certainly true I wasn't there, considering that the meeting there began at the end of February when 

---

I was just making my farewells in Brazil, and continued up through the overlap with this Buenos Aires meeting. I don't think that should be any surprise. I at least haven't yet learned to be in two places at one time.

Ambassador Bunker was the chief of our delegation, and we had hoped he could spend several days there at the beginning, middle, and the end, but his duties in the Dominican Republic simply made that impossible.

Ambassador Woodward, who was the actual head of our delegation, is a very experienced senior man-one of my predecessors, incidentally, in this present position-and I don't think it would be at all fair to characterize this as low-level representation on the part of the 

United States. 

The principal trouble, I think, was that in the discussion of the economic and social provisions based on the Economic and Social Act of Rio, a number of the Latin American delegations were desirous of incorporating in the charter everything agreed to at Rio more or less in its full verbiage. 

That was not- -as you recall from the discussions at Rio, that was not really the intention there. The Act of Rio says that its articles are the guiding principles upon which our Governments agree, and that the essence of them, as general standards, are to be incorporated in the charter.

The question as to the form and language in which they were to be incorporated was, of course, left to be discussed first at Panama, and then refined in the Council here, and finally decided at the next Buenos 

Aires meeting, which is now scheduled for July 30.

Mr. MAILLIARD. To get in my own mind the sequence of events, will there be any further special meetings to proceed with this business between Panama and the July meeting in Buenos Aires, or is this now just going to be staff consultations here in Washington?

Mr. GORDON. In fact, we expect the Council of the OAS, the Permanent Council, to take this in hand, probably with some special advisers on the economic side, and to continue the process of negotiation. 

Technically, there was supposed to be negotiation at Panama and a period of 2 months in which the Council would bring together the documents, get advice from governments, make its own observations, and then send those on to governments in 60 days before the meeting is held.

Technically, the Council is not supposed to negotiate. I have discussed this question with many of the Ministers and, in fact, everyone expects there will be a process of further negotiation during the next 2 months. My hope and real expectation is that these misunderstandings in Panama, already largely dispelled, can be fully resolved, and that by May 31, which is the Council's deadline, we can either have complete agreement or have the residual disagreements reduced to a point where there will be no reason why the Foreign Ministers shouldn't be able to negotiate the final form.

We must have a continuing process of consultation with the Senate. We are talking about treaty language and, I think myself, it is unfortunate that the Panama and the Buenos Aires meetings happened to overlap in the way they did. Our original idea was to suspend work on the economic provisions at Panama if they hadn't finished up at the time the Buenos Aires meeting began.

We didn't secure general agreement to that, and it resulted in an overlap in time and a very considerable amount of confusion which was reflected in the press. I think it is also fair to say that some of the press reports from Panama were completely without foundation. The press had a report saying that we had backed off entirely from the Economic and Social Act of Rio because we were unhappy that a permanent Inter-American Peace Force hadn't been agreed to. 

Actually, nobody at Panama was promoting a permanent Inter- American Peace Force. This was a pure piece of press invention. 

Mr. MAILLIARD. Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Zablocki. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Secretary, in scanning your statement I fail to find any reference or description of the work of the population staff of the Office of Insti tutional Development in AID's Latin American Bureau. 

To what extent does AID take part in programs of that character in this field?

Mr. GORDON. I think there is a phrase somewhere in the statement, Mr. Congressman, on the subject, but only in very general terms. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Chairman, if we could have for the record-I don't expect the Secretary to take time now-but if he could amplify for the record the type of programs being carried on. I have no objection if we assist the international organizations in this field, but I do think we are asking for political repercussions if AID does it directly. 

Mr. GORDON. Certainly, Mr. Congressman. I will be delighted to provide a complete statement for the record. I might say that in general our policy here has been to help, of course, only at the request of the people being helped, first, in studies of the problem so that it is better understood, demographic studies; and second, on an extremely small scale, on public health activities either governmentally or privately sponsored in the countries concerned, which will assist families desiring assistance in family planning.

We do not help with birth control supplies or devices but we do offer help of the sort that I have just described. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Then we do not have an action program in Latin America?

Mr. GORDON. We have an action program- we have a very small action program of the sort I have described, but let us supply the details for the record if we may, which will indicate exactly what is being done and what is being contemplated, if that is satisfactory. 

(The information follows:) AID ASSISTANCE TO POPULATION PROGRAMS IN LATIN AMERICA 

AID assistance to population programs in Latin America is directed solely to the fields of research, training, and education. No AID funds have been used or are programed for contraceptive devices or the manufacture thereof. 

Fiscal year 1965 

AID provided $1,097 million in fiscal year 1965 in the following ways: (1) To the Centro Para El Desarrollo Economico y Social de America Latina (DESAL) 

in Santiago, Chile, headed by Rev. Roger E. Vekemans, S.J., $400,000 to establish a center for the study of family and population; (2) to the University of Cali fornia, International Population and Urban Research, Institute of International 

Studies, $164,500 for demographic and population consultant services in specific Latin American countries upon request; (3) to the Instituto Colombiano de 

Desarrollo Social (ICODES) in Bogota, Colombia, headed by Rev. Gustavo Perez,

$40,000 for developing a Latin American resource for production of audiovisual materials depicting the role of family planning in social development in Latin America; (4) to Notre Dame University, $177,500 for research and training in family sociology with particular emphasis on the relationships of family value systems to changing social-economic expectations; (5) to the Population Council, $100,000 for support of research and a study of fertility patterns in Latin 

America; (6) to the International Planned Parenthood Federation, $121,000 for the training of medical and paramedical personnel with respect to family planning as an integral aspect of preventive medicine and for the education of leadership groups on the relationship of family planning to responsible parenthood and maternal and child health; (7) to the Family Planning Association of Jamaica, $24,800 to support the Jamaican Government in carrying out a limited educational program in family planning; also $17,000 for two AID-owned audiovisual trucks to travel to public and private health centers and hospitals; and (8) to the American International Association for Economic and Social Development, 

$25,000 for population studies in Costa Rica; also $25,000 for U.S. AID mission support of the Government of Costa Rica as it seeks to determine the economic, social, and health implications for family planning.

So far in fiscal year 1966 an existing contract has been extended with Celade, the U.N. regional training center in Santiago, Chile, for training and research in demography. The additional amount provided is $100,000.

Familia-Celap) in Santiago, Chile. Seminar and training courses would be instituted to develop an active and continuing program relating to family regulation with the cooperation of church, education, and Government representatives; $50,000 has been budgeted.

Catholic organization; $140,000 has been budgeted.
