Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Morse. 

Mr. MORSE. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I, too, would like to take this opportunity to welcome the distinguished Assistant Secretary on his first appearance before this committee in his new capacity. 

As a very junior member on the minority side, I think the United States is mighty fortunate to have a man of his ability, integrity, experience, and devotion. I think I speak for the committee when I say that we have immense confidence in the job you propose to do. 

Two or three very brief questions, Mr. Secretary. Does the Under 

Secretary for Economic Affairs still carry the title of Special Assistant to the President for Latin America?

Mr. GORDON. No. 

Mr. MORSE. Is there any such person designated?

Mr. GORDON. No. As you know, when Mr. Rostow was named 

Special Assistant to the President the other day in a press conference, the President indicated Mr. Rostow would have an interest in longterm development in Latin America, which of course- 

Mr. MORSE. Without any formal designation? 

Mr. GORDON. That is right. 

Mr. MORSE. Has the Department recommended, or does the White

House contemplate the appointment of a new U.S. representative on the Committee for the Alliance for Progress?

Mr. GORDON. We have that under discussion. I just got back 2 days ago from Buenos Aires. I haven't had a chance to discuss that with Mr. Rostow or other authorities. We have to consider it because it may be difficult for him to carry on fulfilling that responsibility. 

Mr. MORSE. I would think it would be impossible for Mr. Rostow to continue, with the intimate association he has now with the White House, to bring the kind of detachment to the CIAP that is essential. 

Mr. GORDON. We would like to find someone who can do as fine a job as he has done on CIAP.

Mr. MORSE. I was one of the Members of the House who supported the 5-year authorization for the Alliance when it was first enacted. 

Can you tell me, sir, what you deem to be the advantages of the 5-year authorization that the Alliance has had since 1961?

Mr. GORDON. It is essentially a matter of our feeling that we have the backing of the Congress in the notion that the Alliance is a longterm program and that we can talk with the Latin American governments about their making corresponding long-term self-help efforts that they can and think about programing their development on a longterm basis.

As you recall, one of the items in the Charter of Punta del Este was the notion of long-term planning for economic development and social progress. 

We believe that this kind of explicit congressional endorsement of a long-term approach does help get that spirit of being abroad for the long haul across to the Latin American governments. 

Our aid relationships with Latin America over the period since the beginning of the Second World War have tended to be in my judgment on much too much of a hand-to-mouth basis, dealing with immediate crises and balance of payments or budgets or things of this emergency nature. We would like to get out of that kind of business and, to the extent that we can, into long-term cooperation supporting long-term developmental efforts. 

Mr. MORSE. And the 5-year period has been, in your opinion, a desirable period of time for the authorization? 

Mr. GORDON. Yes, I think it is reasonable.

As you know, we talked about a 10-year program. We have had one 

5 years. Now we are talking about the second 5 years. It seems to me a very good approach.

Mr. MORSE. If we were to revert, insofar as the Alliance is concerned, to a 1-year program, would that have an beneficial or adverse effect upon the operation of the program? 

Mr. GORDON. I think that would have an adverse effect, certainly psychologically, particularly in the face of previously having had 

5-year authorization. 

Mr. MORSE. Would it also make it most difficult to attain the kind of things you are seeking to accomplish? 

Mr. GORDON. I think it would raise in the minds of the Latin 

American governments some question as to whether the Congress supports the executive branch in the notion that the Alliance for Progress is a long-term joint effort among all of the hemisphere countries.

Mr. MORSE. To a lesser extent, but to some extent, an authorization of 2 years would have an adverse effect. 

Mr. GORDON. Yes, sir.

Mr. MORSE. Two years would not be sufficient to give you the kind of operational flexibility properly to do your job? 

Mr. GORDON. I can't honestly say that we couldn't and wouldn't operate. After all, our operations are controlled by annual appropriations. I would think that that much of a reduction would have a seriously adverse psychological effect.

Mr. MORSE. I brought up these questions, Mr. Secretary, to share with you my very deep concern that the administration's effort to expand all programs to a 5-year authorization may result in the Alliance for Progress being cut back to a 2-year authorization with the other programs.

I would think that is something that we should all try to avoid. 

I am very apprehensive about this. I think the administration came up here with a 5-year request really believing it was going to get no more than 2 years for the other programs, and I fear it will be a 2-year program for the Alliance as well. 

Mr. GORDON. I share that concern. I hope such a result can be avoided.

Congressman Morse was identified with a statement, along with a number of other Members of this House, in connection with our interest in and cooperation with Latin America, and all of us on the executive side have found that to be a very helpful initiative. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

In the rundown on the funding which you have, Mr. Secretary, I didn't see any reference to the money that might be devoted to multilateral projects. Would that come out of developmental loans? 

Mr. GORDON. Yes; both for feasibility studies and for financing of projects. 

Mr. FRASER. How strongly are we pushing regional projects?

Mr. GORDON. We are pushing hard. One of the main loans projected to Central America, for example, is a follow-on loan to the development bank for Central America, which is part of their integration movement.

In South America, the Inter-American Development Bank is proposing at its governors' meeting, which comes later this month, to establish, and the United States will support this, a fund out of its own resources, a small fund at the beginning for multinational feasibility studies.

We have said-I said in my speech in Buenos Aires, and Secretary Fowler at the Mexico City meeting this month will repeat this, that where there are large feasibility studies and the resources of the IDB are not sufficient, we would like to join with them to help finance those studies and recruit technical help to do them well. 

I think this is an enormously important thing, not only to support the integration movement, but also to speed up the development of that large interior area of South America which is very good for human settlement and has lots of resources which are almost completely undeveloped in any way.

As I said last week in Buenos Aires, this is the largest area on the face of the globe which is easily habitable by man and which is still largely empty. 

Mr. FRASER. In your statement made down at Buenos Aires, you say that lagging are agricultural modernization, export diversification, and education beyond the primary level. 

I am interested in agricultural modernization problems. I don't see anything in our programs which is designed to do very much very rapidly to develop the rural sector of these countries where most of the people live.

I am aware of a number of the programs, to build credit institutions, to develop agricultural training institutions and extension services and so on. I find the order of magnitude of these efforts to be about 

1 out of a needed scale of 100. This is my frank opinion.

It seems to me what happens in Latin America is going to be determined more by this than any other area of concern, speaking in terms of the rural-versus-city situation. 

Mr. GORDON. This is enormously important. I think we fully agree on that.

Just to give some idea of the order of magnitude of our involvement in this field, loans for agricultural projects in Latin America by AID in the last fiscal year, 1965, were $63.9 million.

Mr. FRASER. That is primarily what kind of programs? 

Mr. GORDON. Feeder roads, agricultural credit, assistance in fertilizer imports and fertilizer distribution, agricultural cooperatives, rural electrification in a few cases, and community development in rural areas. These are the main categories. 

For the current fiscal year we expect it may go up to $97.1 million, which is roughly a 50-percent expansion over last year. 

In a very tentative way for fiscal 1967, we have a series of projects in mind that may run as high as $161 million, which would be two and a half times the amount in the last year and about 70 percent higher than the amount contemplated for this year. 

You can see by these figures that we have a very steeply rising curve for work in this field. More resources ought to go into it. More local resources of the countries concerned ought to go into it, as well as ours.

After all, to be effective we are talking about getting to thousands of communities and hundreds of thousands or millions of farmers, and this does require a lot of organization. Very few of the Latin 

American countries have really satisfactory extension services, for example. That is one of the fields in which we have been working for a long time.

The number of qualified university-level agronomists and agricultural technicians is small. In our technical assistance program one of the main fields of emphasis in education is getting the land-grant college type of education expanded in Latin America. There has been activity of that kind for many years in several countries, including Brazil. I have visited several of these institutions myself when Ambassador there. They simply aren't on a large enough scale at the present time.

Mr. FRASER. Would you agree that for most of the rural communities after 5 years of the Alliance there has been very little visible impact?

Mr. GORDON. No, I think there has been substantial impact. I would say in relation to the needs it has been seriously inadequate. 

That is why I made the statement in Buenos Aires. 

Mr. FRASER. I don't get the sense that we are organized to give enough emphasis to agricultural development. We kind of work away at various points, facets that sort of surface more than others, like working to develop extension systems, development of credit institutions and so on.

My sense is that these efforts are--I don't know how to say it-they are not a part of a coordinated, determined program of substantial enough proportions and don't involve enough U.S. personnel with technical capabilities, operating capabilities, to really make the kind of difference that I think we need to make. 

We don't have a clear time period within which we really expect to see the kind of progress that should be made. This is an impression, and I may be wrong about it. I know that in many of the Latin 

American countries agricultural production is falling behind on a per capita basis. This is after 5 years of the Alliance. 

Mr. GORDON. That is right. It is a very worrisome thing. 

Mr. FRASER. They are moving backward measured on this one scale. 

Mr. GORDON. In food production-

Mr. FRASER. Take Chile for example. They use $150 million or more of their export earnings to buy food.

Mr. GORDON. That is right. They are large food importers and over a period of time this doesn't make any sense. Latin America should be a large food exporting area as already is the case from Argentina and Uruguay. It makes no sense for these countries to have net food imports. 

Mr. FRASER. As they slip back and have to use export earnings our aid is canceled out? 

Mr. GORDON. That is right.

I would like to comment though, Congressman, that it isn't just a question of how we organize U.S. resources and U.S. personnel. Personnel has been something of a bottleneck. We had great trouble, for example, in recruiting in Brazil, where I know the situation best, sufficient numbers of competent personnel. We finally made one breakthrough in a large contract arrangement with our own U.S. Department of Agriculture for a substantial team of highly qualified people, some long-term employees of the Department and some brought in from agricultural colleges and State agricultural services in various parts of the United States. But the main bottleneck is organization in the countries concerned.

As you know, public administration in general is not very advanced in Latin America. This is especially true of the agricultural services except for a few traditional major export commodities. 

In Brazil, for example, they are pretty good in relation to coffee and sugar in São Paulo. But in the northern part of the country where a large number of farmers live, where production is low [security deletion] there is not adequate network to cover this collossal piece of territory. We can't go in ourselves and fill that gap. [Security deletion.]

Chairman MORGAN. Mrs. Bolton, 

Mrs. BOLTON. I am wondering how far advanced are we in the discussions of possibly building another canal? 

Mr. GORDON. As you know, Mrs. Bolton, in January we when I say "we" I mean the U.S. negotiating team, headed by Ambassador Robert 

Anderson-submitted to the Panamanians drafts of two treaties and one agreement. One treaty was to replace the 1903 treaty which governs the present arrangements on the existing locked canal. The second was a treaty governing future arrangements for a sea level canal and its operation and maintenance. And the third was a military base rights agreement. Those have been under very extensive study by the Panamanian negotiating team and the authorities. 

On my way to Buenos Aires I passed through Panama for a day and a half and spoke there with President Robles, with the Foreign Minister, and attended a lunch with all three members of their negotiating team, and had a chance to talk with all of those people about the status of the negotiations. Although of course Ambassador Robert 

Anderson is in charge of handling the negotiations from our side. 

They have been working diligently on preparing their comments and counterproposals to our proposals. There is not yet a firm date. 

The next step for them is to come back to us- 

Mrs. BOLTON. May I interrupt? Are we planning to buy outright land for the canal? That is part of our agreement?

Mr. GORDON. Not as I understand it. I am not familiar with all of the treaty details. I do not believe that that is the idea. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Do we have any idea of the possible cost?

Mr. GORDON. There are very preliminary estimates. The Inter- 

Oceanic Canal Commission, headed by Ambassador Anderson, which also has on it Dr. Milton Eisenhower and several other distinguished. members, has under consideration this question of costs at alternative sites. There are [security deletion] sites being studied as one of its major areas of activity. 

Mrs. BOLTON. No decision has been made?

Mr. GORDON. No. This study under the congressional authority goes at least until June of 1968 and it may perhaps have to be extended. beyond that. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you.

I wanted to ask you about what has been brought to my attention, the difficulty we have and what has been called our blunder in Panama.. 

And the 19 Latin American delegates led by Brazil and Chile approved a document, 21 specific articles that sought to commit the United States to a whole series of unexpected and unpalatable provisions. 

Mr. GORDON. This is the problem of the OAS Charter discussions? 

Mr. BOLTON. Yes. Apparently the men who went down were notdidn't have the background that they might have had. That, of course, is unfortunate. It happens all the time in different areas of our foreign policy and diplomacy and actions because we are really very ignorant about a good many of these things. 

It seems to me there is given a sort of impetus to the idea that the best thing we can do would be to have a permanent Under Secretary for South America, for Latin America. Would that be something that you would consider possible? 

Mr. GORDON. I haven't really thought about that, Mrs. Bolton. I was a member of a task force back in late 1960 advising President-elect Kennedy on Latin American policy. One member of the task force suggested an idea of this kind. 

There were problems as to what this would do about our relationships to Europe, Africa, to the Far East and so on. And the idea was dropped. 

I don't find after being Assistant Secretary for only a few weeks, that my authority is particularly impaired by having the title of 

Assistant Secretary instead of Under Secretary. 

Mrs. BOLTON. We get all snarled up in semantics, don't we?

Mr. GORDON. I think so. 

If I could comment on the report on the Panama negotiations on the OAS Charter, I think that report is a serious distortion of what actually happened. Both the Latin Americans and our delegation were working on the basis of what was agreed to at Rio. It is true that the Latin American delegations wanted to put into the charter revision proposals language that was a good deal more extensive and complicated than we thought was appropriate for inclusion in the charter. 

This is not really a basic difference of principle. It is not a problem. of resisting all kinds of unpalatable and unreasonable demands. It is a question of working out what is the right kind of language to go into a charter.

Mrs. BOLTON. I wonder if it would be possible for you to have one of your assistants write out a statement, because I think this particular article should be refuted. • Mr. GORDON. Indeed.

Mrs. BOLTON. I would be very grateful if you would do that. It appears in Newsday, which is a small publication by Harry Guggenheim, with Paterson as publisher, Garden City. 

Mr. GORDON. We will be glad to. 

(The information follows:) STATE DEPARTMENT REPLY TO NEWSDAY ARTICLE CONCERNING MEETING HELD IN PANAMA TO CONSIDER PROPOSALS TO REVISE OAS CHARTER 

The Newsday article alleges that the U.S. delegation to the Panama meeting was inadequate. The presence of Assistant Secretary Gordon was required in 

Rio de Janeiro and Buenos Aires and Ambassador Bunker was needed in the Dominican Republic during the period of the Panama meeting. However, Ambassador Woodward, who actually led the U.S. delegation in Panama, is a very experienced and senior representative of the United States who has long dealt with Latin American officials under varied conditions. At one time he occupied the position of Assistant Secretary for Inter-American Affairs. 

He was supported at all times by four to six experienced senior officers from the Bureau of 

Inter-American Affairs. 

The Newsday report and a number of similar articles which appeared in the press constitute a considerable distortion of what actually happened. There was no conflict on basic principles, which have been a matter of growing consensus in recent years and were unanimously supported at the Rio de Janeiro conference last November. Nor was there any Latin American attempt to impose "unexpected and unpalatable provisions" *** "based upon U.S. aid moneys.”

The desirability of the Latin American governments' seeking to increase the fntensity and scope of self-help efforts, supported by assistance from the United States and other member states of the inter-American system, for accelerated economic and social development, has been recognized and spelled out in the Charter of Punta del Este and in the act of Rio. The U.S. delegation both to the 

Panama meeting and to the IA-ECOSOC meeting at the ministerial level which was holding sessions at Buenos Aires during the same last days of March, reaffirmed the U.S. commitment to these principles. . The difference in the Latin American and the U.S. views-a difference expected to be a temporary one centered principally upon the type of language considered appropriate for inclusion within a constitutional type of document having the enduring character of a charter of the Organization of American States. Any allegation that the differences were more extensive is simply incorrect. 

Equally incorrect and entirely unjustified is the statement in the news article mentioned above that the Latin American delegates sought to impose upon the United States "a whole series of unexpected and unpalatable provisions" or that the provisions were "based upon U.S. AID moneys" or included requests for "trade preferences."

Finally, the article in Newsday alleges that the sequel to the Panama meeting has been "Latin bitterness and U.S. embarrassment." This is a serious exaggeration. Assistant Secretary Gordon discussed the progress and problems of the 

Panama meeting with Economic Ministers who were present in Buenos Aires. 

He found that the Ministers gave evidence of a lively appreciation of the contributions that the Alliance for Progress has made to the solution of Latin America's problems. They are aware that the United States will continue to assist in appropriate ways to assure the continued progress of the region. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Selden. 

Mr. SELDEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I want to join with my other colleagues in welcoming you here in your first appearance as Assistant Secretary of State- 

Mrs. BOLTON. May I join you in that.

Mr. SELDEN (continuing). And wish you well in what I know is a very difficult job.

I also would like to say, Mr. Secretary, that I watched your performance in Brazil over a period of about 4 years and I, for one, think you did a very fine job as our Ambassador there. 

Time and a quorum call will not allow me to ask you any questions this morning. I do want to say, however, that at your earliest convenience I would like to have you come before the subcommittee and go into depth on some of the problems on which you touched this morning on a country-by-country basis. 

Mr. GORDON. I would be most grateful for the opportunity. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Secretary. 

The committee stands adjourned until 10 o'clock tomorrow morning. (Whereupon, at 12:30 p.m., the committee was adjourned, to reconvene at 10 a.m., Wednesday, April 6, 1966.)
